Galois module structures and the Hasse principle in twist families via the distribution of Selmer groups
Alex Bartel and Adam Morgan

Abstract
. We address several seemingly disparate problems in arithmetic geometry: the statistical behaviour of the Galois module structure of Mordell–Weil groups of a fixed elliptic curve over varying quadratic extensions; the frequency of failure of the Hasse principle in quadratic twist families of genus 1 1 hyperelliptic curves; and the Hasse principle for Kummer varieties. The common technical ingredient for all of these is a result on the distribution of 2 2 -Selmer ranks in certain sparse families of quadratic twists of a given abelian variety.



1.
Introduction

In this article, we study the distribution of
2
-Selmer groups of abelian varieties over
\mathbb{Q}
in certain zero-density
subfamilies of quadratic twists defined by Frobenian conditions. These results
have several applications, which we address in the second part of the paper:
distribution of Galois module structures of Mordell–Weil groups, Hasse principle for
twists of a hyperelliptic curve of genus
1
, and the Hasse principle for Kummer varieties.
A more general point that this paper illustrates, and that, we hope, will inform future work,
is that incorporating Frobenian conditions into the breakthrough work of Smith

[
36
,
37
]
more generally has many potential applications.

1.1.
Galois module structure of Mordell–Weil groups

There is a lot of beautiful work investigating the average behaviour of the
group structure of
E(F)
where
F
is a number field and
E
is an elliptic curve or a more general abelian variety.
Typically one of these will be fixed and the other one will be varying in some
natural family. In this paper we put ourselves in a situation where the group
E(F)

has additional structure, namely that of a Galois module, and we ask about the
statistical behaviour of that additional structure.

Suppose that
E/\mathbb{Q}
is a principally polarised abelian variety such that
E(\mathbb{Q})

has rank
\rk E(\mathbb{Q})=1
, and let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a quadratic
extension such that
E(F)
has rank
2
. Let
G
be the Galois group of
F/\mathbb{Q}
.
Then it follows from
[
31
]
that the
\mathbb{Z}
-free
quotient
E(F)/E(F)_{\tors}
of
E(F)
is isomorphic, as a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module,
either to a free
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module of rank
1
, which we also denote by

\mathbb{Z}[G]
, or a direct sum
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)
, where the two summands are
both free over
\mathbb{Z}
of rank
1
, and with the non-trivial element of
G

acting trivially, respectively by multiplication by
-1
.

Question 1.1
.

As
F/\mathbb{Q}
runs over quadratic extensions for which
E(F)
has rank
2
,
ordered by the radicals of their discriminants,
what is the proportion of those for which there is an isomorphism

E(F)/E(F)_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
of Galois modules?

Question
1.1
has close parallels to, and indeed was
inspired by Stevenhagen’s heuristic on the solubility of negative Pell
equations
[
39
]
, now a theorem of Koymans–Pagano
[
18
]
.
In the appendix we reformulate Stevenhagen’s conjecture to highlight this parallel.
In particular, as in the case of Pell equations, it will turn out that this
question is too naive to admit an interesting answer, and that it should be
modified.

If
d
is a square-free integer, we abbreviate
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
to
F_{d}
.
Since we assume that
E(\mathbb{Q})
has rank
1
, the condition
\rk E(F_{d})=2

is equivalent to the condition that
\rk E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})=1
, where

E_{d}
is the quadratic twist of
E
by
d
.
Let

\mathcal{F}^{\temp}=\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d\text{ square-free},\rk_{2}E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})=1\},

where
\rk_{2}
denotes the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank, which is widely expected to be equal to the rank.
Our first result is the following.

Proposition 1.2
.

Suppose that
E
has full rational
2
-torsion, and assume that the family
\mathcal{F}^{\temp}

is non-empty. Then one has

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}:|d|<X,E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}:|d|<X\}}=0.

The assumption on full rational
2
-torsion will be made throughout the paper.

Proposition

1.2
, which will be proven as Corollary
6.15
, may, at first,
seem surprising. For example if one expects the frequencies of
\mathbb{Z}[G]
and
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)

in this family to be dictated by the sizes of their automorphism groups, analogously to the
Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for class groups and many of its generalisations, then one should
expect both modules to appear with positive probability. However, as we will explain in
Section
6
,
there is a local obstruction to that happening. More precisely, we will show
that, disregarding finitely many exceptional fields, one has

E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
if and only if a non-divisible point on

E(\mathbb{Q})
is a norm from
E(F_{d})
. For that, a necessary condition is that such a
point be a norm
everywhere locally
. Making this condition explicit, we
deduce that there is a set
\mathcal{P}
of prime numbers of positive Dirichlet density
such that if
d
has any prime divisors not in
\mathcal{P}
, then one has
E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)
.
By a well known argument, Proposition
1.2
follows from this observation.

We define
\mathcal{F}^{+}
to be the
set of
d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}
that satisfy the necessary local conditions just mentioned. In light of
the above observations, it is natural to modify
1.1
as follows.

Question 1.3
.

Does the limit

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{+}:|d|<X,E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{+}:|d|<X\}}

exist, and if so, then what is its value?

Before we explain our approach to this question, we introduce a seemingly entirely
unrelated one, which is very natural in its own right.

1.2.
Hasse principle for genus
1
curves

In
[
4
]
Bhargava investigated how many genus
1

hyperelliptic curves with affine model
y^{2}=f(x)
have a rational point,
as
f
runs over the set of separable quartic polynomials with integer coefficients
bounded by a given constant.
Here we investigate the analogous question for quadratic twist families.
Of course that question is only interesting if
f
has no rational roots.
We assume, from now on, that
f
has no rational roots and that, moreover,
the Galois group of
f
is contained in the Klein
4
-group, equivalently
that the Jacobian
E
of the hyperelliptic curve
y^{2}=f(x)
has full rational
2
-torsion.

Note that unlike in
[
4
]
, 100% of quadratic twists of such a genus

1
curve fail to be everywhere locally soluble, see e.g.
[
28
]
.
Thus, the interesting question in this context is the following.

Question 1.4
.

In the limit as

X\to\infty
, among all square-free integers
d
with
|d|<X
for which the
hyperelliptic curve
dy^{2}=f(x)
has points over all
completions of
\mathbb{Q}
, what is the proportion of those for which
the curve has
\mathbb{Q}
-points?

We now describe our work on the above questions and explain the connection between them.

1.3.
Selmer groups

The
Jacobian of
C:y^{2}=f(x)
is an elliptic curve
E
, and for each

d
, the quadratic twist
C_{d}:dy^{2}=f(x)
of
C
defines a
2
-covering of the quadratic
twist
E_{d}
of
E
, hence a class in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E_{d}[2])
.
There is a natural isomorphism between the Galois modules
E[2]
and
E_{d}[2]
,
which identifies the class of
C_{d}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E_{d}[2])

with the class of
C
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
.
The curve
C_{d}
has a rational point if and only if
the corresponding class
[C]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
lies in the image of the
coboundary map
\delta_{d}\colon E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})/2E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
,
and
C_{d}
has a point everywhere locally if and only if
[C]
lies in the
image of
\delta_{d}
everywhere locally, i.e. if and only if one has
[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
.

Note, in particular, that
C
does have a rational point (a point “at infinity” with
respect to the given affine model), so that the class
[C]

lies in the image of
\delta=\delta_{1}
. Let
\Sigma

be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
2
,
\infty
, and all places
at which
E
has bad reduction. One can show, as in
[
32
]
*§3–4, that a necessary condition for
[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is that
all prime divisors of
d
that are not in
\Sigma
be totally split
in the splitting field of
f
.
Moreover, once that condition is satisfied, the question of whether one has

[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
depends only on the class
b
of
d
in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
.
For a Galois number field
L
and
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
, define

\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}=\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d\text{ square-free},d\equiv b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2},(\text{prime }p\not\in\Sigma\text{ and }p|d)\Rightarrow p\text{ totally split in }L\}.

Then the above discussion can be summarised with the observation that
the family of square-free integers
d
for which one has
[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

is a union of families of the form
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}

for suitable classes
b
as above, and with
L
being the splitting field of
f
.

1.4
therefore reduces to the following question.

Question 1.5
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety, let
\zeta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
,
let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension that trivialises
\zeta
, assume that

\Sigma
contains all primes that ramify in
L
, and let

b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}

one has
\zeta\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
. Then as
d
runs over
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
,
how often is
\zeta
in the image of
\delta_{d}
?

We now make a further assumption on the Galois image

G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\Aut E[4]
: in addition to assuming
E[2]\subset E(\mathbb{Q})
, assume that the
Galois image in
\Aut E[4]
is suitably large subject to the constraint on the

2
-torsion. See
5.1
for the precise formulation.

It turns out that
1.3
also reduces to
1.5
; see Proposition
6.14
for details.
For the rest of the introduction we concentrate on
1.5
.
Our answer comes in two parts:
a theorem on the distribution of
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
as
d
runs through
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
,
and, in the case of
E
being an elliptic curve, a conjecture on the probability of

\zeta\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
being in the image of

\delta_{d}
, conditional on the dimension of the Selmer group. Taken together,
these result in a precise conjectural answer to
1.5

when
E
is an elliptic curve, and hence to the other questions discussed above.
Before we explain this in detail, we make some preparatory remarks.

The distribution of
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
as
d
runs over
all
square-free
integers was determined by Kane
[
16
]
when
E
is an elliptic curve,
following foundational work by
Heath-Brown
[
13
]
and Swinnerton-Dyer
[
40
]
.
Smith
[
36
,
37
]
determined this distribution when
E
is a principally polarised
abelian variety. However, within the
set of all square-free integers,
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is a zero-density subfamily.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is defined in such a way as to skew the Selmer distribution,
since
\zeta
is a non-trivial element of
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
for
all but finitely many
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
.
Actually, the local conditions on
d
may inadvertently force
more elements into the Selmer group.
In Section
4.8
we define the
systematic subspace

\mathcal{S}_{b}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
, of dimension
n_{b}
,
such that we have
\mathcal{S}_{b}\subset\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
for all

d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
. If
E
is an elliptic curve and
L=\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
for
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})
,
then we have
n_{b}\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
We can now state our theorem on the
2
-Selmer distribution, the proof of which will occupy Sections
4

and
5
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety with full rational
2
-torsion and
satisfying
5.1
, let
g
be its dimension,
and let
L
be a Galois number field. Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing

2
,
\infty
, all places at which
E
has bad reduction, and all places that are ramified
in
L
. Define the discrete probability distribution
\alpha
on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

\alpha(r)=\prod_{j\geq 1}(1+2^{-j})^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{2}{2^{j}-1}

for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
,
and let
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
be as defined before
1.5
. Assume that
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
n_{b}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the dimension of the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
. Then there exists
m_{b}\in\{0,1\}
such that
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
we have
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\equiv n_{b}+m_{b}\pmod{2}
, and
for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X,\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=2g+n_{b}+m_{b}+2r\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}}=\alpha(2r+m_{b}).

5.1
is equivalent, in the case of full rational
2
-torsion,
to the hypotheses in
[
37
]
*Theorem 2.14; see
Corollary

6.15
. In particular, in the case
L=\mathbb{Q}
, i.e.
when there are no Frobenian conditions on the prime divisors of
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, our theorem
reduces to a special case of
[
37
]
*Theorem 2.14.
To our knowledge, the theorem has not been known for any other field
L
.

Note that if
C/\mathbb{Q}
is a hyperelliptic curve of genus
1
,
then
one expects
[
41
]
that
inside the family of square-free
d\in\mathbb{Z}
for which
C_{d}
has a rational point
there is a density
1
subfamily in which the Jacobian of
C_{d}
has rank
1
.
This observation justifies the condition on
m_{b}
in the following corollary of
Theorem

1.6
,
which we will deduce in
Theorem

7.2
.

Corollary 1.7
.

Let
C/\mathbb{Q}:y^{2}=f(x)
be a hyperelliptic curve as in Section
1.2
,
and let
E
be its Jacobian.
Assume that
E
satisfies the hypotheses of
Theorem

1.6
.
Let
L
be the splitting field of
f
.
With
\Sigma
as in the theorem, let

b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that
n_{b}=1
and such that
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, the curve
C_{d}
has points everywhere locally
and the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is odd, i.e.,
in the notation of
Theorem

1.6
, we have
m_{b}=0
. Assume that the

2
-parts of Shafarevich–Tate groups of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
are finite.
Then we have

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X,C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}}\geq\prod_{j\geq 1}(1+2^{-j})^{-1}>0.41.

The analogous result on
1.3
, giving, under similar hypotheses,
a lower bound on the frequency
of
E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
among
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, will be proven in
Theorem

6.17
.

Lastly, we formulate
a conjecture on the value of this last limit for all possible values of
n_{b}
.
In the next subsection we will explain our heuristics that lead to this conjecture.

Conjecture 1.8
.

Let
C
,
E
, and
\Sigma
be as in
Corollary

1.7
.
Let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, the curve
C_{d}
has points everywhere locally
and the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is odd.
Let
\alpha(r)
be as in Theorem
1.6
,
and
n_{b}=\dim\mathcal{S}_{b}
as introduced before Theorem
1.6
.
Then we have

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X,C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}}=\begin{cases}1/2,&n_{b}=1;\\
1/8,&n_{b}=2;\\
5/64,&n_{b}=3;\\
29/1024,&n_{b}=4.\end{cases}

1.4.
Equidistribution and random matrix heuristics

Keep the notation of Section
1.3
.
Recall that we have
C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
if and only if the element
[C]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])

is in the image of
\delta_{d}(E_{d}(\mathbb{Q}))
. Let
L
be the splitting field of
f
, and fix

b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
such that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
one has

[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
and
\rk_{2}E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is odd. One can show that for all but finitely many
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}

one has
[C]\not\in\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
.
One expects that outside of a density
0
exceptional subset
of
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
we have
E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, so that
outside of that exceptional subset, the projection of the image

\delta_{d}(E_{d}(\mathbb{Q}))
on the quotient
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
is
1
-dimensional,
and we have
C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
if and only if the unique non-trivial
element of that image is equal to
[C]
in
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
.
As
d
varies over
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, it seems natural
to model the non-trivial element in
\delta_{d}(E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})/E[2])
as a uniformly random non-trivial element of
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
.
This heuristic together with Theorem
1.6
leads to the following concrete conjecture.

Conjecture 1.9
.

Let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}

one has
[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
and
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
odd,
and let
n_{b}\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
,
m_{b}\in\{0,1\}
, and
\alpha(r)

be as in Theorem
1.6
. Then we have

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}_{b}:|d|<X,C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}_{b}:|d|<X\}}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\frac{\alpha(2r+m_{b})}{2^{n_{b}+2r+m_{b}}-1}.

A similar sum also occurs in Stevenhagen’s conjecture – see the Appendix –
only with a slightly different Selmer group distribution.
Rather astonishingly, unlike the sum in Stevenhagen’s conjecture,
the infinite sum in
1.9
turns out to
be a rational number. While one could evaluate
it by explicit manipulation of the sum, we opt for a different route, which also offers an alternative
heuristic explanation for
1.9
, as we now describe.

It is known
[
3
]
that one can
model
p^{\infty}
-torsion subgroups of Tate–Shafarevich groups of quadratic
twists of an elliptic curve as cokernels of random large alternating matrices
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. In Section
9.3

we modify this heuristic so as to incorporate the
local conditions on
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
. It turns out that our modification “knows” not only
the distribution of Selmer ranks, but also the image of
[C]
in
the Selmer groups of the twists. Both the probabilities in Theorem
1.6

and the equidistribution hypothesis then arise via pushforward of Haar measure on
a suitable space of large alternating matrices. What is more, this description naturally leads
to an expression for the
infinite sum in
1.9
as a rational number.
Explicitly, we will prove the following result as
Corollary

9.18
.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and let
m\in\{0,1\}
be such that one has
m\equiv n+1\pmod{2}
.
Let

\beta_{n}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\frac{\alpha(2r+m)}{2^{n+2r+m}-1}

be the sum in
1.9
. Then we have
\beta_{1}=1/2
, and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}

we have
\beta_{n}=2^{-n}\cdot\left(1-(2^{n-1}-1)\beta_{n-1}\right)
.

This is the source of the otherwise perhaps rather mysterious looking numbers in
1.8
.

1.5.
Hasse principle for Kummer varieties

Our final application of Theorem
1.6
concerns the Hasse principle for Kummer varieties. Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety and let
0\neq\zeta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
. We can associate to
\zeta
a generalized Kummer variety
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
. The existence of rational points on classes of such Kummer varieties is the subject of several works
[
35
,
11
,
12
,
27
]
. These all use versions of Swinnerton-Dyer’s descent-fibration method to establish, conditional on finiteness of relevant Shafarevich-Tate groups, either the Hasse principle or the sufficiency of the Brauer–Manin obstruction.

For each squarefree integer
d
, denote by
Y_{\zeta,d}
the
2
-covering of
E_{d}
associated to
\zeta
. This is a torsor under
E_{d}
equipped with an involution
\iota_{d}
compatible with multiplication by
-1
on
E_{d}
. Denoting by
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}
the blow up of
Y_{\zeta,d}
at the fixed points of
\iota_{d}
, there is a natural degree
2
morphism
f_{d}:\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}\to\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
(the quotient by the unique extension of
\iota_{d}
). In each of the cited works, one establishes the existence of a rational point on
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
by first proving the existence of a squarefree integer
d
for which
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
. As a result of Theorem
1.6
, in certain cases we are able not only to establish the existence of such a
d
, but to quantify how many such
d
there are.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be the smallest Galois extension trivialising
\zeta
, and let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places containing
2,\infty
, all places ramified in
L/\mathbb{Q}
, and all places of bad reduction for
E
. For the statement of Theorem
1.11
below, recall that Condition
5.1
asserts that the Galois action on
E[4]
is suitably large, subject to being trivial on
E[2]
. In the terminology of the statement of Theorem
1.6
, Condition
8.1
is the assertion that there exists
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
such that both the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is generated by
\zeta
, and the parity
m_{b}+n_{b}\pmod{2}
is equal to
1
. This condition is closely related to
[
35
, Condition E]
; see Remark
8.2
for more details.

Theorem 1.11
.

Suppose that
E
has full rational
2
-torsion. Let
0\neq\zeta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
, let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be as above, and suppose that Conditions
5.1
and
8.1
are satisfied.
Assume further that the
2
-primary part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of each quadratic twist of
E
is finite. Then we have

\#\{d\in\mathbb{Z}~~:~~d\textup{ square-free},~|d|<X,~f_{d}(\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}\big(\mathbb{Q})\big)\neq\emptyset\}\asymp X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

An immediate consequence is that, under the hypotheses of Theorem
1.11
, one has
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
. To the best of our knowledge, this statement is already new. In Proposition
8.10
, we show that Condition
5.1
is implied by a version of
[
35
, Conditions
\textup{Z}_{1}
and
\textup{Z}_{2}
]
. In particular, one can deduce from Theorem
1.11
a similar result to
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
which, though working over
\mathbb{Q}
, allows abelian varieties of arbitrary dimension; see Corollary
9.1
and the following remark. As alluded to in
[
12
, Remark 2.10]
, such a result (i.e. Corollary
9.1
minus the quantitive aspect) can likely be established via a more direct application of Swinnerton-Dyer’s method, and should, roughly speaking, be viewed as being weaker than the main result of
[
12
]
. Nevertheless, we believe there is much potential for proving new results about rational points on Kummer varieties via analytic methods. Indeed, fully adapting Smith’s work
[
36
,
37
]
to twist families defined by suitable Frobenian conditions should yield a version of Theorem
1.11
that applies over general number fields, allows more general Galois structures on
E[2]
, and incorporates information from higher descents.

1.6.
Strategy of proof of the main theorem

We broadly follow the strategy of Heath–Brown
[
13
]
, but with some modifications, and in several places take inspiration from the more recent works of Kane
[
16
]

and Smith
[
36
]
.
To determine the distribution of
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
over
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
we
determine, for all
r
, the
r
-th moments. For
X\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
write
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)=\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}
.
By the
r
-th moment one usually means

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}
,
equivalently the average number of homomorphisms from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
to
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
,
although we will in fact compute something slightly different, see below.

To determine the
r
-th moment, it is convenient to work with
the abelian variety
A=E^{r}
. One clearly has
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}=\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
.

In common with the works of Kane and Smith, we begin by using the local Tate pairing to express, in
Lemma

4.4
,
the characteristic function of
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])

in terms of quadratic characters on local cohomology groups.

Write
D
for the “part of
d
coprime to
\Sigma
”.
We parametrise both local and global cohomology
groups in terms of factorisations of
D
into square-free integers indexed by
A[2]
.
The expression for
\#\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\#\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2},\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q}))

becomes a sum of Jacobi symbols indexed by factorisations of
D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}D_{\mathbf{u}}
– see

Proposition

4.21
.
One wants to evaluate the sum of such expressions over
d<X
, in the limit as
X\to\infty
.
It turns out to be fruitful to partition that latter sum into subsums
\mathcal{S}^{T}(X)

according to the subsets
T=\{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}:D_{\mathbf{u}}\neq 1\}
. Thus, for example,
there are
\#A[2]^{2}
subsums corresponding to singletons
T
, i.e. to trivial
factorisations of
D
into just a single factor. This is explained in detail in

Definition

4.12
.

At this point, a central role is played by the notion of linked indices. We define
in
Definition

4.27
what it means for two elements of
A[2]^{2}
to be linked.
The role of
Proposition

4.29

is to show that in the contribution to the
r
-th moment, subsums
\mathcal{S}^{T}(X)
for
subsets
T
that are not maximal unlinked will go into the error term.

It remains to estimate the sum
\mathcal{S}^{T}(X)
over maximal unlinked subsets
T
.
In fact, we determine the average number of
injective
homomorphisms
from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
to
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
, or to be even more precise, from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
to
the quotient
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/(\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])\oplus\mathcal{S}_{b})
by the image of
2
-torsion and the systematic subspace,
which natually produces the shift in the distribution by
2g+n_{b}
appearing in the statement of Theorem
1.6
.

The systematic subspace is defined in Section
4.8
.

Theorem

4.60
explains that the passage from all homomorphisms

\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
to those that are injections into
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\mathcal{S}_{b}

corresponds to summing only over particular maximal unlinked subsets
T
.
That restriction turns out to be very convenient for the final count.

Section
5
is devoted to computing the main term
of the injection-count and deducing the distribution.
Maximal unlinked subsets turn out to be cosets of maximal isotropic subspaces
for a certain quadratic form, as in
[
13
]
. One novelty
of our approach is that by shifting Selmer elements by the images
of
2
-torsion, we manage to reduce the analysis to only maximal
isotropic subspaces.

It is shown in
Lemma

5.5

that those maximal isotropic subspaces admit a very explicit linear-algebraic description:
they are parametrised by pairs
(U,P)
consisting of a subspace
U
of
A[2]

and an alternating pairing
P
on
U
. Moreover, it is shown in
Proposition

5.18
that
the only such pairs
(U,P)
contributing to the final count are those for which
U
is fixed by a subgroup
\Gamma
of
\textup{End}(A[2])
arising from the natural Galois
action on
A[4]
, as described in Section
2.7
.
It is here that our assumption on the Galois image on
A[4]
comes in. Under that assumption,
one can explicitly describe the
\Gamma
-invariant subspaces
U\subset A[2]
– see
Lemma

5.19
.

This is where the strategy of counting injections pays off. It turns out that for injections, the only relevant maximal isotropic
subspaces are those for which
U=A[2]
, so that the sum becomes only a sum over the alternating
pairings
P
. As above, only the
\Gamma
-invariant pairings
P
are shown to
contribute to the sum. Writing
A[2]
as
E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, the
\Gamma
-invariant pairings
P
are exactly the ones of the form
e_{2}\otimes\langle,\rangle
,
where
e_{2}
is the Weil pairing on
E[2]
and
\langle,\rangle
is an arbitrary symmetric
pairing on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. In particular, the number of such pairings is
2^{r(r+1)/2}
, which is
exactly the
r
-th injection-moment – see
Theorem

5.41
.

1.7.
Future directions

Question
1.1
is a special case of a question that,
we believe, deserves more attention than it has received.
Fix an algebraic closure
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be an elliptic curve, let
G
be a finite group, and let
V
be
a finitely generated module over the group ring
\mathbb{Q}[G]
. By a
G
-extension
of
\mathbb{Q}
we mean a pair
(F,\iota)
consisting of a Galois number field

F
inside
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}

and an isomorphism
\iota
between the Galois group of
F/\mathbb{Q}
and
G
.
If
(F,\iota)
is a
G
-extension of
\mathbb{Q}
, then the Galois module
E(F)
may be
viewed as a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module via
\iota
. If, moreover, the

\mathbb{Q}[G]
-module
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}E(F)
is isomorphic to
V
, then by the
Jordan–Zassenhaus theorem

[
43
]
, there are only finitely many possible isomorphism classes for the

\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice
E(F)/E(F)_{\tors}
, where
E(F)_{\tors}
denotes the torsion
subgroup of
E(F)
, and where by a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice we mean a finitely generated

\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module that is free over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Question 1.12
.

For each of the finitely many, up to isomorphism, lattices
\Lambda\subset V
,
as
(F,\iota)
varies in natural families of
G
-extensions of
\mathbb{Q}

satisfying
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}E(F)\cong V
,
how often do we have
E(F)/E(F)_{\tors}\cong_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}\Lambda
?

It can be deduced from the proof of
[
2
]
*Theorem 5.6.1 that
Artin’s induction theorem implies that prescribing the isomorphism class
of
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}E(F)

as a
\mathbb{Q}[G]
-module is equivalent to prescribing the ranks of
E
over all
subfields of
F
. Thus, Question
1.12
can be interpreted
as asking about the Galois module structure of
E(F)/E(F)_{\tors}
,
provided one knows everything about ranks.

Our approach readily generalises to the following case:
G=\langle g\rangle
has order
2
,

E
has full rational
2
-torsion, but
V
is arbitrary. Indeed, it follows
from the computations in Section
6.1
that when
G
has order
2
, a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice

M
is determined up to isomorphism by its
\mathbb{Z}
-rank, the
\mathbb{Z}
-rank of the
G
-fixed
submodule
M^{G}
, and the index of
(1+g)M
in
M^{G}
. Thus, Question

1.12
for
G
cyclic of order
2
and
E
with full rational
2
-torsion
may always be reduced to Question
1.4
in the same manner as we proceeded
for Question
1.3
.

Of course the scope of
1.12
can be expanded further by
replacing the base field
\mathbb{Q}
by any other field that is finitely generated over
its prime field. Another natural variant is to investigate the statistical
behaviour of the
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-modules
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
as
F
runs over
G
-extensions
of a fixed base field.

We believe that
1.8
, while presenting a
formidable technical challenge, is within reach. A proof
can be expected to involve a generalisation of
Theorem

1.6

from
2
-Selmer groups to
2^{n}
-Selmer groups. Smith
[
36
,
37
]

has developed powerful techniques for computing the distributions of
2^{n}
-Selmer
groups in very general twist families. Zero-density families such as ours, where the systematic subspace is typically non-trivial, are explicitly excluded from Smith’s work. However, the difficulties involved in generalising Smith’s techniques to such
a family have already been overcome in one specific instance, namely
in the proof by Koymans–Pagano

[
18
]
of Stevenhagen’s conjecture, which, as we mentioned above,
we regard as a number field analogue of our conjecture and which was one of the main
inspirations for the present work.

1.8.
Notation

By
\mu
we will denote the Moebius function
\mathbb{Z}\to\{-1,0,1\}
.
If
k
is a field, then we will denote by
\bar{k}
a fixed algebraic closure
of
k
, by
k^{s}
the separable closure inside
\bar{k}
, and by
G_{k}
the absolute
Galois group of
k
, i.e. the Galois group of
k^{s}/k
. We denote by
k^{\times 2}

the subgroup of squares in the unit group
k^{\times}
of
k
. If
k
is a global field,
then by
\mathcal{O}_{k}
we denote its ring of integers, and, for a place
v
of
k
,
by
k_{v}
we denote the completion of
k
at
v
.
If
E/k
is an elliptic curve over a number field with rank
1
,
then we will refer to non-divisible elements of
E(k)
as “Mordell–Weil generators”.
If
U
is a set, then
\boldsymbol{1}_{U}
will denote the characteristic function
of
U
(on some superset of
U
). For a finite group, we will denote by
\boldsymbol{1}
the trivial character.

Part I
Distribution of
2
-Selmer groups

2.
Preliminaries on
2
-Selmer groups

In this section, let
k
be a number field, and let
E/k
be a
principally polarised abelian variety with full rational
2
-torsion. We denote
by
\Sigma
a finite set of places of
k
containing all places dividing
2
,
all archimedean places, and all places at which
E
has bad reduction. Let
g=\dim E
.

2.1.
Weil pairings

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let

e_{n}\colon E[n]\otimes E[n]\to\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}

denote the Weil pairing on
E[n]
associated to the given principal polarisation,
where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}
denotes the group of
n
-th roots of unity in
\bar{k}
.
We also write
e
for
e_{2}
but taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
instead of
\{\pm 1\}
,
so that we have
e_{2}(x,y)=(-1)^{e(x,y)}
for all
x,y\in E[2]
.

2.2.
Quadratic twists

Fix
d\in k^{\times}
,
let
K=k(\sqrt{d})
and let

\psi_{d}\colon G_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
be the associated quadratic character with kernel
G_{K}
. We allow for the possibility that
\psi_{d}
is trivial, i.e. that
d\in k^{\times 2}
.

Let
E_{d}/k
denote the quadratic twist of
E
by
K/k
, and
fix a
K
-isomorphism
\xi\colon E_{d}\to E

such that, for all
\sigma\in G_{k}
, one has
\xi^{-1}\xi^{\sigma}=(-1)^{\psi_{d}(\sigma)}
. Via the isomorphism
\xi
, the principal polarisation on
E
descends to a principal polarisation on
E_{d}
(
[
26
, Lemma 4.16]
). In this way, we view
E_{d}
as a principally polarised abelian variety also.
Note that
\xi
induces a
G_{k}
-isomorphism
E_{d}[2]\cong E[2]
. This identifies the Weil pairing on
E[2]
with the corresponding pairing on
E_{d}[2]
(to see this, e.g.
combine
[
26
, Lemmas 4.5(ii) and 4.17]
).

Denote by
\delta\colon E(k)/2E(k)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(k,E[2])
the coboundary
homomorphism associated with the multiplication-by-
2
Kummer sequence

0\longrightarrow E[2]\longrightarrow E(k^{s})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[2]}}{{\longrightarrow}}E(k^{s})\longrightarrow 0.

In what follows, use the map
\xi
to identify
H^{1}(k,E_{d}[2])
with
H^{1}(k,E[2])
.
In this way, we view the corresponding coboundary homomorphism associated to
E_{d}
as taking values in
H^{1}(k,E[2])
. We denote the resulting homomorphism by
\delta_{d}\colon E_{d}(k)/2E_{d}(k)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(k,E[2])
.

2.3.
Selmer conditions

For each place
v
of
k
, denote by

\delta_{v}\colon E(k_{v})/2E(k_{v})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and}\hskip 10.00002pt\delta_{d,v}\colon E_{d}(k_{v})/2E_{d}(k_{v})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])

the local versions of the maps
\delta
and
\delta_{d}
, respectively.
We denote by
\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
the image of
\delta_{d,v}
. When
d\in k^{\times 2}
, we drop it from the notation and write
\mathscr{S}_{v}=\im(\delta_{v})
.
For a nonarchimedean place
v
of

k
we denote by
H^{1}_{\ur}(k_{v},E[2])
the set of classes in
H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])

that restrict trivially to the inertia group
I_{v}
at
v
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
v\notin\Sigma
be a place of
k
. Then one has

\mathscr{S}_{d,v}=\begin{cases}\delta_{d,v}(E[2]),&v\textup{ ramifies in }k(\sqrt{d});\\
H^{1}_{\ur}(k_{v},E[2]),&\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

When
v
is unramified in
k(\sqrt{d})
, the abelian variety
E_{d}
has good reduction at
v
and the result is standard (see e.g.

[
30
, Remark 4.11 and Proposition 4.12]
).
Suppose that
v
ramifies in
k(\sqrt{d})
. Arguing as in

[
24
, Lemma 5.2]
, we see that
E_{d}
has no points of order
4
defined over the maximal unramified extension of
k_{v}
. In particular,
E(k_{v})
contains no points of order
4
, hence

\delta_{d,v}
is injective on
E[2]
. Thus we have
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\delta_{d,v}(E[2])=\dim E[2]
.
Since
v\nmid 2
, we also have

\dim E[2]=\dim E_{d}(k_{v})/2E_{d}(k_{v})=\dim\mathscr{S}_{d,v},

where the first equality follows e.g. from
[
33
, Proposition 3.9]
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
v\not\in\Sigma
be a place of
k
. Then:

(1)

if
v
is unramified in
k(\sqrt{d})
, then we have

\mathscr{S}_{v}=\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
;

(2)

if
v
is ramified in
k(\sqrt{d})
, then we have

\mathscr{S}_{v}\cap\mathscr{S}_{d,v}=0
.

Proof.

Part
(1)
follows from
[
11
]
*Lemma 4.1
(see also Lemma
6.8
below) and

[
21
]
*Corollary 4.4. For part
(2)

see
[
11
]
*Lemma 4.3.
∎

2.4.
Parity of Selmer ranks

Let
\rk_{2}(E/k)
denote the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank of
E/k
,

\rk_{2}(E/k)=\rk_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\Hom\Big(\varinjlim_{n}\Sel_{2^{n}}(E/k),\mathbb{Q}_{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\Big).

Notation 2.3
.

For a place
v\in\Sigma
and an element
b\in k_{v}^{\times}/k_{v}^{\times 2}
, define
\kappa_{v}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
as

\kappa_{v}(b)=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathscr{S}_{v}/\mathscr{S}_{v}\cap\mathscr{S}_{b,v}\pmod{2}.

We write
\kappa_{v}(d)
for the result of evaluating
\kappa_{v}
at the class of
d
in
k_{v}^{\times}/k_{v}^{\times 2}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

We have
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)\equiv\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/k)\pmod{2}
. Moreover, we have

\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)\equiv\rk_{2}(E/k)+\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\kappa_{v}(d)\pmod{2}.

Proof.

Let
\text{\tencyr Sh}_{\nd}(E_{d}/k)
denote the quotient of
\text{\tencyr Sh}(E_{d}/k)
by
its maximal divisible subgroup. We have

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/k)=\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)+\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}E_{d}(k)[2]+\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\text{\tencyr Sh}_{\nd}(E_{d}/k)[2]
.
By
[
11
]
*Lemma 5.1, the assumption that
E[2]
is defined over
k

implies that
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\text{\tencyr Sh}_{\nd}(E_{d}/k)[2]
is even. Since
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}E_{d}(k)[2]=2g
,
we see that
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)\equiv\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/k)\pmod{2}
.

The second assertion follows from
[
26
]
*Theorem 10.12 and Lemma 10.8.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
d^{\prime}\in k^{\times}
be such that
d
and
d^{\prime}

have the same class in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}k_{v}^{\times}/k_{v}^{\times 2}
.
Then we have
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)\equiv\rk_{2}(E_{d^{\prime}}/k)\pmod{2}

and

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/k)\equiv\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}(E_{d^{\prime}}/k)\pmod{2}.

Proof.

Immediate from
Theorem

2.4
.
∎

2.5.
Local Tate pairings

For each place
v
of
k
, composition of cup-product associated to the Weil pairing
e

and the local invariant map gives a non-degenerate pairing

(2.6)

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{v}:H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])\times H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])\longrightarrow\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2},

with respect to which the subspace
\mathscr{S}_{v}
is maximal isotropic (see
[
30
, Proposition 4.10]
).

We denote by
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
the sum of the local Tate
pairings (
2.6
) over
v\in\Sigma
.
It is a non-degenerate
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-valued bilinear pairing on
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])
.

We denote by
H_{v}
the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-valued pairing on
H^{1}(k_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})
given similarly by composition of cup-product and the local invariant map, and denote by
H_{\Sigma}
its sum over
v\in\Sigma
. For
d_{1},d_{2}\in k^{\times}
, we have
(d_{1},d_{2})_{v}=(-1)^{H_{v}(\psi_{d_{1}},\psi_{d_{2}})}
, where
(~,~)_{v}
denotes the quadratic Hilbert symbol.

Since the action of
G_{k}
on
E[2]
is trivial, the natural cup-product map

H^{1}(k,\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes E[2]\longrightarrow H^{1}(k,E[2])=\Hom_{\cts}(G_{k},E[2])

is an isomorphism. We will frequently identify these groups, via this isomorphism, in what follows. By associativity of the cup-product, the analagous isomorphism with
k
replaced by
k_{v}
identifies the local Tate pairing
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{v}
with the pairing
H_{v}\otimes e
, and
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
with
H_{\Sigma}\otimes e
.

2.6.
Tate quadratic forms

Let
\phi
be an endomorphism of
E
defined over
k
that is invariant under the Rosati involution associated to the given principal polarisation.
Denote by
P_{\phi,2}\colon E[2]\otimes E[2]\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
the pairing defined by

P_{\phi,2}(x,y)=e_{2}(x,\phi(y))
. This pairing is alternating and
G_{k}
-equivariant.
The same is true of the pairing
P_{\phi,4}\colon E[4]\otimes E[4]\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}

defined by
P_{\phi,4}(x,y)=e_{4}(x,\phi(y))
.

From these pairings, we can follow
[
25
, Definition 5.19, Example 5.20]
to define
a Theta group
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
for
E[2]
in the sense of
[
25
, Definition 5.1]
. Indeed, denote by
U_{\phi}
the
G_{k}
-group with underlying
G_{k}
-set
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}\times E[4]
, and with group structure given by
(a,x)\cdot(b,y)=(abP_{\phi,4}(x,y),x+y).

This has a normal subgroup
N=\{(1,x)~~\colon~~x\in E[2]\}
, and we define
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}=U_{\phi}/N
.
We have a commutative diagram of
G_{k}
-groups with exact rows

(2.7)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{\phi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{\phi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1,}

realising
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
as a central extension of
E[2]
by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}
.
The associated commutator pairing
E[2]\otimes E[2]\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}
is
P_{\phi,2}
.
For each place
v
of
k
, define

q_{\phi,v}\colon H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

to be the composition of the connecting map associated to the bottom row of (
2.7
) with the local invariant map,
and let

q_{\phi,\Sigma}\colon\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

be the sum over
v\in\Sigma
of the maps
q_{\phi,v}
.
We deduce from
[
29
, Proposition 2.9]
that
q_{\phi,v}
and

q_{\phi,\Sigma}
are quadratic forms whose associated bilinear pairings send
(f,g)
to

\langle f,\phi(g)\rangle_{v}
, respectively
\langle f,\phi(g)\rangle_{\Sigma}
.
Since
\phi
is an endomorphism of
E
, it follows from
[
30
, Proposition 4.10]
that, for every
v\in\Sigma
, the isotropy condition
[
25
, Equation (5.18)]

is satisfied for the images
\mathscr{W}_{v}
of the multiplication-by-
4
Kummer maps

\delta^{(4)}_{v}\colon E(k_{v})/4E(k_{v})\to H^{1}(k_{v},E[4]).

Since, for every
v\in\Sigma
, we have
\mathscr{S}_{v}=2\mathscr{W}_{v}
,
it follows from
[
25
, Example 5.20]
that we have

q_{\phi,v}(\mathscr{S}_{v})=0\text{ for }v\in\Sigma,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptq_{\phi,\Sigma}\big(\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}\mathscr{S}_{v}\big)=0.

By reciprocity for the Brauer group of
k
, the form
q_{\phi,\Sigma}
also vanishes
on the image of the group
H^{1}_{\Sigma}(k,E[2])
of global cocycles that are unramified
outside
\Sigma
. Moreover, if in the construction of
q_{\phi,v}
the Galois module
E[4]
is
replaced by
E_{d}[4]
for
d\in k^{\times}
, a direct computation shows that one obtains the same quadratic form.
Thus,
q_{\phi,v}
also vanishes on the image of
\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
for every
d\in k^{\times}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
x\in E[2]
, let
v
be a place of
k
, and let
\chi\in H^{1}(k_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})
.
Then the connecting maps associated to the bottom row of (
2.7
) send:

(i)

x\in H^{0}(k_{v},E[2])
to the image of
\delta(x)\cup_{e}\phi(x)
in
H^{1}(k_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4})
,

(ii)

\chi\cup x\in H^{1}(k_{v},E[2])
to the image of
\delta(x)\cup_{e}\phi(x)\cup\chi
in
H^{2}(k_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4})
.

Here
\cup_{e}
denotes the cup-product with respect to the Weil pairing
e
on
E[2]
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
\overline{x}\in E[4]
be such that
2\overline{x}=x
.
Then
(1,\overline{x})
is a lift of
x
to
U_{\phi}
under the maps in (
2.7
).
We then compute, for
\sigma\in G_{k_{v}}
,

(1,\overline{x})^{-1}\cdot\sigma(1,\overline{x})=\big(P_{\phi,4}(\overline{x},\sigma\overline{x}),\sigma\overline{x}-\overline{x}\big).

Since
\sigma\overline{x}-\overline{x}
lies in
E[2]
, reducing the above expression modulo
N

we see from commutativity of (
2.7
) that the image of
x
under the connecting
map associated to the bottom row of (
2.7
) is the
1
-cocycle
\rho\colon G_{k_{v}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}
defined by

\rho(\sigma)=P_{\phi,4}(\overline{x},\sigma\overline{x})=P_{\phi,4}(\overline{x}-\sigma\overline{x},\sigma\overline{x}),

the last equality following from the fact that
P_{\phi,4}
is alternating.
Again using that
\sigma\overline{x}-\overline{x}
lies in
E[2]
, this is equal to

e_{2}(\overline{x}-\sigma\overline{x},\phi(x))=\big(\delta(x)\cup\phi(x)\big)(\sigma),

as desired.

(ii)
Let
\omega\colon G_{k_{v}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be such that, for all
\sigma\in G_{k_{v}}
,
we have
\chi(\sigma)=(-1)^{\omega(\sigma)}
. Let
\theta\in\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
be any lift of
x
.
Then the function
\rho\colon G_{k_{v}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
sending
\sigma\in G_{k_{v}}
to

\rho(\sigma)=\theta^{\omega(\sigma)}
gives a lift of
\chi\cup x
to
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
.
We then compute, for
\sigma,\tau\in G_{k_{v}}
,

\displaystyle\rho(\sigma)\cdot\sigma

\displaystyle\rho(\tau)\cdot\rho(\sigma\tau)^{-1}=\theta^{\omega(\sigma)}\cdot\sigma(\theta)^{\omega(\tau)}\cdot\theta^{-\omega(\sigma\tau)}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\omega(\sigma)}\cdot\sigma(\theta)^{\omega(\tau)}\cdot\theta^{-\omega(\sigma)}\cdot\theta^{-\omega(\tau)}\cdot\theta^{(d\omega)(\sigma,\tau)}

\displaystyle=[\theta^{\omega(\sigma)},\sigma(\theta)^{\omega(\tau)}]\cdot(\sigma(\theta)\cdot\theta^{-1})^{\omega(\tau)}\cdot\theta^{(d\omega)(\sigma,\tau)}

\displaystyle=[\theta^{\omega(\sigma)},\sigma(\theta)^{\omega(\tau)}]\cdot[\sigma(\theta),\theta^{-1}]^{\omega(\tau)}\cdot(\theta^{-1}\cdot\sigma(\theta))^{\omega(\tau)}\cdot\theta^{(d\omega)(\sigma,\tau)},

where
(d\omega)(\sigma,\tau)
is equal to
2
if
\omega(\sigma)=\omega(\tau)=1
, and is
0
otherwise.
Since the commutator pairing associated to the bottom row of (
2.7
) is equal
to
P_{\phi,2}
, the first two terms vanish. With
\overline{x}
as in the proof of
(i)
,
we can take
\theta
to be the image of
(1,\overline{x})
in
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
.
Then
\theta^{2}
is the image of
(1,\overline{x})^{2}=(1,x)
in
\mathcal{H}_{\phi}
, which is trivial.
From this we conclude that the final term vanishes also. Finally, the computation in part
(i)

shows that
(\theta^{-1}\cdot\sigma(\theta))^{\omega(\tau)}
is a
2
-cocycle representing the class
of
\delta(x)\cup\phi(x)\cup\chi
.
∎

2.7.
Galois action on the
4
-torsion

Denote by
\Aut(E[4]/E[2])
the subgroup of

\Aut E[4]
consisting of elements that restrict to the trivial automorphism of
E[2]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

For
\phi\in\End(E[2])
, the map
f(\phi)\colon E[4]\to E[4]
defined by

f(\phi)(x)=x+\phi(2x)

is an element of
\Aut(E[4]/E[2])
. The resulting map
f\colon\End(E[2])\to\Aut(E[4]/E[2])
is an isomorphism of groups.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in\End(E[2])
and let
f(\phi)
be as in the statement. It is clear that
f(\phi)
is an endomorphism of
E[4]
which restricts to the identity on
E[2]
. Let
x\in E[4]
be such that
f(\phi)(x)=0
. Then
x=-\phi(2x)
is an element of
E[2]
, hence
0=-\phi(2x)=x
. Thus
f(\phi)
is injective, hence is an automorphism of
E[4]
by counting. A straightforward computation shows that
f
is a homomorphism. To complete the proof, we note that the inverse of
f
is given by sending
\sigma\in\Aut(E[4]/E[2])
to the endomorphism of
E[2]
defined by

y\longmapsto\sigma x-x\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for any }x\in E[4]\text{ such that }2x=y.

Here the choice of
x
is irrelevant, since
\sigma
acts trivially on
E[2]
.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

Since
E/k
has full rational
2
-torsion, the absolute Galois group
G_{k}
acts on
E[4]
through a homomorphism
G_{k}\to\Aut(E[4]/E[2])
. We define

\gamma\colon G_{k}\to\End(E[2])

as the composition of this map with the isomorphism
\Aut(E[4]/E[2])\cong\End(E[2])
afforded by Lemma
2.9
. Explicitly, for
\sigma\in G_{k}
and
x\in E[2]
, we have

\gamma(\sigma)(x)=\delta(x)(\sigma).

Note that
\gamma
factors through
k(E[4])/k
, and as such is unramified at primes outside
\Sigma
.
It realises
\Gal(k(E[4])/k)
as a subgroup of
\End(E[2])
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
v
be a place of
k
and
d\in k^{\times}
. Then, for all
x\in E[2]
, we have
\delta_{d,v}(x)=\delta_{v}(x)+\psi_{d}\cup x
. For all
\sigma\in G_{k_{v}}
, we have

\delta_{d,v}(x)(\sigma)=\gamma(\sigma)(x)+\psi_{d}(\sigma)x.

Proof.

Via the fixed identification of
E
and
E_{d}
over
k(\sqrt{d})
we identify
E[4]
and
E_{d}[4]
.
Unlike in the case of
2
-torsion, this identification does not respect the Galois action;
instead,
E_{d}[4]
corresponds to
E[4]
with the new action

\sigma\cdot y=(-1)^{\psi_{d}(\sigma)}\sigma(y).

Now with
\sigma
and
x
fixed as in the statement, take
y\in E[4]
such that
2y=x
. We then have

\displaystyle\delta_{d,v}(x)(\sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\psi_{d}(\sigma)}(\sigma)\sigma(y)-y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sigma(y)-y)+((-1)^{\psi_{d}(\sigma)}-1)\sigma(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma(\sigma)(x)+\psi_{d}(\sigma)x.

This proves the second part of the statement. For the first, note that the right hand side of the above formula is the result of evaluating
\delta_{v}(x)+\psi_{d}\cup x
at
\sigma
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\sigma\in G_{k}
. Then for all
x,y\in E[2]
we have

e(x,\gamma(\sigma)y)=e(\gamma(\sigma)x,y)+\psi_{-1}(\sigma)e(x,y).

Proof.

From the definition of
\gamma
, each
\sigma\in G_{k}
acts on
E[4]
as

x\longmapsto x+\gamma(\sigma)2x.

Moreover, for all
x,y\in E[4]
, we have
e_{4}(\sigma x,\sigma y)=\sigma e_{4}(x,y)
.
The result follows from substituting
\sigma x=x+\gamma(\sigma)2x
and
\sigma y=y+\gamma(\sigma)2y
into this identity.
∎

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\End^{+}(E[2])
denote the additive subgroup of
\End(E[2])
consisting of elements
\phi
such that

e(x,\phi(y))=e(\phi(x),y)

for all
x,y\in E[2]
(i.e. consisting of endomorphisms that are self-adjoint for the Weil pairing).

Remark 2.14
.

By Lemma
2.12
, the image of
\Gal\big(k(E[4])/k(\sqrt{-1})\big)
under
\gamma
is contained in
\End^{+}(E[2])
.

Note that
\End^{+}(E[2])
is a sub-Lie algebra of
\End(E[2])
(where we view the latter as a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
with Lie bracket given by the commutator).

Proposition 2.15
.

For
x\in E[2]
, denote by
\phi_{x}\in\End(E[2])
the endomorphism of
E[2]
defined by
\phi_{x}(y)=e(y,x)x
.
Then the following assertions hold.

(1)

The maps
\phi_{x}
, for
x\in E[2]
,
lie in
\End^{+}(E[2])
.

(2)

Let
x_{1},...,x_{2g}
be a basis for
E[2]
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space. Suppose that, for all
i\neq j
, we have
e(x_{i},x_{j})=1
. Then the maps
\{\phi_{x_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{2g}
generate
\End^{+}(E[2])
as a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

(3)

As an
\End^{+}(E[2])
-module,
E[2]
is simple.

(4)

The ring of
\End^{+}(E[2])
-equivariant endomorphisms of
E[2]
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Proof.

(1)
Straightforward computation.

(2)
This is essentially
[
42
, Lemma 3.2]
. Consider the isomorphism

(2.16)

\End(E[2])\cong E[2]\otimes\Hom(E[2],\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong E[2]\otimes E[2]

of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces,
where the first map is the standard isomorphism, and the second map is induced by the
Weil pairing on the second factor. Under this map,
\End^{+}(E[2])
corresponds to the
subgroup
\Sym_{2}(E[2])
of symmetric tensors. Moreover, for
x,y\in E[2]
, one computes that the map
\phi_{x}
corresponds to the pure tensor
x\otimes x
, and the commutator
\phi_{x}\phi_{y}-\phi_{y}\phi_{x}
corresponds to
e(x,y)(x\otimes y+y\otimes x).

In particular, our assumptions mean that the image under (
2.16
) of the Lie algebra generated by
\{\phi_{x_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{2g}
contains the elements
x_{i}\otimes x_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,....,2g\}
, and
x_{i}\otimes x_{j}+x_{j}\otimes x_{i}
for all
i,j\in\{1,...,2g\}
with
i<j
. Such elements span
\Sym_{2}(E[2])
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space.

(3)
Let
V
be a subspace of
E[2]
which is invariant under the action of
\End^{+}(E[2])
,
and suppose that a non-zero vector
y
lies in
V
. Given any
x\in E[2]
satisfying
e(x,y)=1
,
we see that
\phi_{x}(y)=e(x,y)x=x
lies in
V
. In particular,
V
contains both
0
and the
unique non-trivial coset of
\left\langle y\right\rangle^{\perp}
. This is only possible if
V=E[2]
.

(4)
Suppose
\alpha\in\End(E[2])
commutes with all elements of
\End^{+}(E[2])
. Then for any
x,y\in E[2]
, we have

0=(\alpha\phi_{x}-\phi_{x}\alpha)(y)=e(x,y)\alpha(x)-e(\alpha(y),x)x.

Take
x\neq 0
and take
y
to be any element of
E[2]
with
e(x,y)=1
. Then the above identity shows that
x\in E[2]
is an eigenvector for
\alpha
. That is, every
x\in E[2]
is an eigenvector for
\alpha
. This is only possible if
\alpha
is a scalar multiple of the identity (if
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are linearly independent, and if
\alpha(v_{i})=\lambda_{i}v_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then considering
\alpha(v_{1}+v_{2})
we see that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
).
∎

3.
Analytic preliminaries

For the rest of the section we fix the following data.
Let
L
be a finite Galois number field, let
n_{L}
be its degree, let
\Sigma
be a set
of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
2
,
\infty
, and all places that ramify in
L
,
let
N
be the product of the prime numbers corresponding to the finite places in
\Sigma
,
and let
\mathcal{P}_{L}
be the set of prime numbers
p\not\in\Sigma
that are totally split in
L/\mathbb{Q}
.

Given a subset
U\subset(\mathbb{Z}/4NZ)^{\times}
, we will be interested in the family of squarefree integers

\mathcal{F}_{U,L}=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}:n\text{ square-free, }p|n\Rightarrow p\in\mathcal{P}_{L},n\hskip-5.0pt\mod{4N}\in U\}.

For all
X\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{U,L}(X)=\{n\in\mathcal{F}_{U,L}:n<X\}
.

Note that if a prime number
p\not\in\Sigma
is totally split in
L
, then the
p
-Frobenius automorphism
in
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q})
is contained in the subgroup
S=\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/L\cap\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N}))
.
Thus, if we identify
S
with its image in
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
under the usual isomorphism
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/4NZ)^{\times}
,
then we have
\mathcal{F}_{U,L}=\mathcal{F}_{U\cap S,L}
.

If
U=\{c\}
is a
singleton, we will abbreviate
\{c\}
to
c
in the subscript. If we have
U=(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, then we will
drop it from the notation and just write
\mathcal{F}_{L}
, respectively
\mathcal{F}_{L}(X)
.
In particular, we also have
\mathcal{F}_{L}=\mathcal{F}_{S,L}
.

3.1.
Asymptotics for the families
\mathcal{F}_{U,L}(X)

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
4N
. Then we have

\sum_{n\in\mathcal{F}_{L}(X)}\chi(n)=c_{\chi}X(\log X)^{m(\chi)-1}+O(X(\log X)^{m(\chi)-2}),

where

m(\chi)={\Bigl\{\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\begin{array}[]{ll}\scriptstyle 1/n_{L},&\scriptstyle\chi|_{S}=\boldsymbol{1};\cr\scriptstyle 0,&\scriptstyle\text{otherwise},\end{array}}

and

c_{\chi}=\prod_{p\text{ prime}}\left(1+\frac{\chi(p)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p)}{p}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{m(\chi)}.

Proof.

Define the function
\rho_{\chi}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\rho_{\chi}(n)=\mu^{2}(n)\chi(n)\prod_{p|n}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p),

where recall that
\mu
denotes the Möbius function, and the product runs over the prime
divisors of
n
. This is
a Frobenian multiplicative function in the sense of
[
20
]
*Definition 2.7,
with corresponding Galois field
\tilde{L}=L\cdot\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})
. More precisely,
for every prime number
p
that is unramified in
\tilde{L}
we have

\rho_{\chi}(p)=\chi(\Frob_{p})\mathbbm{1}_{\Gal(\tilde{L}/L)}(\Frob_{p}),

where
\Frob_{p}\in\Gal(\tilde{L}/\mathbb{Q})
denotes any choice of Frobenius element at
p
,
and the character
\chi
is viewed as a character of
\Gal(\tilde{L}/\mathbb{Q})
lifted from
the quotient
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
.
The sum in the statement of the lemma is equal to
\sum_{n=1}^{X}\rho_{\chi}(n)
.
By
[
20
]
*Lemma 2.8 we have

\sum_{n=1}^{X}\rho_{\chi}(n)=c_{\chi}X(\log X)^{m(\chi)-1}+O(X(\log X)^{m(\chi)-2}),

where
c_{\chi}=\prod_{p\text{ prime}}\left(1+\frac{\chi(p)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p)}{p}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{m(\chi)}

and

\displaystyle m(\chi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[\tilde{L}:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\Gal(\tilde{L}/\mathbb{Q})}\chi(\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\Gal(\tilde{L}/L)}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[\tilde{L}:\mathbb{Q}]}\sum_{\sigma\in\Gal(\tilde{L}/L)}\chi(\sigma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{L}}\langle\chi|_{\Gal(\tilde{L}/L)},\boldsymbol{1}\rangle.

Here
\langle\;,\;\rangle
denotes the inner product of group characters, and
\boldsymbol{1}
, recall, denotes the trivial
character. Now considering
\chi
as a character of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q}
, that inner product
is equal to
\langle\chi|_{U},\boldsymbol{1}\rangle
, which is equal to
1
if
\chi
restricts to the trivial character
of
U
and
0
otherwise. This proves the lemma.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

There exists a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
U\subset S
one has

\#\mathcal{F}_{U,L}(X)=\#U\cdot cX(\log X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1}+O\left(\frac{X}{\log X}\right).

Proof.

For every
u\in S
, define
\rho_{u}\colon\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\rho_{u}(n)=\mu^{2}(n)\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\}}(n\hskip-5.0pt\mod 4N)\prod_{p|n}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p).

Then we have

\#\mathcal{F}_{U,L}(X)=\sum_{u\in U}\sum_{n=1}^{X}\rho_{u}(n).

Let
u\in S
. We have
\mathbbm{1}_{\{u\}}=\tfrac{1}{\varphi(4N)}\sum_{\chi}\chi(u)\chi
,
with the sum running over all characters of
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, and where
\varphi
denotes Euler’s totient function. Accordingly,
we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\varphi(4N)\sum_{n=1}^{X}\rho_{u}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\chi(u)\sum_{n=1}^{X}\mu^{2}(n)\chi(n)\prod_{p|n}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi}\chi(u)\sum_{n\in\mathcal{F}_{L}(X)}\chi(n),

where the summands
\chi
run over all Dirichlet characters modulo
4N
.
We now apply Lemma
3.1
. Note that the sum (
3.3
)
is dominated by the summands of the outer sum corresponding to those
\chi

for which
m(\chi)
is maximal, which are those that restrict to the trivial character on
S
.
These characters
\chi
, in particular, satisfy
\chi(u)=1
for all
u\in U
.
For each of those, the inner sum is
c_{\chi}X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1}+O(X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-2})
,
while for the others it is
O(X(\log X)^{-1})
.
It follows that we have

\#\mathcal{F}_{U,L}(X)=\#U\cdot cX(\log X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1}+O(X(\log X)^{-1})

with
c=\tfrac{1}{\varphi(4N)}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\chi:}{\chi|_{S}=\charfunc}}c_{\chi}
, where the sum runs
over the characters of
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
.

It remains to show that
c
is non-zero. Now, the function
\rho\colon\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{C}

given by
\rho(n)=\mu^{2}(n)\prod_{p|n}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{L}}(p)
is a Frobenian multiplicative
function: for every prime number
p\not\in\Sigma
we have

\rho(p)=\mathbbm{1}_{\{1\}}(\Frob_{p})
, where
\Frob_{p}
is a
Frobenius element at
p
in
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
. Moreover, we have

\#\mathcal{F}_{L}(X)=\sum_{n=1}^{X}\rho(n).

By
[
20
]
*Lemma 2.8 this is equal to
c_{\rho}X(\log X)^{m(\rho)-1}+O(X(\log X)^{m(\rho)-2})
,
where
m(\rho)=\frac{1}{n_{L}}>0
and
c_{\rho}
is real and positive.
Since we have
c_{\rho}=\#S\cdot c
, this proves the proposition.
∎

3.2.
Auxiliary analytic results

In the rest of this section, we collect some additional analytic results that will be useful later. We begin by recalling some well-known results.

For a positive integer
n
, we denote by
\omega(n)
the number of distinct prime factors of
n
.

Lemma 3.4

(
[
9
]
*§2.2, Lemma A)
.

There is an absolute constant
B_{0}
such that, for every real number
X\geq 3

and for every integer
l\geq 0
, we have

\#\{n\leq X~~\big|~~\omega(n)=l,~\mu^{2}(n)=1\}\leq\frac{B_{0}X}{\log(X)}\cdot\frac{(\log\log(X)+B_{0})^{l}}{l!}.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
A>0
be a real number. Then there is a constant
c(A)>0
such that, setting

\Omega=c(A)e\cdot(\log\log(X)+B_{0})
for
B_{0}
as in
Lemma

3.4
, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\#\{n\leq X~~\big|~~\mu^{2}(n)=1,~\omega(n)>\Omega\}\ll X\log(X)^{-A}.

Proof.

Fix some
c(A)>0
to be determined later, and let
\Omega
be as in the statement.
By
Lemma

3.4
, the left hand side of (
3.6
) is bounded above by

\frac{B_{0}X}{\log(X)}\sum_{l>\Omega}\frac{(\log\log(X)+B_{0})^{l}}{l!}\ll\frac{X}{\log(X)}\sum_{l>\Omega}\left(\frac{e\cdot(\log\log(X)+B_{0})}{l}\right)^{l}

where for the
\ll
we use Stirling’s formula. Since
l
is always larger than
\Omega
in the sum, we find

\#\{n\leq X~~\big|~~\mu^{2}(n)=1,~\omega(n)>\Omega\}\ll\frac{X}{\log(X)}\sum_{l>\Omega}c(A)^{-l}\ll\frac{X}{\log(X)}\cdot c(A)^{-\Omega}.

Choosing
c(A)
large enough gives the result.
∎

If
F/\mathbb{Q}
is a Galois extension,
G
is its Galois group, and
p
is a prime number
that is unramified in
F/\mathbb{Q}
, then we denote by
(F/\mathbb{Q},p)
the
conjugacy class in
G
of the Frobenius elements at
p
in
F/\mathbb{Q}
.
If
\chi
is the character of a complex representation of
G
, we write

\chi(p)=\begin{cases}\chi\big((F/\mathbb{Q},p)\big),~~&~~p\textup{ unramified in }F/\mathbb{Q};\\
0,~~&~~\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
d
be a square-free integer, and let
\chi
be the character of a non-trivial
irreducible complex representation of
\Gal(L(\sqrt{d})/\mathbb{Q})
.

Then for all real numbers
A>1
and
X\geq 2
we have

(3.8)

\bigg|\sum_{p\leq X}\chi(p)\bigg|\ll_{A}\sqrt{d}X\log(X)^{-A},

where the sum is taken over all primes
p\leq X
, and the implied constant is independent of
d
and
\chi
(but depends on
L
).

Proof.

Fix
A>1
. Henceforth we let all implied constants depend on
L
and
A
,
but not on
d
or
\chi
. We assume that
d\ll\log(X)^{2A-2}
, since
otherwise we can bound the left hand side of (
3.8
) by
\sum_{p\leq X}1
and use the Prime Number Theorem.

Write
F=L(\sqrt{d})
, write
G=\Gal(F/\mathbb{Q})
, and let
C
be a conjugacy class in
G
. Further, write

\pi_{C}(X)=\#\big\{p\leq X~~\colon~~(F/\mathbb{Q},p)\in C\big\}.

Claim:
We have

\bigg|\pi_{C}(X)-\frac{|C|}{|G|}\Li(X)\bigg|\ll X\log(X)^{-A}.

Proof of claim:
Denoting by
\Delta_{F}
the discriminant of
F/\mathbb{Q}
, by

[
38
, Lemma 7]
we have
\log(\Delta_{F})\ll\log(d)
. Given our assumption
on
d
, we may thus apply the effective Chebotarev density theorem
[
19
]

(we use the precise form stated as
[
34
, Théorème 2]
) to give

\bigg|\pi_{C}(X)-\frac{|C|}{|G|}\Li(X)\bigg|\ll\Li(X^{\beta})+X\log(X)^{-A},

where
\beta
is the possible exceptional zero of the Dedekind zeta function

\zeta_{F}(s)
of
F
. By
[
38
, Lemma 8]
, if such a
\beta
exists there
is a quadratic subextension
K/\mathbb{Q}
of
F/\mathbb{Q}
such that
\zeta_{K}(\beta)
is
0
also.
Since the discriminant of any quadratic subsextension is
\ll n
, we can apply
Siegel’s theorem
[
15
, Theorem 5.28 (2)]
to show that, for any fixed

\epsilon>0
, we have
1-\beta\gg d^{-\epsilon}
. Choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently
small in terms of
A
, our assumption on the size of
n
gives

\Li(X^{\beta})\ll X\log(X)^{-A}
, proving the claim.

Returning to the proof of the lemma, we compute

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{p\leq X}\chi(p)\bigg|=\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\textup{ conj. class}\\
\textup{of }G\end{subarray}}\hskip 10.00002pt\sum_{p\leq X,~(F/\mathbb{Q},p)\in C}\chi(C)\bigg|=\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\textup{ conj. class}\\
\textup{of }G\end{subarray}}\chi(C)\pi_{C}(X)\bigg|,

where
\chi(C)
is the common value of
\chi
on
C
. Both the number of conjugacy
classes of
G
, and the absolute value of any
\chi(C)
, is bounded indepepdently
of
d
. Since
\chi
is non-trivial, we have
\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)=0
,
so the result follows from the claim.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
m
be a
fixed positive integer and let
\psi
be a Dirichlet character modulo
m
, viewed
as a
1
-dimensional character of
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m})/\mathbb{Q})
(we allow
\psi
to be trivial).
Let
q
be a positive integer, and let
\chi
be a real Dirichlet character modulo
q
.
Suppose that for each irreducible character
\chi_{i}
of
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
, the product

\chi\chi_{i}\psi
, viewed as an irreducible character of
\Gal(L(\zeta_{mq})/\mathbb{Q})
, is non-trivial.

Then for each positive integer
r
and real numbers
\kappa\geq 1
,
A>1
, and
2\leq Y\leq X
, we have

(3.10)

\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\leq n\leq X\\
n\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(nr)\kappa^{-\omega(n)}\chi(n)\psi(n)\bigg|\ll_{A,\kappa}\frac{(\omega(r)+\sqrt{q})X}{\log(X)^{A}},

where the implied constant is independent of
Y
and
\chi
, but depends on
L
and
\psi
.

Proof.

The general case follows from the case
Y=2
, which we now restrict to. By

Proposition

3.5
it suffices to prove the statement with the
sum running only over those
n
that have at most

\Omega
prime factors, where
\Omega=\Omega(A)
is defined as in the statement
of that Proposition.We now partition the sum (
3.10
)
with that additional restriction according to the number
1\leq l\leq\Omega
of
prime factors of
n
. Write
n=n_{0}p
where
p
is the largest prime factor of
n
,
denote by
P^{+}\mskip-3.0mu(n_{0})
the largest prime factor of
n_{0}
if
n_{0}\neq 1
, and
let
P^{+}\mskip-3.0mu(1)=1
.
Note that the condition
n_{0}p\leq X
forces
n_{0}\leq X^{1-1/\Omega}
. By the
triangle inequality, it suffices to bound the sum

(3.11)

\sum_{l=0}^{\Omega-1}~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{0}\leq X^{1-1/\Omega}\\
n_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\\
\omega(n_{0})=l\end{subarray}}~\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P^{+}\mskip-3.0mu(n_{0})<p\leq X/n_{0}\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L},~(p,r)=1\end{subarray}}\psi(p)\chi(p)\bigg|.

We can drop the condition
(p,r)=1
in the innermost sum at the expense of adding
at most
\omega(r)
to its value, which we henceforth do. Replacing
r
by
Nr

we can assume that the condition
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}
is simply the condition that
p

splits completely in
L/\mathbb{Q}
. To treat this condition, we express the indicator
function of the identity element in
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
as a sum
\sum_{1\leq i\leq t}a_{i}\chi_{i}

for some complex numbers
a_{i}
and irreducible characters
\chi_{i}
of
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
.
Applying the triangle inequality once more, we see that it suffices to bound the sum

(3.12)

\sum_{l=0}^{\Omega-1}~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{0}\leq X^{1-1/\Omega}\\
n_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\\
\omega(n_{0})=l\end{subarray}}~\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq t}\bigg|\sum_{P^{+}\mskip-3.0mu(n_{0})\leq p\leq X/n_{0}}\chi_{i}(p)\psi(p)\chi(p)\bigg|+\omega(r)\bigg).

Since
\chi
is a real Dirichlet character modulo
q
, it corresponds to an
irreducible character of
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})/\mathbb{Q}
for some
d\ll q
. By assumption,
each
\chi_{i}\psi\chi
is a non-trivial irreducible character of
L(\zeta_{m})(\sqrt{d})
.
By
Lemma

3.7
, for any
B>1
, the quantity in (
3.12
) is bounded above by some constant (which may depend on
B
) times the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\Omega-1}~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{0}\leq X^{1-1/\Omega}\\
n_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\\
\omega(n_{0})=l\end{subarray}}~\left(\frac{\sqrt{q}X}{n_{0}}\log\left(\frac{X}{n_{0}}\right)^{-B}+\omega(r)\right)\ll_{B}\omega(r)X^{1-1/\Omega}+\sqrt{q}X\left(\frac{\log(X)}{\Omega}\right)^{-B}\sum_{1\leq n_{0}\leq X}\frac{1}{n_{0}}.

Choosing
B
to be sufficiently large compared to
A
gives the result.
∎

We will also want the following bound on divisor sums over Chebotarev sets.

Lemma 3.13
.

For every real
\kappa>0
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n\leq X\\
n\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(n)\kappa^{\omega(n)}\ll X\log(X)^{\frac{\kappa}{n_{L}}-1}.

Proof.

Let
f(n)
be the multiplicative function defined by

f(p^{k})=\begin{cases}\kappa,~~&~~p\in\mathcal{P}_{L},~k=1;\\
0,~~&~~\textup{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
[
23
, Corollary 2.15]
with this choice of
f
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n\leq X\\
n\in\mathcal{F}_{L}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(n)\kappa^{\omega(n)}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\frac{X}{\log(X)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{\kappa}{p}\right)\ll\frac{X}{\log(X)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{\kappa}.

Now, we have

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\ll\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\ll\log(X)^{1/n_{L}},

where the final inequality is an analogue of Merten’s theorem for Chebotarev sets;
see
[
1
]
for a precise asymptotic for the product
\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq X\\
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}\end{subarray}}(1-p^{-1})^{-1}
.
∎

4.
Moments of the Selmer group

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety, let
g
be its dimension, and assume that
E
has full rational
2
-torsion. In this section we derive a formula for the moments of the distribution of
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
, where
d
runs through a family of square-free integers defined by certain frobenian conditions.
In the next section we will evaluate this formula under additional assumptions
on the Galois module
E[4]
, and will deduce
Theorem

1.6
.

4.1.
Setup

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois number field. Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places containing
2
,
\infty
, all places of bad reduction for
E
, and all places that ramify in
L
.

The quadratic twist family.

Let
N
be the product of the prime numbers corresponding
to the finite places in
\Sigma
, let
d_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
be a divisor of
N
(we allow
d_{0}<0
),
and let
c\in(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{L}
,
\mathcal{F}_{L}
,
\mathcal{F}_{c,L}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{c,L}(X)
be as defined in Section
3.1
. To ease notation in what follows, we omit
L
from the subscripts. We write
d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}=\{d_{0}D:D\in\mathcal{F}_{c}\}
. For all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we will compute the
r
-th moment of
\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

as
d
runs over
d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
.

Let
S
be the image of
\mathcal{F}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, so that the family
\mathcal{F}_{c}

is non-empty if and only if we have
c\in S
. Equivalently,

S
is the image of the Galois group of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/L\cap\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})

under the natural isomorphism
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q})\to(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
.
We assume henceforth that we have
c\in S
. We remark that the pair
(c,d_{0})
determines a unique class
b=(b_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
with the property that, for every
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
, we have
d\equiv b\textup{ mod }\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
. The constructions in this section will typically depend on
(d_{0},c)
only through the class
b
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
denote the image of
S
in
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times 2}
.
Write
K_{\Sigma}
for the maximal multiquadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
unramified at all primes outside
\Sigma
.
For every positive integer
m
that is
coprime to
N
, let
\sigma_{m}\in\Gal(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})
denote the image of
m

under the Artin map. The map
m\mapsto\sigma_{m}
factors through

(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times 2}
, and the image of
\mathbb{S}

under this map is
\Gal(K_{\Sigma}/K_{\Sigma}\cap L)
. Recall that, for a squarefree integer

m
, we denote by
\psi_{m}
the corresponding
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-valued quadratic character. Later on, we will often view
\mathbb{S}
as a subgroup of
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, as explained in Notation
4.50
. This identification is made in such a way that if
m\in\mathcal{F}
is a lift of
s\in\mathbb{S}
, then
s
corresponds to the tuple
(\psi_{m})_{v\in\Sigma}
.

The abelian variety
A
.

Fix
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. We write
A=E^{r}
, and equip
A
with the product polarisation.
For a squarefree integer
d
, we have
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}
.

We identify
A[2]
with
A_{d}[2]
as in Section
2
.
For each place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
we write

\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}\colon A_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])

for the coboundary map associated with the multiplication-by-
2
Kummer sequence for
A_{d}
,
and denote by
\mathscr{S}_{d,v}^{(r)}
the image of
\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}
. When
d=1
, we drop it from the notation. As in Section
2
, we denote by

e\colon A[2]\times A[2]\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

the Weil pairing, taken to have values in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
It is the sum of the corresponding pairings on
E[2]
.
For each place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
, we denote by

(4.1)

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{v}\colon H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

the local Tate pairing associated to
e
, and denote by
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
the sum, over
v\in\Sigma
, of these pairings.

The map

\gamma\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\End E[2]
of
Definition

2.10
factors through
\textup{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}(E[4])/\mathbb{Q})
, hence through
\Gal(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\Gamma\subset\End E[2]
be the image of
\Gal\big(\mathbb{Q}(E[4])/\mathbb{Q}(E[4])\cap L\big)\cong\textup{Gal}(L(E[4])/L)
under this map.
Let
\gamma^{(r)}\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\End A[2]
be the composition of
\gamma
with the
diagonal embedding
\End E[2]\to\End A[2]
. We view
\Gamma
as operating on
A[2]
via this map.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we denote by
\pi_{i}\colon A[2]^{2}\to A[2]
the
i
-th projection. Define
\Phi\colon A[2]^{2}\times A[2]^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}

to be the map sending
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
to

\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})+\pi_{1}(\mathbf{v}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})).

Parametrising cohomology groups.

For a finite set
\Sigma^{\prime}
of places,
write
H^{1}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])
for the subgroup of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])=\Hom_{\cts}(G_{\mathbb{Q}},A[2])

consisting of homomorphisms unramified outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Define

V_{0}=H^{1}_{\Sigma}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])=\textup{Hom}(\textup{Gal}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q}),A[2])\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptZ_{0}=\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}^{(r)}.

Given
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
we write
\mathcal{P}(d)
for the set of primes outside
\Sigma
which divide
d
(thus if
d=d_{0}D
with
D\in\mathcal{F}_{c}
, then
\mathcal{P}(d)
is the set of prime factors of
D
). Define finite-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces

V^{\prime}(d)=H^{1}_{\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptZ^{\prime}(d)=\bigoplus_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\mathscr{S}_{d,v}^{(r)}.

We also set
Z(d)=\oplus_{p\in\mathcal{P}(d)}\mathscr{S}_{d,p}^{(r)}
. Writing
\mathcal{A}(d)
for the subgroup of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{F}_{2})
generated by the characters
\{\psi_{p}\colon p\in\mathcal{P}(d)\}
, we define
V(d)=\mathcal{A}(d)\otimes A[2]
, viewed as a subgroup of
H^{1}_{\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])
. Note that we have

V^{\prime}(d)=V_{0}\oplus V(d)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptZ^{\prime}(d)=Z_{0}\oplus Z(d).

Summing the
local Tate pairings (
4.1
) over
v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)
gives a pairing

(4.2)

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{d}\colon V^{\prime}(d)\times Z^{\prime}(d)\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2},

defined by the formula

\left\langle x,y\right\rangle=\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\left\langle\res_{v}(x),y_{v}\right\rangle_{v},

where we write
y=(y_{v})_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\in Z^{\prime}(d)
. When the choice of
d
is clear from context, we often omit it from the notation. Thus, for example, we will typically write
V
and
Z
in place of
V(d)
and
Z(d)
, respectively, and write
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle
in place of
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{d}
.

4.2.
Counting
2
-Selmer elements

From now until Section
4.6
,
we
fix
a
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
. Write
d=d_{0}D
, with
D\in\mathcal{F}_{c}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The left kernel of the pairing (
4.2
) is
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Proof.

For a prime
p\notin\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)
, Lemma
2.1
gives
\mathscr{S}_{d,p}^{(r)}=H^{1}_{\ur}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},A[2])
.
Consequently,
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is the subspace of
V^{\prime}
consisting of elements
whose restriction to
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
lies in
Z^{\prime}
.
The result now follows from the fact that each summand
\mathscr{S}_{d,v}^{(r)}
of
Z^{\prime}

is maximal isotropic with respect to the local Tate pairing.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

We have

(4.5)

\frac{1}{\#Z^{\prime}}\sum_{b\in Z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\left\langle a,b\right\rangle}=\begin{cases}1,&a\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q});\\
0,&\textup{else}.\end{cases}

Proof.

For any
a\in V^{\prime}
, write
\alpha_{a}\colon Z^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
for the homomorphism

b\mapsto\left\langle a,b\right\rangle
. By Lemma
4.3
,
one has
a\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
if and only if
\alpha_{a}
is identically
0
. One then has

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#Z^{\prime}}\sum_{b\in Z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\left\langle a,b\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#Z^{\prime}}(\#\ker(\alpha_{a})-\#\alpha^{-1}_{a}(1))=\begin{cases}1,&\alpha_{a}=0;\\
0,&\textup{else},\end{cases}

as claimed.
∎

Combining Lemma
4.4
with the decompositions

Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\oplus Z
and
V^{\prime}=V_{0}\oplus V
, we obtain

(4.6)

\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}=\frac{1}{\#Z_{0}}\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\frac{1}{\#Z}\sum_{(v,z)\in V\times Z}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z\right\rangle+\left\langle v,z_{0}\right\rangle+\left\langle v,z\right\rangle}.

The following lemma gives a parametrisation for the spaces
V
and
Z
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The associations
\Map(\mathcal{P}(d),A[2])\rightarrow V
and
\Map(\mathcal{P}(d),A[2])\rightarrow Z
given by

\epsilon\mapsto\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}(d)}\psi_{p}\epsilon(p)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\eta\mapsto\sum_{p\mid\mathcal{P}(d)}\delta_{d,p}^{(r)}(\eta(p))

are bijections.

Proof.

For the parameterization of
V
,
having defined
V
as
\mathcal{A}\otimes A[2]
, we may uniquely write any element of
V

in the form
\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}(d)}\psi_{p}x_{p}
for some
x_{p}\in A[2]
.

For the parameterization of
Z
, note that by Lemma
2.1
(applied to
A
), any element of
Z
can be
expressed uniquely in the form
\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}(d)}\delta_{p,d}^{(r)}(a_{p})
for some elements
a_{p}
of
A[2]
.
∎

Recall that
d=d_{0}D
, with
D\in\mathcal{F}_{c}
. Suppose we have a pair
(v,z)\in V\times Z
. By Lemma
4.7
,
this corresponds to a pair of functions
(\epsilon,\eta)
from
\mathcal{P}(d)
to
A[2]
.
For fixed
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
we define the squarefree integer

(4.8)

D_{\mathbf{u}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{P}(d)\\
(\epsilon(p),\eta(p))=\mathbf{u}\end{subarray}}p.

In this way the pair
(v,z)
determines a factorisation

(4.9)

D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}D_{\mathbf{u}}
