of
D
into positive coprime integers.
Conversely, a factorisation of the form (
4.9
) uniquely
determines the functions
\epsilon
and
\eta
: for each
p\mid D
, define

(\epsilon(p),\eta(p))
to be the unique
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
such that
p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}
. We have thus shown the following. In the statement, recall that for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we denote by
\pi_{i}:A[2]^{2}\to A[2]
the
i
-th projection.

Lemma 4.10
.

The rule (
4.8
) defines a bijection between
V\times Z
and the collection of factorisations
of
D=d/d_{0}
as a product of pairwise coprime positive squarefree integers indexed by
elements
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
, as in (
4.9
). The element
(v,z)\in V\times Z
associated to such a factorisation is given by

(4.11)

v=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptz=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\textbf{u
}}}\delta_{p,d}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})).

4.3.
Aside: the support of an element of
V^{\prime}\times Z^{\prime}

The following definition will be useful later.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(v,z)\in V\times Z
, and let
D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}D_{\mathbf{u}}
be the corresponding factorisation of
D
. Then we define the
support

T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
of
(v,z)
to be the subset

T=\{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}~~\colon~~D_{\mathbf{u}}>1\}.

Given an element
(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in V^{\prime}\times Z^{\prime}
, written as
(v_{0}+v,z_{0}+z)
according to the decompositions
V^{\prime}=V_{0}\oplus V
and
Z^{\prime}=Z_{0}\oplus Z
above, we define the support of
(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})
to be the support of
(v,z)
.

Suppose we have
x,y\in A[2]
. Then
\delta_{d}^{(r)}(x)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])
lies in

V^{\prime}
and is an element of
\Sel_{2}(A_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
. In particular, it is in the left
kernel of the pairing (
4.2
). Similarly, the element

\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(y)
of
Z^{\prime}
is in the right
kernel of the pairing, by reciprocity. Thus given
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\in Z^{\prime}
, we
can adjust
v^{\prime}
by
\delta_{d}^{(r)}(x)
and
z^{\prime}
by

\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(y)
without changing the value
of
\left\langle v^{\prime},z^{\prime}\right\rangle
. For this flexibility to be useful later,
it will be necessary to understand how the corresponding factorisation of
D
changes upon adjusting
v^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}
in this way.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}
, and
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
. Then the map

(4.14)

(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})\longmapsto\Big(v^{\prime}+\delta_{d}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w})),z^{\prime}+\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(D)}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}))\Big)

is a bijection from the set of elements of
V^{\prime}\times Z^{\prime}
with support
T
to the set of elements with support
T+\mathbf{w}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})=(v_{0}+v,z_{0}+z)
has support
T
, and let
D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}}
be the corresponding factorisation of
(v,z)
. Then from (
4.11
) and Lemma
2.11
we have

\displaystyle v_{0}+v+\delta_{d}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{0}+\delta_{d_{0}}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))+\psi_{D}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w})+\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{0}+\delta_{d_{0}}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))+\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})+\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{0}+\delta_{d_{0}}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))+\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{w}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}).

Similarly, one computes

z_{0}+z+\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(D)}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}))=z_{0}+\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}))+\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{w}}}\delta_{p,d}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})).

Consequently, we see that the right hand side of (
4.14
) is equal to
(\widetilde{v_{0}}+\widetilde{v},\widetilde{z_{0}}+\widetilde{z})
, where

(\widetilde{v_{0}},\widetilde{z_{0}})=\Big(v_{0}+\delta_{d_{0}}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w})),z_{0}+\sum_{v\in\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}))\Big)\in V_{0}\times Z_{0},

and where
(\widetilde{v},\widetilde{z})\in V\times Z
corresponds to the factorisation
D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{w}}
.
∎

Recall that
A[2]=E[2]^{r}=E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, so that
V^{\prime}=H^{1}_{\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])=H^{1}_{\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])^{r}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in V^{\prime}\times Z^{\prime}
, let
T
be its support. Then the
r
-tuple of
elements of
H^{1}_{\Sigma\cup\mathcal{P}(d)}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
corresponding to
v^{\prime}
consists
of
r
vectors that are
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linearly independent in

\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])}{H^{1}_{\Sigma}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])+\delta_{d}(E[2])}

if and only if there exist
\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}
, and a codimension
1
subspace

M\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, such that
\pi_{1}(T+\mathbf{w})\subseteq E[2]\otimes M
.

Proof.

Let
p_{i}\colon E[2]^{r}\rightarrow E[2]
denote the
i
-th projection. From (
4.11
) and the definition of the support of
(v^{\prime},z^{\prime})
, we see that the
r
-vectors
\{p_{i}(v^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{r}
fail to be linearly independent in the quotient space appearing in the statement if and only if there exist
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
, not all
0
, such that

\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}))\in H^{1}_{\Sigma}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])+\delta_{d}(E[2]).

By an analogous argument to that used in the proof of Lemma
4.13
, we see that this happens, for given
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{r}
, if and only if there is
x\in E[2]
such that

(4.16)

\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}))=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in T}\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}x.

Since the positive integers
\{D_{\mathbf{u}}\}_{\mathbf{u}\in T}
are all greater than
1
and coprime,
the characters
\{\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\}_{\mathbf{u}\in T}
are linearly independent. We thus see
that (
4.16
) is equivalent to the equality

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}))=x
holding for all
\mathbf{u}\in T
. With

\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{r}
and
x
fixed as in (
4.16
),
define
\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
to be the linear map sending the
i
-th
basis vector to
\lambda_{i}
. Note that this is surjective since some
\lambda_{i}

is assumed non-zero. The induced surjective map

1\otimes\phi\colon E[2]^{r}=E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\rightarrow E[2]
sends
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})

to
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}))
, so we can write this final equality
as
(1\otimes\phi)(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{w}))=0
, where
\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}
is any element
mapping to
x
under
(1\otimes\phi)\circ\pi_{1}
. Now

\ker(1\otimes\phi)=E[2]\otimes\ker(\phi)
(one inclusion is obvious and the other follows from counting dimensions), so we conclude that the condition (
4.16
) is equivalent to the condition that
\pi_{1}(T+\mathbf{w})\subseteq E[2]\otimes\ker(\phi)
. From this, one readily obtains the result.
∎

4.4.
Counting
2
-Selmer elements (cont.)

We now use the explicit parameterization of
V\times Z
given in Lemma
4.10
to convert (
4.6
) into a sum of Jacobi symbols. Recall that we have fixed
d=d_{0}D\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
. Recall also that we denote by
\Phi
the map

A[2]^{2}\times A[2]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

sending a pair
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
in
A[2]^{2}\times A[2]^{2}
to

\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})+\pi_{1}(\mathbf{v}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})).

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
(v,z)\in V\times Z
, corresponding via Lemma
4.10
to a factorisation of
D
of the form (
4.9
). Then we have

(-1)^{\left\langle v,z\right\rangle}=\prod_{\mathbf{u}}\left(\frac{-d_{0}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))}(-1)^{e\left(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{D_{\mathbf{u}}})\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right)}~\cdot~\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})},

where the first product is taken over all
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
, and the
second product is taken over all pairs of distinct elements
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}

of
A[2]^{2}
.

Proof.

From (
4.11
) we find that
\left\langle v,z\right\rangle
is equal to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}\\
\mathbf{v}\in A[2]^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\delta_{d,p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\right\rangle_{p}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}\\
\mathbf{v}\in A[2]^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))+\psi_{d}\cdot\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})\right\rangle_{p},

where the second equality follows from Lemma
2.11
. Now
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})\cup\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))
is a quadratic character that is unramified outside
\Sigma
. Consequently, recalling the pairing
H_{p}
for Section
2.5
, we have

\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\right\rangle_{p}=H_{p}\big(\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}},\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})\cup\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\big)=\begin{cases}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})\cup\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})))(\sigma_{p}),~~&~~p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}};\\
0,~~&~~\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Now
(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})\cup\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})))(\sigma_{p})
is equal to
e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{p})\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))
.
Since the
D_{\mathbf{u}}
are coprime as
\mathbf{u}
varies over elements of
A[2]^{2}
,
we conclude that we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}\\
\mathbf{v}\in A[2]^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))\right\rangle_{p}=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}e\big(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{D_{\mathbf{u}}})\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\big).

Since
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{p}=H_{p}\otimes e
, we have

(-1)^{\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\psi_{d}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})\right\rangle_{p}}=(-1)^{H_{p}(\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}},\psi_{d})e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))}=(D_{\mathbf{u}}~,~d)_{p}^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))},

where
(~,~)_{p}
is the quadratic Hilbert symbol at
p
.
Evaluating the Hilbert symbol in terms of Legendre symbols and recalling that
d=d_{0}D
, we find

(-1)^{\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\psi_{d}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})\right\rangle_{p}}=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{p}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))},~~&~~p\nmid D_{\mathbf{u}};\\
\left(\frac{-d_{0}D/D_{\mathbf{u}}}{p}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))},~~&~~p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}.\end{cases}

Consequently, temporarily writing
t={\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}\\
\mathbf{v}\in A[2]^{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})~,~\psi_{d}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v})\right\rangle_{p}}
, we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{t}\hskip 10.00002pt=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))}~\cdot~\prod_{\mathbf{u}}\left(\frac{-d_{0}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))}~\cdot~\prod_{\mathbf{u}}\left(\frac{D/D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))}.

Now note that the rightmost product is equal to
\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{v}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))}
.∎

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
z\in Z
, written in the form (
4.11
), and let
v_{0}\in V_{0}
. Then we have

(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z\right\rangle}=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}.

Proof.

From (
4.11
) and Lemma
2.11
we have

\left\langle v_{0},z\right\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}}\left\langle v_{0},\delta_{d,p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))\right\rangle_{p}=\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}}\left\langle v_{0},\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))+\psi_{d}\cdot\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{p}.

Since both
\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))
and
v_{0}
are
unramified at all
p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}
, it follows that
\big\langle v_{0},\delta_{p}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))\big\rangle_{p}
is
zero at all
p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}
. Next, since
\psi_{d}
and
\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}
differ by a quadratic
character unramified at all
p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}
, and since
v_{0}
is unramified at all
p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}
, it follows that

\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}}\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{d}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{p}=\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}}\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{p}.

Since both
v_{0}
and
\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}
are unramified outside
\Sigma\cup\{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}\}
, it follows from reciprocity that we have

\sum_{p\mid D_{\mathbf{u}}}\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{p}=\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{\Sigma},

from which the result follows.
∎

The remaining term in (
4.6
) is
(-1)^{\left\langle v,z_{0}\right\rangle}
. With
v
written in the form (
4.11
) above, we have

(4.19)

(-1)^{\left\langle v,z_{0}\right\rangle}=\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}(-1)^{\left\langle\psi_{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}.

To explicate (
4.19
) further we would have to examine more
closely the Selmer conditions at the ‘bad’ primes
p\mid N
. However, it will
suffice for now to note that the dependence of the value of (
4.19
)
on the factors
D_{\mathbf{u}}
is only mild: it only depends on the values of
D_{\mathbf{u}}
modulo
4N
, since
those values determine the classes of
D_{\mathbf{u}}
in
\prod_{p\mid N}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times 2}
.

Notation 4.20
.

For each
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
,
v_{0}\in V_{0}
, and
z_{0}\in Z_{0}
, there is a unique quadratic Dirichlet character
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}

modulo
4N
that, for all squarefree integers
m
coprime to
N
, satisfies

\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}(m)=(-1)^{\left\langle\psi_{m}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle v_{0},\psi_{m}\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\cdot\left(\frac{-d_{0}}{m}\right)^{e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))}\cdot(-1)^{e\left(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{m})\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})\right)}.

Proposition 4.21
.

With the notation above, we have

\displaystyle\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}=\frac{1}{\#Z_{0}}\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{D=\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}2^{-2rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})},

where the inner sum is taken over all factorisations of
D=d/d_{0}
as a product
of pairwise coprime positive squarefree integers indexed by
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
,
as in (
4.9
), the first product is taken
over all
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
, and the second product is taken over all pairs
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})

of distinct elements of
A[2]^{2}
.

Proof.

Note first that
\#Z=\#A[2]^{\omega(D)}=2^{2rg\omega(D)}
. The result now follows by combining
(
4.6
) with (
4.19
) and Lemmas

4.17
and
4.18
.
∎

4.5.
A formula for the
r
-th moment of the Selmer group

We now consider the effect of varying
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
in the formula for

\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}
given in Proposition
4.21
.
We begin by noting that the subspace

Z_{0}=\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}\mathscr{S}_{d,v}^{(r)}
of
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])

is independent of
d
, since the fixed image
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
of
d
determines the
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
-isomorphism class of
A_{d}
for each

v\in\Sigma
(recall that
b
is a function of
d_{0}
and
c
). The Dirichlet characters
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}
, and the subspace
V_{0}
of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},A[2])
, are independent of
d
by definition. It will also be useful later to note that the characters
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}
are independent of
c
, and that
V_{0}
is independent of both
c
and
d_{0}
.

Definition 4.22
.

We define
\mathcal{D}(X)
to be the set of
all
\#A[2]^{2}
-tuples
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}
of positive, pairwise coprime,
squarefree integers
D_{\mathbf{u}}
satisfying

(4.23)

\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}D_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{F}_{c}(X).

For a subset
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
, we define
\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
to be the subset
of
\mathcal{D}(X)
consisting of tuples
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}
for which

D_{\mathbf{u}}>1
precisely when
\mathbf{u}\in T
(cf.
Definition

4.12
). Note that

\mathcal{D}(X)
is the disjoint union of the sets
\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
as
T
ranges over all subsets of
A[2]^{2}
. Finally, for
z_{0}\in Z_{0}
,
v_{0}\in V_{0}
, and
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
, define

(4.24)

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\sum_{(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}2^{-2rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})},

where the products are taken over
A[2]^{2}
.

Proposition 4.25
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}=\frac{1}{\#Z_{0}}~\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle}\sum_{T\subseteq A[2]^{2}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X).

Proof.

Sum the formula in the statement of Proposition
4.21
over
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)
.
∎

Remark 4.26
.

It follows from
Lemma

4.13
(or rather, its proof and the discussion preceding the statement) that for any
\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}
, we have

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\mathcal{S}^{T+\mathbf{w}}_{\widetilde{v_{0}},\widetilde{z_{0}}}(X),

where

\widetilde{v_{0}}=v_{0}+\delta_{d_{0}}^{(r)}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{w}))\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{z_{0}}=z_{0}+\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\delta_{d,v}^{(r)}(\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w})).

4.6.
Asymptotics for the sums
\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}

A key role in understanding the sum (
4.24
) is played by the notion of
linked indices
.

Definition 4.27
.

Let
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
be distinct elements of
A[2]^{2}
. We say that
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are
linked
if precisely one of the symbols

\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ or }\hskip 10.00002pt\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{v}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)

appears with exponent
1
in the sum (
4.24
), equivalently
if

\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})+\Phi(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u})=1.

Denote by
q\colon A[2]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
the non-degenerate quadratic form on
A[2]^{2}
sending
\mathbf{u}
to

q(\mathbf{u})=e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u})).

We have

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})+\Phi(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u})+\pi_{1}(\mathbf{v}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{v}))+e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{v})+\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}).

In particular,
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are linked if and only if
q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})=1
.

We call a subset
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
a
maximal unlinked subset
if no two
elements
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}
of
T
are linked, and if
T
is maximal with
respect to this property. The following lemma is essentially
[
13
, Lemma 7]
(see also
[
7
, Lemma 18]
).

Lemma 4.28
.

The maximal unlinked subsets of
A[2]^{2}
are precisely those of the form

\mathbf{c}+W
where
\mathbf{c}\in A[2]^{2}
, and
W
is a maximal isotropic
subspace with respect to the quadratic form
q
. In particular, every maximal unlinked subset has size
\#A[2]=4^{rg}
.

Proof.

As above,
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in A[2]^{2}
are unlinked if and only if
q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})=0
.
From this it follows that if
T
is unlinked, then so is
\mathbf{c}+T
for any

\mathbf{c}\in A[2]^{2}
. Now fix a maximal unlinked subset
W
of
A[2]^{2}
with

0\in W
, so that
q(\mathbf{u})=0
for all
\mathbf{u}\in W
. Given
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in W
,
we claim that
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
is in
W
. Indeed, given any
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{U}
, since
q
is a quadratic form we have

q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{w})=q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v})+q(\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{w})+q(\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{w})+q(\mathbf{u})+q(\mathbf{v})+q(\mathbf{w})=0.

So
\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are unlinked for each
\mathbf{w}\in W
. Maximality of
W

then gives the claim. Consequently,
W
is an isotropic subspace of
A[2]^{2}
with
respect to
q
, and it is immediate that such subspaces are necessarily unlinked.
This proves the characterisation of maximal unlinked subsets.

Finally, since the first factor of
A[2]^{2}
is visibly isotropic for
q
, we see that

q
contains at least one isotropic subspace of size
\dim A[2]=\frac{1}{2}\dim A[2]^{2}
.
By
[
6
, Corollary 8.12]
it follows that all maximal isotropic
subspaces of
A[2]^{2}
have this dimension, completing the proof.
∎

4.6.1.
The error term

The aim of this subsection is to prove the following bound. In what follows we will write
n_{L}
for the degree of
L/\mathbb{Q}
.

Proposition 4.29
.

Suppose that
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
is not maximal unlinked. Then

|\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)|\ll X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg+1}}}.

To begin the proof, we first divide the range of summation into intervals.
Set
\Delta=1+\log(X)^{-4^{rg}}
and divide the ranges of the variables
D_{\mathbf{u}}

into intervals of the form
[\Delta^{n},\Delta^{n+1}]
for
n=0,1,2,...
. Note that
the only integer in the
n=0
interval is
1
. For
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
we let

A_{\mathbf{u}}
denote a real number
1
1

1

We have chosen the notation
A_{\mathbf{u}}
to align with
[
7
]
. We hope that the similarity with the notation for twists of the abelian variety
A
will not be confusing.
of the form
\Delta^{n}
with
1\leq\Delta^{n}\leq X
,
let
\mathbf{A}=(A_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}
, and define

(4.30)

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)\\
A_{\mathbf{u}}\leq D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}2^{-2rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}.

Since for small positive
\alpha
we have
\log(1+\alpha)\approx\alpha
, it
follows that for large
X
we have
\log(X)/\log(\Delta)\approx\log(X)^{1+4^{rg}}
,
so the number of choices of
\mathbf{A}
for which the sum in (
4.30
)
is non-empty is
O(\log(X)^{(1+4^{rg})\cdot 16^{rg}})
.
Following
[
7
, Section 5.4]
we split the collection of all such
choices of
\mathbf{A}
into families.

First family:
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}A_{\mathbf{u}}
large.
The first family is defined by the condition

(4.31)

\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}A_{\mathbf{u}}\geq\Delta^{-16^{rg}}X.

We treat this family as in
[
7
, Equations (33)-(34)]
. We have

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{\mathbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:first_family_of_A}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:first_family_of_A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)\\
A_{\mathbf{u}}\leq D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta^{-16^{r}}X\leq n\leq X}\mu^{2}(n)\cdot 4^{rg\omega(n)}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle(1-\Delta^{-16^{rg}})X\log(X)^{4^{rg}-1}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle X\log(X)^{-1}.

Here for the second inequality we take
n=\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}}
. The term
4^{rg\omega(n)}

arises as the product
4^{-rg\omega(n)}\cdot 16^{rg\omega(n)}
where
16^{rg\omega(n)}

is the number of ways of writing a squarefree
n
as an ordered product
of
16^{rg}

positive integers. The third inequality is
[
7
, Lemma 12]
. For the final inequality, we note that

\displaystyle 1-\Delta^{-16^{rg}}=1-(1+\log(X)^{-4^{rg}})^{-16^{rg}}=1-(1-16^{rg}\log(X)^{-4^{rg}}+O(\log(X)^{-2\cdot 4^{rg}})).

In summary, we have

(4.32)

\bigg|\sum_{\mathbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:first_family_of_A}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})\bigg|\ll X\log(X)^{-1}.

Note that if
\mathbf{A}
does not satisfy (
4.31
) then the
condition
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq X
is automatically satisfied for any
collection
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}
with
A_{\mathbf{u}}\leq D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}
.
We thus ignore this condition henceforth.

Second family:
2
large linked variables.
Set
X^{\dagger}=\log(X)^{3(1+16^{rg}(1+4^{rg}))}
.
We now consider
\mathbf{A}
satisfying

(4.33)

\prod_{\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}}A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq\Delta^{-16^{rg}}X,\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{there exist linked }\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\textup{ such that }A_{\mathbf{u}},A_{\mathbf{v}}\geq X^{\dagger}.

The argument follows
[
7
, Equations (40)-(42)]
drawing on results of
Heath-Brown
[
14
]
exploiting double oscillation of Jacobi symbols.
Specifically, given
\mathbf{A}
satisfying (
4.33
)
and corresponding linked variables
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}
, after swapping
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
if necessary, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq D_{\mathbf{w}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{w}}\\
\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}\\
1\leq D_{\mathbf{v}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{v}}\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{v}}\equiv h\pmod{4N}\end{subarray}}f_{1}(D_{\mathbf{u}})f_{2}(D_{\mathbf{v}})\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)\bigg|,

where in the outer sum the
D_{\mathbf{w}}
are coprime to
N
and to each other, and
in the inner sum the
D_{\mathbf{u}}
and
D_{\mathbf{v}}
are odd and coprime integers with no
further constraints than those shown. The terms
h
,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
depend on

(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
. Specifically, they are defined as

h=c(\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}D_{\mathbf{w}})^{-1}\pmod{4N}
,

\displaystyle f_{1}(D_{\mathbf{u}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{F}\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\geq A_{\mathbf{u}}\end{subarray}}~4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\mu^{2}\bigg(D_{\mathbf{u}}\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0muD_{\mathbf{w}}\bigg)\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{w}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})}\mskip-3.0mu\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{w}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{u})},

and
f_{2}(D_{\mathbf{v}})
is defined similarly by swapping
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
in the definition of
f_{1}
.
The constraint that the collections
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}}
arising lie in

\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
rather than just
\mathcal{D}(X)
is automatically satisfied thanks to
(
4.33
). Since the functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}

are bounded above by
1
in absolute value, we can use
[
7
, Lemma 15]

to bound the inner sum, provided we can remove the congruence condition on

D_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{v}}
. To do this, we split the inner sum into sub-sums by
fixing values
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
modulo
4N
whose product is
h
, and insisting
that
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv h_{1}
and
D_{\mathbf{v}}\equiv h_{2}
. These conditions can then be
moved into the functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Now applying
[
7
, Lemma 15]

with
\epsilon=1/6
to the inner sum, and summing the result over the remaining variables gives

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}^{T}(X,\mathbf{A})|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}A_{\mathbf{u}}A_{\mathbf{v}}(X^{\dagger})^{-1/3}\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}\Delta A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq X(X^{\dagger})^{-1/3}.

Summing over the
O(\log(X)^{(1+4^{rg})\cdot 16^{rg}})
-many possibilities for
\mathbf{A}
gives

(4.34)

\sum_{\mathbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:second_family_defining_conditions}}\mathcal{S}^{T}(X,\mathbf{A})\ll X\log(X)^{-1}.

Third family:
1
large and
1
small linked variables.

We now take
\epsilon>0
to be chosen later, and define
X^{\ddagger}=\Delta^{l}

to be the least integral power of
\Delta
that is larger than
\exp(\log(X)^{\epsilon})
.
Note that for sufficiently large
X
we have
X^{\ddagger}>X^{\dagger}
. We now consider all
\mathbf{A}
satisfying

(4.35)

\textup{Neither \eqref{eq:first_family_of_A} nor \eqref{eq:second_family_defining_conditions} hold,
and }\exists~\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}\textup{ linked with }1<A_{\mathbf{v}}<X^{\dagger},~A_{\mathbf{u}}\geq X^{\ddagger}.

Note once again that, given
\mathbf{A}
satisfying (
4.35
),
if we have
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
with
A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq D_{\mathbf{w}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{w}}

for each
\mathbf{w}
, then
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}}
is automatically in
\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
.

Taking
\mathbf{A}
satisfying (
4.35
), with corresponding
linked indices
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{u}
, in the expression (
4.30
) for

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})
we group all terms involving
D_{\mathbf{u}}
. By quadratic
reciprocity for Jacobi symbols, if we fix
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}
, there is a
real Dirichlet character
\chi_{\mathbf{u},(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}}
modulo
4N
such that

\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{w}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{w}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{u})}=\chi_{\mathbf{u},(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{w}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})+\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{u})}.

We then have

|\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq D_{\mathbf{w}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{w}}\\
\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}\end{subarray}}\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{\mathbf{u}}\leq D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv cd_{1}^{-1}\hskip-7.37497pt\pmod{4N}\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{F}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d_{1}D_{\mathbf{u}})4^{-{rg}\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\chi_{\mathbf{u},(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{d_{2}}\right)\Bigg|,

where we set
d_{1}=\prod_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{w}}
and
d_{2}=\prod_{\mathbf{w}\textup{ linked to }\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{w}}
,
supressing the dependency on the collection
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\neq\mathbf{u}}
to ease notation.
In the first sum we leave implicit the remaining conditions on the
D_{\mathbf{w}}
(it will suffice
to take them only to be odd and coprime to
N
; relaxing the conditions in this way
only enlarges the sum). Now by the assumption (
4.35
) on
\mathbf{A}
,

d_{2}
is divisible by at least one odd prime
p
which is coprime to
N
and unramified in
L/\mathbb{Q}
.
It follows that for every irreducible character
\chi
of
L(\zeta_{4N})
, the product

\chi(-)\cdot\left(\frac{-}{d_{2}}\right)
corresponds to a non-trivial irreducible
character of
L(\zeta_{4N})(\sqrt{d})
for some
d\ll(\Delta X^{\dagger})^{16^{rg}}
.
Expressing the indicator function of the condition
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv cd_{1}^{-1}\pmod{4N}

as a finite sum of Dirichlet characters modulo
4N
(with complex coefficients bounded
in absolute value), it follows from
Proposition

3.9
that, for
every real
B>0
, we have

|\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\mathbf{A})|\ll_{B}\frac{X}{\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}}\cdot\frac{\left(\log(X)+(X^{\dagger}\right)^{16^{rg}/2})\Delta A_{\mathbf{u}}}{\log(X^{\ddagger})^{B}}\ll X(X^{\dagger})^{16^{rg}/2}\log(X)^{-\epsilon B}.

Since
X^{\dagger}
is a fixed power of
\log(X)
, summing over the
O(\log(X)^{(1+4^{rg})\cdot 16^{rg}})
possibilities for
A
and taking
B
large enough compared to
\epsilon
, we find

(4.36)

\sum_{\textbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:third_family_definition}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\textbf{A})\ll X\log(X)^{-1}.

Fourth family: fewer than
4^{rg}
large variables.

We now consider those
A
that satisfy:

(4.37)

\textup{we have }A_{\mathbf{u}}\geq X^{\ddagger}\textup{ for at most }4^{rg}-1\textup{ indices }\mathbf{u}.

Here the argument proceeds as in
[
8
, Lemma 38]
. We trivially have

(4.38)

\bigg|\sum_{\textbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:4th_family_def}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\textbf{A})\bigg|\leq\sum_{(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})},

where the right hand sum is taken over all collections
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}

of pairwise coprime integers satisfying
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq X
, with each

D_{\mathbf{u}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{L}
, and at most
4^{rg}-1
of the
D_{\mathbf{u}}
larger than or equal
to
X^{\ddagger}
in absolute value. We split this sum up according to the number
t

of those
D_{\mathbf{u}}
that are
\geq X^{\ddagger}
, let
n
be the product of these
‘large’ variables, and let
m
be the product of the remaining ‘small variables’.
This gives

\textup{RHS of }\eqref{eq:few_large_vars_1}\leq\sum_{t=0}^{4^{rg}-1}\sum_{m\leq(X^{\ddagger})^{16^{rg}-t}}\mu^{2}(m)4^{(rg-2t)\omega(m)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq X/m\\
n\in\mathcal{F}\end{subarray}}(t\cdot 4^{-rg})^{\omega(n)}.

Here, similarly to the treatment of the first family, the term
4^{(rg-2t)\omega(m)}
arises as the product
(16^{rg-t})^{\omega(m)}\cdot 4^{-rg\omega(m)}
, the first factor being the number of ways to write a positive coprime integer
m
as an ordered product of
16^{rg-t}
positive integers. The term
(t\cdot 4^{-rg})^{\omega(n)}
arises similarly. Applying
Lemma

3.13
to the innermost sum gives

RHS of
(
4.38
)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{4^{rg}-1}\sum_{m\leq(X^{\ddagger})^{16^{rg}-t}}\mu^{2}(m)4^{(rg-2t)\omega(m)}\cdot(X/m)\log(X)^{t\cdot 4^{-rg}\cdot n_{L}^{-1}-1}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle X\left(\sum_{t=0}^{4^{rg}-1}\log(X)^{t\cdot 4^{-rg}n_{L}^{-1}-1}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{m\leq(X^{\ddagger})^{16^{rg}}}\mu^{2}(m)\frac{4^{rg\omega(m)}}{m}\right)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg}}}\cdot\log(X^{\ddagger})^{4^{rg}}\ll X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg}}+4^{rg}\epsilon}

where for the second last inequality we use Merten’s formula (see e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
). Taking
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, we finally arrive at

(4.39)

\bigg|\sum_{\textbf{A}\textup{ satisfies }\eqref{eq:4th_family_def}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X,\textbf{A})\bigg|\ll X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg+1}}}.

Completion of the proof of
Proposition

4.29
.
Combining (
4.32
),
(
4.34
) (
4.36
), and (
4.39
),
it only remains to treat the
\mathbf{A}
that fall outside each of the
4
families above.
Take
X
sufficiently large so that
X^{\ddagger}>X^{\dagger}
, and consider such an
exceptional
\mathbf{A}
. Since
\mathbf{A}
lies outside the
4
-th family, there are at least

4^{r}
indices
\mathbf{u}
such that
A_{\mathbf{u}}\geq X^{\ddagger}
. Since
\mathbf{A}
lies outside
the
1
st and
2
nd families, the collection of all these
\mathbf{u}
form an
unlinked subset. Thus by
Lemma

4.28
the collection of
such indices
\mathbf{u}
form a maximal unlinked subset of
A[2]^{2}
. Call this
subset
\mathcal{U}
. Finally, since
\mathbf{A}
lies outside the
3
rd family, we see that

A_{\mathbf{v}}=1
for each
\mathbf{v}\notin\mathcal{U}
. But then from the definition of

\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
there can be no element
(D_{\mathbf{w}})_{\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}}
in
\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)

with
A_{\mathbf{w}}\leq D_{\mathbf{w}}\leq\Delta A_{\mathbf{w}}
for each
\mathbf{w}
. This completes the proof of
Proposition

4.29
.

4.6.2.
The main term

We now turn to the sums

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)
where
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
is maximal unlinked. Recall that we have

(4.40)

\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\sum_{(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}2^{-2rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})},

where
\mathcal{D}^{T}(X)
is the subset of elements
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}
of
\mathcal{D}(X)
for which
D_{\mathbf{u}}>1
precisely when
\mathbf{u}\in T
. Recall from
Lemma

4.28
that any maximal unlinked subset
T
of
A[2]^{2}
has the form
\mathbf{c}+T_{0}
where
\mathbf{c}\in A[2]^{2}
and
T_{0}
is a maximal isotropic subspace with respect to the quadratic form
q
of
Definition

4.27
. By
Remark

4.26
, it suffices to understand the sums
\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)
where
T
is a maximal isotropic subspace. Recall also from the beginning of the section that the condition that all prime factors of elements of
\mathcal{F}
split completely in
L/\mathbb{Q}
forces each element of
\mathcal{F}
to reduce
modulo
4N
into a specific subgroup
S\leq(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, and that we have chosen
c\in S
.

Notation 4.41
.

Given
h\in S
, we define
\alpha(h)\in\{0,1\}
so that
h\equiv(-1)^{\alpha(h)}\pmod{4}
.
Note that both
\alpha
and all of the characters
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}
,
\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}
, factor through
\tfrac{(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}{(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times 2}}
. Consequently, we can unambiguously evaluate them on elements of
\mathbb{S}
.

For
W\subseteq A[2]^{2}
maximal isotropic, define

(4.42)

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)=\frac{1}{\#\mathbb{S}^{4^{rg}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{S}^{W}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(h_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}}(-1)^{\alpha(h_{\mathbf{u}})\alpha(h_{\mathbf{v}})\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}.

Proposition 4.43
.

Let
W\subseteq A[2]^{2}
be a maximal isotropic subspace. Then we have

\mathcal{S}^{W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\frac{\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)}{\#S}\cdot\#\mathcal{F}(X)+O\left(X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg+1}}}\right).

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\subseteq W}(X)
the subset of elements
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in A[2]^{2}}
of
\mathcal{D}(X)
for which
D_{\mathbf{u}}=1
for all
\mathbf{u}\notin W
.
Further, write
\mathcal{S}^{\subseteq W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\sum_{T\subseteq W}\mathcal{S}_{v_{0},z_{0}}^{T}(X)
, and note that we have

\mathcal{S}^{\subseteq W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\sum_{(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\subseteq W}(X)}\prod_{\mathbf{u}}2^{-2rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}.

It suffices to prove the result for
\mathcal{S}^{\subseteq W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)
in place of
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)
, since by
Proposition

4.29
these two quantities agree up to an acceptable error.

Projecting onto
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in W}
,
we identify
\mathcal{D}^{\subseteq W}(X)
with the set of tuples
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in W}

of pairwise coprime elements of
\mathcal{F}
such that
\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq X

and
\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv c\pmod{4N}
. Since
W
is unlinked, given

\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}
in
W
we have
\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\Phi(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u})
. In particular,
by quadratic reciprocity for Jacobi symbols we have

\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}}{D_{\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}\left(\frac{D_{\mathbf{v}}}{D_{\mathbf{u}}}\right)^{\Phi(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u})}=(-1)^{\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\cdot\frac{D_{\mathbf{u}}-1}{2}\cdot\frac{D_{\mathbf{v}}-1}{2}}.

Conditioning on the value of each
D_{\mathbf{u}}

modulo
4N
, and recalling that each
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}
is a Dirichlet character modulo
4N
, we find

(4.44)

\mathcal{S}^{\subseteq W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in S^{W}\\
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}h_{\mathbf{u}}=c\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(h_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}}(-1)^{\alpha(h_{\mathbf{u}})\alpha(h_{\mathbf{v}})\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{W}(X)\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv h_{\mathbf{u}}\hskip-6.5556pt\pmod{4N}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})},

where the second product is taken over all unordered pairs
\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}
of (distinct) elements of
W
.

Lemma

4.46
below, whose
proof is similar to the treatment of the
3
rd family in the proof of
Proposition

4.29
,
gives asymptotics for the inner sum in (
4.44
). Specifically it shows that, up to an acceptable error, its value is
\#\mathcal{F}(X)\cdot|S|^{-4^{rg}}
. Thus, again up to an acceptable error, we have

(4.45)

\mathcal{S}^{\subseteq W}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)=\frac{\#\mathcal{F}(X)}{\#S^{4^{rg}}}\cdot\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in S^{W}\\
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}h_{\mathbf{u}}=c\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(h_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}}(-1)^{\alpha(h_{\mathbf{u}})\alpha(h_{\mathbf{v}})\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}.

Now, consulting
4.20
we see that, when
\mathbf{u}=0
, the character
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{u}}^{(v_{0},z_{0})}
is trivial. Further, if either
\mathbf{u}
or
\mathbf{v}
is zero, then
\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=0
. In particular, given any tuple
(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in S^{W}
, we can change the value at
\mathbf{u}=0
by an arbitrary element of
S
, without changing the value of

\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(h_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}}(-1)^{\alpha(h_{\mathbf{u}})\alpha(h_{\mathbf{v}})\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}.

Consequently, the sum on the right hand side of (
4.45
) is indepenent of the choice of
c\in S
. In particular, we may remove the constraint
\prod_{\mathbf{u}}h_{\mathbf{u}}=c
at the cost of dividing by
|S|
. Finally, since
\alpha
, and the functions
\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\mathbf{u}\in U
, depend only on
h\in S
via the image of
h
in
\mathbb{S}
, we can replace
S
by
\mathbb{S}
in the expression

\frac{1}{\#S^{4^{rg}}}\sum_{(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in S^{W}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\Upsilon^{(v_{0},z_{0})}_{\mathbf{u}}(h_{\mathbf{u}})\cdot\prod_{\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}}(-1)^{\alpha(h_{\mathbf{u}})\alpha(h_{\mathbf{v}})\Phi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}

without changing its value.
∎

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
W\subseteq A[2]^{2}
be a maximal isotropic subspace. Then
for all elements
(h_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\in S^{W}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{D}^{W}(X)\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv h_{\mathbf{u}}\hskip-6.5556pt\pmod{4N}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}=\frac{\#\mathcal{F}(X)}{\#S^{4^{rg}}}+O\left(X\log(X)^{\frac{1}{n_{L}}-1-\frac{1}{n_{L}\cdot 4^{rg+1}}}\right).

Proof.

Recall that
S
is the image of
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/F)
in
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, where
F=L\cap\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})
.
Let
\chi_{1},...,\chi_{n}
be the irreducible characters of
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
,
chosen so that the first
k=\frac{\varphi(4N)}{|S|}
characters are precisely
those that lift from the quotient
(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}/S
. Here
\varphi
is
Euler’s phi function. For any
h_{\mathbf{u}}\in S
we can write the indicator
function of
h_{\mathbf{u}}
as the sum

\mathbbm{1}_{h_{\mathbf{u}}}=\frac{1}{\varphi(4N)}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\chi_{i}+\frac{1}{\varphi(4N)}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\chi_{i}(h_{\mathbf{u}}^{-1})\chi_{i}.

Since each
p\in\mathcal{P}_{L}
splits completely in
L
(so in particular in
F
),
we have, for every
D_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}
and for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, that

\chi_{i}(D_{\mathbf{u}})=1
. Thus we have

(4.47)

\mathbbm{1}_{h_{\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})=\frac{1}{\#S}+\frac{1}{\varphi(4N)}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\chi_{i}(h_{\mathbf{u}}^{-1})\chi_{i}(D_{\mathbf{u}}).

In the sum in the statement of the lemma, we drop the conditions
D_{\mathbf{u}}\equiv h_{\mathbf{u}}\pmod{4N}

at the expense of multiplying the summand by
\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}\mathbbm{1}_{h_{\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})
.
Expanding this product using (
4.47
) leaves the sum

\frac{1}{\#S^{4^{rg}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in D_{\mathcal{P}}^{W}(X)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in W}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}=\frac{1}{\#S^{4^{rg}}}\cdot\#\mathcal{F}_{L}(X),

along with
O(1)
-many sums of the shape

(4.48)

\textup{constant}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}\in D^{W}(X)\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{u\in W}4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\prod_{\mathbf{u}\in U}\chi_{i_{\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\bigg)

for some non-empty subsets
U\subseteq W
and indices
k+1\leq i_{\mathbf{u}}\leq n
.
The argument used to treat the
4
th family in the proof of
Proposition

4.29

shows that, at the expense of an acceptable error term, in
(
4.48
) we may restrict the range of summation
to elements
(D_{\mathbf{u}})_{\mathbf{u}}
such that for each
\mathbf{u}
we have

D_{\mathbf{u}}\geq X^{\ddagger}=\exp(\log(X)^{\epsilon})
for some sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\mathbf{u}
be an index lying in
U
. Then we have

(4.49)

\left|\eqref{eq:the_error_term_sums_non_triv_chi}\right|\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(D_{\mathbf{v}})_{\mathbf{v}\in W\setminus\{\mathbf{u}\}}\\
\prod_{\mathbf{v}}D_{\mathbf{v}}\leq X\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(m)4^{-rg\omega(m)}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{\mathbf{u}}\leq X/m\\
D_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{F}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(mD_{\mathbf{u}})4^{-rg\omega(D_{\mathbf{u}})}\chi_{i_{\mathbf{u}}}(D_{\mathbf{u}})\bigg|,

where we have set
m=\prod_{\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{u}}D_{\mathbf{v}}
. Now from the
definition of
S
as the subgroup of
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q})

consisting of elements that act trivially on
L\cap\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{4N})
, we see that
for each
k+1\leq i\leq n
and every irreducible character
\chi
of
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
,
the product
\chi_{i}\chi
is a non-trivial irreducible character of

\Gal(L(\zeta_{4N})/\mathbb{Q}).
Thus applying
Equation

3.10
,
for all
B>0
we have

RHS of (
4.49
)

\displaystyle\ll_{B}

\displaystyle\frac{X\log(X)}{\log(X^{\ddagger})^{B}}\sum_{m\leq X}\frac{1}{m}\ll\frac{X\log(X)^{2}}{\log(X)^{\epsilon B}}.

Choosing
B
sufficiently large in terms of
\epsilon
we see that each of the
sums (
4.48
) go into the error term, giving the result.
∎

4.7.
A combinatorial formula for the moments

We begin with a few simplifications of the summands in (
4.42
).

Notation 4.50
.

The natural map
\mathcal{F}\to\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})
sending
m
to
(\textup{res}_{v}\psi_{m})_{v}
factors through an injection
\mathbb{S}\hookrightarrow\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})
. More intrinsically, this is the restriction to
\mathbb{S}
of the natural map
\tfrac{(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}{(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z})^{\times 2}}\cong\prod_{p\mid N}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times 2}\hookrightarrow\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2}),
where the second map takes a tuple to the corresponding tuple of local quadratic characters, along with the trivial character at the real place.
Via this map, we will view
\mathbb{S}
as a subgroup of
\bigoplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
as a subgroup of
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
. Given
u\in A[2]
, and
m\in\mathcal{F}
lifting
h\in\mathbb{S}
, this latter identification takes the class of
h\otimes u
in
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
to the tuple
(\textup{res}_{v}\psi_{m}u)_{v\in\Sigma}
.

Let
W\subseteq A[2]^{2}
be a maximal isotropic subspace for
q
. Define

\eta\colon\mathbb{S}^{W}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}\otimes W

sending
(h_{w})_{w}
to
\sum_{w\in W}h_{w}\otimes w
.
Note that for
i=1,2
, the projection
\pi_{i}:A[2]^{2}\to A[2]
induces a map
\mathbb{S}\otimes W\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
, which we denote
\pi_{i}
also. Via the identification above, we view these maps as being valued in
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
.

The natural map restriction map
V_{0}=H^{1}_{\Sigma}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])\to\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
is injective. In what follows, as with
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
, we will typically identify
V_{0}
with its image in
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
.

Lemma 4.51
.

We have

(4.52)

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}|^{4^{rg}}}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{S}\otimes W}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\pi_{2}(t)\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle\pi_{1}(t),z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{W}\\
\eta((h_{w})_{w})=t\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Xi_{W}((h_{w})_{w})},

where

\Xi_{W}((h_{w})_{w})=\sum_{\{w,w^{\prime}\}\subseteq W}\alpha(h_{w})\alpha(h_{w^{\prime}})e(\pi_{1}(w),\pi_{2}(w^{\prime}))+\sum_{w\in W}e(\pi_{1}(w),\gamma(\sigma_{h_{w}})\pi_{2}(w)).

Proof.

Since
W
is isotropic for
q
, we have
e_{2}(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))=0

for all
\mathbf{u}\in W
.
It now follows straight from the definition of
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}|^{4^{rg}}}\sum_{(h_{w})\in\mathbb{S}^{W}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\sum_{w\in W}h_{w}\pi_{2}(w)\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle\sum_{w\in W}h_{w}\pi_{1}(w),z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\Xi_{W}((h_{w})_{w})}.

The result follows from noting that the first two terms in the exponent depend only on the image of the tuple
(h_{w})_{w}
under
\eta
.
∎

Definition 4.53
.

Define

\mathscr{Y}(W)=\frac{1}{|Z_{0}|}\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W).

Theorem 4.54
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension, let
c\in S\subseteq\left(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\times}
and let
d_{0}
be a (positive or negative)
divisor of
N
. Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\frac{\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}}{\#E[2]^{r}}=\sum_{W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max. iso.}}\mathscr{Y}(W).

Proof.

Combining Propositions
4.25
and
4.29
with Proposition
3.2
(the latter giving asymptotics for
\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)
), gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\cdot\frac{1}{\left|Z_{0}\right|}~\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq A[2]^{2}\\
T\textup{ max. unlinked}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)\hskip 10.00002pt+o(1).

By Lemma
4.28
, the maximal unlinked subsets of
A[2]^{2}
are precisely those of the form
W+\mathbf{c}
, where
\mathbf{c}\in A[2]^{2}
and
W
is a maximal isotropic subspace for the quadratic form
q
. Note that each maximal isotropic subspace for
q
, having size
\#A[2]
, has
\#A[2]
distinct cosets in
A[2]^{2}
. By Remark
4.26
we may thus replace the condition that
T
be maximal unlinked in the above displayed formula with the condition that
T
be maximal isotropic for
q
, at the expense of multiplying the right hand side by
\#A[2]=\#E[2]^{r}
. Now Proposition
4.43
and a further application of Proposition
3.2
gives the result.
∎

Remark 4.55
.

Theorem
4.54
shows that the limit

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}

exists and is finite.

We end this section by giving a useful variant of Theorem
4.54
.
Note that we have an isomorphism

\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}\cong\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})),

the map from right to left sending a homomorphism
\phi
to
(\phi(f_{i}))_{i=1}^{r}
, where
f_{i}
denotes the
i
-th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. In particular, we can replace the quantity
\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d_{0}D}/\mathbb{Q})^{r}
in Proposition
4.25
with
\#\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}))
. Using Lemma
4.15
, we can prove a version of Theorem
4.54
where we instead count (a slight variant of) injective homomorphisms into the Selmer group. The resulting sums arising this way will turn out to be easier to work with. Before giving this result however, we need to define a certain ‘systematic subspace’ of the Selmer group.

4.8.
The systematic subspace

Definition 4.56
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
,
c
and
d_{0}
be as above. Recall that the pair
(d_{0},c)
determines a unique class
b=(b_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
. We define the
systematic subspace

\mathcal{S}_{b}
to be the subgroup of
H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
consisting of elements
that satisfy the Selmer conditions associated to
b_{v}
at all places
v\in\Sigma
. That is, we set

\mathcal{S}_{b}=\ker\Big(H^{1}\big(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2]\big)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},E[2])/\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}\Big).

Remark 4.57
.

We have
\mathcal{S}_{b}\subseteq\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
for every
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
. Indeed, the Selmer conditions at
v\in\Sigma
are satisfied by assumption, while the condition that all elements of
\mathcal{S}_{b}
have trivial restriction to
L
ensures that, for all primes
p\notin\Sigma
with
p\mid d
, we have
\res_{p}(\mathcal{S}_{b})=0
.

Note that any element of
H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
is trivial on restriction to the maximal multi-quadratic subextension of
L/\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, since
\Sigma
contains all primes that ramify in
L
, we see that
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is naturally a subspace of
H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}\cap L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
. Thus,
\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}
is naturally a subspace of
V_{0}=H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},A[2])
.

Recall that, per Notation
4.50
, we view both
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
and
V_{0}
as subgroups of
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
.

Lemma 4.58
.

We have

\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}=V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}\cap Z_{0},

where the orthogonal complement is taken with respect to the local Tate pairing
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}
is the subgroup of
V_{0}\cap Z_{0}
consisting of cocycles which factor through
\textup{Gal}(K_{\Sigma}\cap L/\mathbb{Q})
. Recall that
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
is equal to
H_{\Sigma}\otimes e
, where

H_{\Sigma}\colon\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})\times\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

is the sum of the local ‘Hilbert symbol’ pairings. We thus have

V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}=\big(H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{F}_{2})\cap\mathbb{S}^{\perp}\big)\otimes A[2],

where the orthogonal complement on the left, respectively right is taken with respect to

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
, respectively
H_{\Sigma}
. By definition,

\mathcal{F}
is generated by primes
p\nmid N
that split completely in the Galois extension
L/\mathbb{Q}
.
Given such a prime
p
, and given
\chi\in H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{F}_{2})
, it follows from reciprocity that
H_{\Sigma}(\chi,\psi_{p})=0
if and only if
\chi\big(\sigma_{p})=0
.
By the Chebotarev density theorem, this last condition holds for all
p\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if
\chi
factors through
\Gal(K_{\Sigma}\cap L/\mathbb{Q})
.
∎

The following result shows that
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
is equal to the systematic subspace for
100\%
of
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}
.

Proposition 4.59
.

We have

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)~~\colon~~\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])=\mathcal{S}_{b}\}}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}=1.

Proof.

Examining the explicit parameterisation of Selmer elements given in
Section

4.2
, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Big(\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])\Big)^{r}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\cdot\frac{1}{\#Z_{0}}~\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{T\subseteq 0\times E[2]}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X).

The single maximal unlinked subset of
0\times A[2]
is
0\times A[2]
itself.
Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
4.54
, we thus see that we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Big(\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])\Big)^{r}=\mathscr{Y}(0\times A[2]).

Taking
W=0\times A[2]
in Lemma
4.51
(noting that the map
\eta
is surjective) we see that

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(0\times A[2])=\frac{1}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},t\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}=\mathbbm{1}_{v_{0}\in(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}}.

Since
Z_{0}
is its own orthogonal complement with
respect to the pairing
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Big(\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])\Big)^{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}\cap Z_{0}=\#\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r},

where the final equality follows from Lemma
4.58
.
The result follows from the case
r=1
.
∎

4.9.
Counting injections

In the statement of the following result, for
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces
U
,
W
, we denote by
\Inj(U,W)
the set of injective homomorphisms
U\to W
.

Theorem 4.60
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension, let
c\in S\subseteq\left(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\times}
and let
d_{0}
be a (possibly negative)
divisor of
N
.

Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\#\Inj\bigg(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])\oplus\mathcal{S}_{b}}\bigg)=\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max iso.}\\
W\textup{ satisfies }(\star)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{Y}(W),

where the condition in the sum on the right hand side is:

(
\star
)

\textup{there is no codimension }1\textup{ subspace }N\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\textup{ such that }\pi_{1}(W)\subseteq E[2]\otimes N.

Proof.

For
T\subseteq A[2]^{2}
, we say that
T
satisfies condition
(\star)^{\prime}
if
there does not exist an element
\mathbf{w}\in A[2]^{2}
and a codimension
1
subspace

N\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
such that one has
\pi_{1}(T+\mathbf{w})\subseteq E[2]\otimes N
.
When
T
is a maximal isotropic subspace, this condition specialises to condition
(\star)
in the statement.
Arguing as in Proposition
4.25
and using Lemma
4.15
we see that

(4.61)

\frac{1}{|Z_{0}|}~\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq A[2]^{2}\\
T\textup{ satisfies }(\star)^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{S}^{T}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(X)

is equal to the sum, over
d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)
, of the number of homomorphisms
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\rightarrow\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

that are injective after composing with the quotient map

\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])+\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])}.

Denote by
\lambda_{d}
the number of such homomorphisms.
Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
4.54
, we see that the limit, as
X
tends to infinity, of the quantity
\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)\end{subarray}}\lambda_{d}
is equal to

\#E[2]^{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max iso.}\\
W\textup{ satisfies }(\star)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{Y}(W).

Denote by
\lambda_{d}^{\prime}
the number of homomorphisms
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\rightarrow\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
that are injective after composing with the quotient map

\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])+\mathcal{S}_{b}}.

We claim that we have

(4.62)

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\lambda_{d}=\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\lambda_{d}^{\prime}.

Indeed, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\mid\lambda_{d}-\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\mid

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{d}\neq\lambda_{d}^{\prime}}\cdot\mid\lambda_{d}-\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\mid

\phantom{lotsofspace}\leq\bigg(\frac{\#\{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)~~\colon~~\lambda_{d}\neq\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\}}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\bigg)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\mid\lambda_{d}-\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\mid^{2}\bigg)^{\tfrac{1}{2}}.

Now
\mid\lambda_{d}-\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\mid^{2}
is bounded above by
4\#\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})^{2r}
. It thus follows from Remark
4.55
that the limit, as
X
tends to infinity, of the quantity

\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\sum_{d\in d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c}(X)}\mid\lambda_{d}-\lambda_{d}^{\prime}\mid^{2}

exists and is finite.
We have
\lambda_{d}=\lambda_{d}^{\prime}
whenever
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{1}(K_{\Sigma}/\mathbb{Q},E[2])=\mathcal{S}_{b}
, so that the claim follows from
Proposition

4.59
.

Each injective homomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\to\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\big(\delta_{d}(E[2])+\mathcal{S}_{b}\big)
lifts to

\#\big(\delta_{d}(E[2])+\mathcal{S}_{b}\big)^{r}

homomorphisms
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\rightarrow\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
. By Lemma
2.11
we have
\#\big(\delta_{d}(E[2])+\mathcal{S}_{b}\big)^{r}=\#E[2]^{r}\cdot\#\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}
whenever
d\neq d_{0}
, hence

\lambda_{d}^{\prime}=\#E[2]^{r}\cdot\#\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}\cdot\#\Inj\bigg(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])\oplus\mathcal{S}_{b}}\bigg)

for all but finitely many
d
, so that the result follows from
(
4.62
).
∎

5.
Combinatorics of the leading term

Take all the notation of the previous section. The main purpose of this section
is to prove
Theorem

1.6
. This will be achieved by determining the “moments”
of the distribution obeyed by the
2
-Selmer groups in suitable families. In light
of Theorem
4.60
, the purely combinatorial statement of

Theorem

5.2
below, the proof of which will occupy most of the section, will be the final missing ingredient.

Recall from Definition
2.10
that we have a natural injection
\gamma\colon\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(E[4])/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\End(E[2])
,
which is a homomorphism for the additive structure on
\End(E[2])
. Recall also that we denote by
\Gamma
the image of
\Gal\big(\mathbb{Q}(E[4])/L\cap\mathbb{Q}(E[4])\big)\cong\Gal(L(E[4])/L)
under this map. In the notation of the previous section,
\Gamma
is the additive subgroup of
\End E[2]
generated by
\{\gamma(\sigma_{m})\colon m\in\mathbb{S}\}
. Denote by
\End_{\Gamma}(E[2])
the additive subgroup of endomorphisms of
E[2]
commuting with all elements of
\Gamma
. We will sometimes assume the following condition.

Condition 5.1
.

There are no
\Gamma
-invariant subspaces of
E[2]
other than
0
and
E[2]
. Moreover, we have
\End_{\Gamma}(E[2])=\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Take the notation and assumptions of
Theorem

4.60
. Suppose further that Condition
5.1
is satiafied. Then we have

(5.3)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max iso.}\\
W\textup{ satisfies }(\star)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{Y}(W)=\#\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}\cdot 2^{\frac{r(r+1)}{2}}.

The theorem will be proved in Section
5.5
.

5.1.
Description of maximal isotropic subspaces

We begin by explicitly describing the maximal isotropic subspaces of
A[2]^{2}
.
Recall from Definition
4.27
the quadratic form
q\colon A[2]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

sending
\mathbf{u}
to
e(\pi_{1}(\mathbf{u}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{u}))
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(U,\phi)
be a pair consisting of a subspace
U
of
A[2]
, and a homomorphism

\phi\colon U\rightarrow A[2]/U^{\perp}
satisfying
e(u,\phi(u))=0
for all
u\in U
.
Here
U^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
U
with respect to the Weil pairing
on
A[2]
. Define the subspace
W_{U,\phi}
of
A[2]^{2}
as

W_{U,\phi}=\big\{(u,\phi(u)+u^{\prime})~~\colon~~u\in U,u^{\prime}\in U^{\perp}\big\}.

By construction,
W_{U,\phi}
is isotropic for
q
. Since we also have

\dim W_{U,\phi}=\dim U+\dim U^{\perp}=\dim A[2],

we conclude that
W_{U,\phi}
is a maximal isotropic subspace for
q
.
Note that the subspaces
W_{U,\phi}
are distinct as
U
and
\phi
vary.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
W\subseteq A[2]^{2}
be a maximal isotropic subspace for
q
. Then
W
has the form
W_{U,\phi}
for some
U,\phi

as in
Definition

5.4
.

Proof.

Define
U=\pi_{1}(W)\subseteq A[2]
, and define

K=\pi_{2}\left(\ker(\pi_{1}\colon W\rightarrow A[2])\right)\subseteq A[2].

We have a short exact sequence

(5.6)

0\longrightarrow K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v\mapsto(0,v)}}{{\longrightarrow}}W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U\longrightarrow 0.

We claim that
K=U^{\perp}
. Indeed, as in Lemma
4.28
, since
W
is maximal isotropic, we have

\dim W=\dim A[2].

Since we also have
\dim A[2]=\dim U+\dim U^{\perp}
, it suffices to show
that
K\subseteq U^{\perp}
. Let
u\in U
and let
(u,\lambda)
be any element of
W
mapping to
u
under
\pi_{1}
. Since
W
is isotropic for
q
, we have
e(u,\lambda)=0
. Now given any
k\in K
, the element
(u,\lambda+k)
is in
W
, so we have

0=e(u,\lambda+k)=e(u,\lambda)+e(u,k)=e(u,k).

Thus
e(u,k)=0
. Since
u\in U
and
k\in K
were arbitrary, we conclude that
K
is contained in
U^{\perp}
, and the claim is proved.

In light of the claim, we have a well-defined homomorphism
\phi\colon U=\pi_{1}(W)\rightarrow V/U^{\perp}
given by lifting elements of
U
to
W
under
\pi_{1}
, and then applying
\pi_{2}
. Moreover, we have

W=\big\{(u,\phi(u)+U^{\perp})~~\colon~~u\in U\big\}.

The condition that
W
be isotropic for
q
forces
e(u,\phi(u))=0
for all
u\in U
.
∎

Definition 5.7
.

For a given
U\subseteq V
, define

\mathscr{A}_{U}=\big\{\phi\in\Hom(U,A[2]/U^{\perp})~~\colon~~e(u,\phi(u))=0\textup{ for all }u\in U\big\}.

Remark 5.8
.

Since the pairing

U\otimes A[2]/U^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

induced by
e
is non-degenerate, the natural map
A[2]/U^{\perp}\rightarrow U^{*}

sending
x
to
e(-,x)
is an isomorphism. From this we see that the map sending

\phi\in\mathscr{A}_{U}
to the pairing
P_{\phi}\colon U\otimes U\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
, defined by

P_{\phi}(u,w)=e(u,\phi(w))
,
identifies
\mathscr{A}_{U}
with the vector space of alternating pairings on
U
.
In particular, we have
\dim\mathscr{A}_{U}=\tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)
, where
n=\dim U
.

5.2.
Reduction to
\Gamma
-invariant subspaces

We now turn to understanding the left hand side of (
5.3
), i.e. the sum

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max iso.}\\
W\textup{ satisfies }(\star)\end{subarray}}\mathscr{Y}(W)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}W\subseteq A[2]^{2}\textup{ max iso.}\\
W\textup{ satisfies }(\star)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|Z_{0}|}\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W),

where condition
(\star)
is as in the statement of
Theorem

4.60
.
The group
\Gamma\subseteq\End(E[2])
operates diagonally on
A[2]=E[2]^{r}
.
The key observation making the analysis of the sums
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)
manageable is that
for
U
,
\phi
as in
Definition

5.4
we have
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W_{U,\phi})=0

unless
U
is invariant under
\Gamma
.
We will prove a refined version of this that additionally gives information
about the sums
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W_{U,\phi})
when
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. Our starting point will be the expression for the sums
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)
given in the statement of Lemma
4.51
, see also Notation
4.50
. We refer to that lemma for the definition of the function
\Xi_{W}
appearing in the statement of Lemma
5.11
below.

Finally, we introduce the following additional notation.

Notation 5.9
.

For a subspace
U
of
A[2]
,
write
T_{1}=\mathbb{S}\otimes U
and
T_{2}=\mathbb{S}\otimes U^{\perp}
.
Define
\eta_{1}\colon\mathbb{S}^{U}\rightarrow T_{1}
and
\eta_{2}\colon\mathbb{S}^{U^{\perp}}\rightarrow T_{2}
by

\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{u\in U}x_{u}\otimes u\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\eta_{2}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\lambda\in U^{\perp}}y_{\lambda}\otimes\lambda.

For
\phi\in\mathscr{A}_{U}
, we have an isomorphism

(5.10)

U\oplus U^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}W_{U,\phi}

sending
(u,\lambda)
to
(u,\phi(u)+\lambda)
. Via this isomorphism we may write
an element
(h_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{W}
as a tuple
\mathbf{h}=(h_{u,\lambda})_{u\in U,\lambda\in U^{\perp}}
.
The isomorphism (
5.10
) induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{S}\otimes W_{U,\phi}\cong T_{1}\times T_{2}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
U\subseteq A[2]
,
\phi\in\mathscr{A}_{U}
, and
W=W_{U,\phi}
.
Let
t\in\mathbb{S}\otimes W
have image
(t_{1},t_{2})\in T_{1}\times T_{2}
.
Denote by

(5.12)

I_{t}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{W}\\
\eta((h_{w})_{w})=t\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Xi_{W}((h_{w})_{w})}

the innermost sum in (
4.52
).
Then we have
I_{t}=0
unless
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. When
\Gamma U\subseteq U
, we have

I_{t}=\frac{\#\mathbb{S}^{2^{2r}}}{\#\mathbb{S}^{\#U}\cdot\#T_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})},

where

(5.13)

\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq U}\alpha(x_{u})\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})e\big(u,\phi(u^{\prime})\big)+\sum_{u\in U}e\big(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{x_{u}})u,\phi(u)\big).

Proof.

Temporarily define projections
p_{1}\colon\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{U}
and

p_{2}\colon\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{U^{\perp}}
by setting

p_{1}(\mathbf{h})=(\textstyle\sum_{\lambda}h_{u,\lambda})_{u}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptp_{2}(\mathbf{h})=(\textstyle\sum_{u}h_{u,\lambda})_{\lambda}.

Further, write
\widetilde{\eta}
for the map
\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\rightarrow T_{1}\times T_{2}
defined by

\widetilde{\eta}(\mathbf{h})=\big(\eta_{1}\circ p_{1}(\mathbf{h}),\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})\big).

Under the identifications
\mathbb{S}\otimes W\cong T_{1}\times T_{2}
and

\mathbb{S}^{W}\cong\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
above,
the map
\widetilde{\eta}
identifies with the map

\eta\colon\mathbb{S}^{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\otimes W
defined previously

Writing an element
(h_{w})_{w}\in\mathbb{S}^{W}
as a tuple
\mathbf{h}=(h_{u,\lambda})_{u\in U,\lambda\in U^{\perp}}

using the isomorphism (
5.10
), we have

\Xi_{W}(\mathbf{h})=\sum_{\{(u,\lambda),(u^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})\}\subseteq U\oplus U^{\perp}}\alpha(h_{u,\lambda})\alpha(h_{u^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}})e(u,\phi(u^{\prime})+\lambda^{\prime})+\sum_{u\in U,\lambda\in U^{\perp}}e(u,\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})(\phi(u)+\lambda)).

In the first sum, we have
e(u,\lambda^{\prime})=0
since
u\in U
and
\lambda^{\prime}\in U^{\perp}
.
Further, this sum is over unordered distinct pairs
(u,\lambda)
and
(u^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
of
U\oplus U^{\perp}
.
If
u=u^{\prime}
then
e(u,\phi(u^{\prime}))=0
, so we can assume that
u\neq u^{\prime}
. Thus in the first
sum above, we can sum instead over unordered distinct pairs
u,u^{\prime}
of
U
, and then
sum freely over pairs
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
of
U^{\perp}
.
For the second sum, using
Lemma

2.12
we see that, for
u\in U
and
\lambda\in U^{\perp}
, we have

e(u,\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})(\phi(u)+\lambda))=e(\gamma^{(r)}(h_{u,\lambda})u,\phi(u))+e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})u,\lambda).

For
u\in U
, set
x_{u}(\mathbf{h})=\sum_{\lambda\in U^{\perp}}h_{u,\lambda}
. From the above discussion, we conclude that

(5.14)

\Xi_{W}(\mathbf{h})=\Upsilon_{\phi}((x_{u}(\mathbf{h}))_{u})+\sum_{u\in U,\lambda\in U^{\perp}}e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})u,\lambda).

Substituting the expression (
5.14
) for
\Xi_{W}(\mathbf{h})
into (
5.12
) we have

(5.15)

I_{t}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\\
\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})=t_{2}\\
p_{1}(\mathbf{h})=\mathbf{x}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sum_{(u,\lambda)\in U\oplus U^{\perp}}e(\gamma^{(r)}(h_{u,\lambda})u,\lambda)}.

The function
\Theta\colon\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
defined by

\Theta\colon\mathbf{h}\longmapsto(-1)^{\sum_{(u,\lambda)\in U\oplus U^{\perp}}e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})u,\lambda)}

is a homomorphism.
Let us temporarily denote by
B
the subgroup of
\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}

consisting of elements
\mathbf{h}
for which
\eta_{2}(\mathbf{h})=0
and
p_{1}(\mathbf{h})=0
.
Then the innermost sum

(5.16)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\\
\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})=t_{2}\\
p_{1}(\mathbf{h})=\mathbf{x}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sum_{(u,\lambda)\in U\oplus U^{\perp}}e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})u,\lambda)}

in the above expression for
I_{t}
is the sum of the values of
\Theta
over a coset of
B
.
Consequently, this sum is
0
unless
\Theta
is identically
1
on
B
, that is, unless the quantity

(5.17)

\sum_{(u,\lambda)\in U\oplus U^{\perp}}e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h_{u,\lambda}})u,\lambda)

vanishes identically on
B
. We will show that this happens precisely when
U
is invariant under
\Gamma
.
This will show that
I_{t}
is zero unless
\Gamma U\subseteq U
.

Given
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
, we have

\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})=\sum_{\lambda\in U^{\perp}}\big(\textstyle{\sum_{u\in U}}h_{u,\lambda}\big)\otimes\lambda\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptp_{1}(\mathbf{h})=\big(\sum_{\lambda\in U^{\perp}}h_{u,\lambda}\big)_{u\in U}.

We claim that the condition that (
5.17
) vanish on all elements of
B

is equivalent to the condition that it vanish on all elements of
\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
.
Indeed, given an arbitrary element
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
,
we can ensure that
\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})=0=p_{1}(\mathbf{h})
by modifying only the values of

h_{0,\lambda}
and
h_{u,0}
for varying
u\in U
and
\lambda\in U^{\perp}
;
such a modification does not change the value of (
5.17
). Now pick
u\in U
,

\lambda\in U^{\perp}
, and
h\in\mathbb{S}
. Take
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
to be equal to
h
in the
(u,\lambda)
-slot, and equal to
0
everywhere else. The condition that (
5.17
) vanish on
\mathbf{h}
is the condition that

e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})u,\lambda)=0.

Since
h
and
\lambda
were chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
\sigma u\in(U^{\perp})^{\perp}=U
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma
. Since
u\in U
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. Conversely, if
\Gamma U\subseteq U
then the right hand side of (
5.17
) visibly vanishes for all
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
.

Henceforth, suppose that
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. Then (
5.16
) is equal to

N_{t_{2},\mathbf{x}}:=\#\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}~~\colon~~\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h})=t_{2},~~p_{1}(\mathbf{h})=\mathbf{x}\}.

The map
\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}\rightarrow T_{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{U}
sending
\mathbf{h}
to
(\eta_{2}\circ p_{2}(\mathbf{h}),p_{1}(\mathbf{h}))
is readily seen to be surjective (even when restricted to the subset of elements
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{S}^{U\oplus U^{\perp}}
for which
\mathbf{h}_{u,\lambda}=0
whenever both
u
and
\lambda
are non-zero). From this it follows that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}
and
t_{2}\in T_{2}
, we have

N_{t_{2},\mathbf{x}}=\frac{\#\mathbb{S}^{2^{2r}}}{\#\mathbb{S}^{\#U}\cdot\#T_{2}}.

Substituting this into (
5.15
) gives the result.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
U\subseteq A[2]
and
\phi\in\mathscr{A}_{U}
. Then
we have
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W_{U,\phi})=0
unless
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. Suppose that
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. Then we have

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W_{U,\phi})=\mathbbm{1}_{v_{0}\in(\mathbb{S}\otimes U^{\perp})^{\perp}}\cdot\frac{1}{\#\mathbb{S}^{\#U}}\sum_{t_{1}\in T_{1}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})},

where
\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})
is defined as in
Lemma

5.11
.

Proof.

Write
W=W_{U,\phi}
.
Fix a linear map
s\colon A[2]/U^{\perp}\longrightarrow A[2]

giving a section to the quotient map
A[2]\rightarrow A[2]/U^{\perp}
. By an abuse of notation,
we denote the composition
s\circ\phi
by
\phi
also, and thus view
\phi
as a linear map
U\rightarrow A[2]
.
We then define the function
\Upsilon_{\phi}\colon\mathbb{S}^{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}

by the expression in (
5.13
), that is, with the help of the section
s
,
we extend the definition in the statement to the case when
U
is not necessarily
\Gamma
-invariant.

An immediate consequence of Lemma
5.11
and (
4.52
) is that one has

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)=0
unless
\Gamma U\subseteq U
. When
\Gamma U\subseteq U
, substituting the
expression for
I_{t}
afforded by Lemma
5.11
into (
4.52
) we see that

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathbb{S}^{\#U}\cdot\#T_{2}}\sum_{(t_{1},t_{2})\in T_{1}\times T_{2}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})+t_{2}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathbb{S}^{\#U}}\sum_{t_{1}\in T_{1}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})}\frac{1}{\#T_{2}}\sum_{t_{2}\in T_{2}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},t_{2}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}.

The innermost sum is equal to
\#T_{2}
if
v_{0}
is orthogonal to
T_{2}=\mathbb{S}\otimes U^{\perp}
, and is equal to
0
otherwise. This completes the proof.
∎

5.3.
Description of
\Gamma
-invariant subspaces in the case of interest

Lemma 5.19
.

Suppose that Condition
5.1
is satisfied.
Then every
\Gamma
-invariant
subspace of
A[2]=E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
has the form
E[2]\otimes T
for some subspace
T\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be the subring of
\End(E[2])
generated by
\Gamma
, and let
M
be a
\Gamma
-invariant
subspace of
E[2]^{r}
. Thus
M
is an
R
-submodule of
E[2]^{r}
. Consider the map

\Theta\colon\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\mathbb{F}_{2})\rightarrow\Hom_{R}(M,E[2])
