sending
\phi
to the restriction of
1\otimes\phi
to
M
. Let
N
be the kernel of
\Theta
, so that we have an induced injection

(5.20)

\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\mathbb{F}_{2})/N\hookrightarrow\Hom_{R}(M,E[2]).

Let
s=\dim N
. The collection of subspaces of
E[2]^{r}
of the form
E[2]\otimes T

is stable under the action of
\GL_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
on the second factor of
E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.
Consequently, acting by an element of
\GL_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
if necessary, we can assume that
N

is the span of the first
s
coordinate projections, hence we have

M\subseteq 0\times E[2]^{r-s}=E[2]\otimes(0\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r-s}).

We will show that this containment is in fact an equality. By assumption,

E[2]
is a simple
R
-module. In particular, the
R
-submodule
M
of
E[2]^{r}
is semisimple,
and every simple
R
-submodule of
M
is isomorphic to
E[2]
. Thus we have
M\cong E[2]^{t}
for some
integer
t\geq 0
. Counting dimensions, it suffices to show that
t\geq r-s
. Since
we assumed that
\End_{R}(E[2])=\mathbb{F}_{2}
, we have

\dim\Hom_{R}(M,E[2])=\dim\Hom_{R}(E[2]^{t},E[2])=\dim\End_{R}(E[2])^{t}=t.

Taking dimensions in (
5.20
) gives the desired inequality.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

In the notation of the proof of
Lemma

5.19
, before acting by
\GL_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
,
let
N^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
be the annihilator of
N\subseteq\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
Then the proof of
Lemma

5.19
shows that we have
M=E[2]\otimes N^{\circ}
.

5.4.
Combinatorics of the main term

When Condition
5.1
is satisfied, it follows from
Lemma

5.19
and
Proposition

5.18
that
the only maximal isotropic subspaces
W=W_{U,\phi}
that give a non-zero contribution
to (
5.3
) are those for which we have
U=A[2]
. Motivated by this, we restrict attention to these subspaces (but do not presently assume Condition
5.1
).

By
Lemma

5.5
, the set of such subspaces is in bijection with the set

\mathscr{A}:=\mathscr{A}_{A[2]}=\{\phi\in\End(A[2])~~\colon~~e_{2}(u,\phi(u))=0\textup{ for all }u\in A[2]\}.

For
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
and
(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}
, write
\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(\phi)=\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(W)
, where
W=W_{A[2],\phi}
. By
Proposition

5.18
we have

(5.22)

\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}|^{2^{2k}}}\sum_{t\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t)\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\left\langle t,z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{U}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})}.

The next step is to sum this expression over all
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
. The following lemma establishes the key property of
\Upsilon_{\phi}
we will need in order to do this. It is the analogue of
[
13
, Lemma 13]
. For the statement, we denote by
L_{\phi}\colon T_{1}\otimes T_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
the bilinear pairing defined by

L_{\phi}(t,t^{\prime})=e\big((\alpha\otimes 1)(t),(\alpha\otimes\phi)(t^{\prime})\big).

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
, we have

\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=L(\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\eta_{1}(\mathbf{y})).

In particular, the function
\Upsilon_{\phi}\colon\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a quadratic form whose associated bilinear pairing factors through
\eta_{1}
.

Proof.

By definition, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
, we have

\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq A[2]}e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)+\sum_{u\in A[2]}e\big(u,\gamma(\sigma_{x_{u}})\phi(u)\big).

The second sum is linear in
\mathbf{x}
, so we may ignore it for the purposes of the lemma. The first sum is bilinear in
\mathbf{x}
, from which we deduce that

\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq A[2]}\Big(e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(y_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)+e\big(\alpha(y_{u})u,\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)\Big).

It will be convenient to temporarily fix a linear ordering
<
on
A[2]
. Having done this, the above expression is equal to

(5.24)

\sum_{u}\sum_{w>u}e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(y_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)+\sum_{u}\sum_{w>u}e\big(\alpha(y_{u})u,\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big).

Since
e(u,\phi(u))=0
for all
u\in A[2]
, the pairing
(u,u^{\prime})\mapsto e(u,\phi(u^{\prime}))
is alternating, and in particular symmetric. Thus the second of the two sums above is equal to

\sum_{u}\sum_{u^{\prime}>u}e\big(\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})u^{\prime},\alpha(y_{u})\phi(u)\big)=\sum_{u}\sum_{u^{\prime}<u}e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(y_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)

where the equality follows from relabelling
u\leftrightarrow u^{\prime}
and swapping the order of summation. Inserting this into (
5.24
) and again using that
e(u,\phi(u))=0
for all
u
, we have

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{u}\sum_{u^{\prime}}e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(y_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big),

which is equal to
L(\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\eta_{1}(\mathbf{y}))
as desired.
∎

Definition 5.25
.

Define

\Psi\colon\ker(\eta_{1})\rightarrow\Hom(\mathscr{A},\mathbb{F}_{2})

sending
\mathbf{x}
to the map
\phi\mapsto\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})
. Note that
\Psi
is
a homomorphism, since by
Lemma

5.23
, for every

\phi\in\mathscr{A}
, the restriction of
\Upsilon_{\phi}
to
\ker(\eta_{1})
is a homomorphism.
Let
\im(\Psi)^{\circ}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
denote the annihilator of
\im(\Psi)
.

Lemma 5.26
.

For each
t_{1}\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
, fix an
\mathbf{x}(t_{1})\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}

with
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}
. Given
t_{1}\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
,

\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
, and
v_{0}\in V_{0}
, let

\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x},v_{0}}\colon\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be given by
\phi\mapsto\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
.
Then the left hand side of (
5.3
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}\sum_{v_{0}\in V_{0}}\sum_{t_{1}\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}\mathbbm{1}_{v_{0}+t_{1}\in Z_{0}}.

Proof.

Summing (
5.22
) over
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
gives

(5.27)

\sum_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\#\mathbb{S}^{2^{2k}}}\sum_{t_{1}\in T_{1}}(-1)^{\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}.

First we fix
t_{1}\in T_{1}
, and consider the sum

(5.28)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}.

For fixed
\mathbf{x}
, the map
\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
given by

\phi\mapsto\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}

is a homomorphism. Consequently, the expression in (
5.28
) is equal to

\#\mathscr{A}\cdot\#\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}~~\colon~~\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\textup{ and }\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}\textup{ for all }\phi\in\mathscr{A}\}.

By
Lemma

5.23
, the above expression is equal to

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{A}\cdot\#\{\mathbf{x}\in\ker(\eta_{1})~~\colon~~\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x}(t_{1}))+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}\textup{ for all }\phi\in\mathscr{A}\}

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\#\mathscr{A}\cdot\#\{\mathbf{x}\in\ker(\eta_{1})~~\colon~~\Psi(\mathbf{x})(\phi)=\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\Psi(\mathbf{x}(t_{1}))(\phi)\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\phi\in\mathscr{A}\}.

Since
\Psi
is a homomorphism, and so is the map
\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\colon\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
it follows that the quantity (
5.4
) is equal to

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{A}\cdot\#\{\mathbf{x}\in\ker(\eta_{1})~~\colon~~\Psi(\mathbf{x})=\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#\mathscr{A}\cdot\#\ker(\Psi)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}.

Now, we have
\#\ker(\Psi)=\#\ker(\eta_{1})/\#\im(\Psi)
and
\#\im(\Psi)=\#\mathscr{A}/\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}
.
Combining the above, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}\\
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=t_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}(-1)^{\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#\ker(\eta_{1})\cdot\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\#\mathbb{S}^{2^{2k}}\cdot\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}}{\#T_{1}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}.

Substituting into (
5.27
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{Y}_{v_{0},z_{0}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}\cdot\frac{1}{\#T_{1}}\sum_{t_{1}\in T_{1}}(-1)^{\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}.

Substituting this into (
4.53
), we see that the left hand side of (
5.3
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}}{\#Z_{0}}\sum_{(v_{0},z_{0})\in V_{0}\times Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\cdot\frac{1}{\#T_{1}}\sum_{t_{1}\in T_{1}}(-1)^{\left\langle t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}\sum_{v_{0}\in V_{0}}\sum_{t_{1}\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}\frac{1}{\#Z_{0}}\sum_{z_{0}\in Z_{0}}(-1)^{\left\langle v_{0}+t_{1},z_{0}\right\rangle_{\Sigma}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\#\im(\Psi)^{\circ}}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}\sum_{v_{0}\in V_{0}}\sum_{t_{1}\in\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]}\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x}(t_{1}),v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)}\mathbbm{1}_{v_{0}+t_{1}\in Z_{0}},\phantom{lotsofspacea}

where for the last equality we are using that
Z_{0}
is its own orthogonal complement
with respect to the pairing
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 5.30
.

For all
\phi\in\im(\Psi)^{\circ}
and
\sigma\in\Gamma
, we have
\phi\sigma=\sigma\phi
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in\im(\Psi)^{\circ}
. By definition, we have

\im(\Psi)^{\circ}=\{\phi\in\mathscr{A}~~\colon~~\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=0~~\textup{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\ker(\eta_{1})\}.

Recall that
\ker(\eta_{1})
is the set of elements
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
for which
\sum_{u\in A[2]}x_{u}\otimes u=0
.

Fix
h\in\mathbb{S}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in A[2]
. Suppose that
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{1}+u_{2}\}
has size
3
. Define
\mathbf{x}=(x_{u})_{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
by taking
x_{u_{1}}=x_{u_{2}}=x_{u_{1}+u_{2}}=h,
and setting
x_{u}=0
otherwise.
Note that
\mathbf{x}
is in
\ker(\eta_{1})
. For
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
we have

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq A[2]}e\big(\alpha(x_{u})u,\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})\phi(u^{\prime})\big)+\sum_{u\in A[2]}e\big(\gamma(\sigma_{x_{u}})u,\phi(u)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e\big(\alpha(h)u_{2},\alpha(h)\phi(u_{1})\big)+e\big(u_{1},\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})\phi(u_{2})\big)+e\big(u_{2},\gamma^{(r)}(h)\phi(u_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle e\big(u_{1},(\phi\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})-\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})\phi)u_{2}\big),

where the final equality follows from
Lemma

2.12
and the fact that
the pairing
(u,u^{\prime})\mapsto e(u,\phi(u^{\prime}))
is symmetric. Since we have
\phi\in\im(\Psi)^{\circ}
, we deduce that

(5.31)

e(u_{1},(\phi\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})-\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{h})\phi)u_{2})=0.

This equality also holds, for any
h
, when
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{1}+u_{2}\}
has size smaller than
3
. Indeed, if one of
u_{1},u_{2}
is zero then it is trivial, while the case
u_{1}=u_{2}
again follows by combining
Lemma

2.12
with the fact that
the pairing
(u,u^{\prime})\mapsto e(u,\phi(u^{\prime}))
is symmetric. Thus (
5.31
) holds for all

u_{1},u_{2}\in A[2]
and
h\in\mathbb{S}
, giving the result.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.2

Recall that
A[2]=E[2]^{r}=E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
and that, under this identification,
\sigma\in\Gamma
operates on
A[2]
as
\sigma\otimes 1
; this is our convention for viewing
\Gamma
as a subgroup of
\End(A[2])
.

Notation 5.32
.

Denote by
\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
the subspace of
\mathscr{A}
consisting of elements commuting with all elements of
\Gamma
. We also denote by
\Sym_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
the vector space of
r\times r
symmetric matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Recall that, in this section, we are denoting by
e
the Weil pairing on
A[2]
, which by convention takes values in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. When we wish to refer to the corresponding pairing on
E[2]
, we will denote it
e^{\prime}
. We denote by
P
the standard ‘dot-product’ pairing on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.

Lemma 5.33
.

Suppose that Condition
5.1
is satisfied. Then we have

\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}=1\otimes\Sym_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

Proof.

Since
\End_{\Gamma}(E[2])=\mathbb{F}_{2}
it follows that
\End_{\Gamma}(A[2])=1\otimes\End(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r})
. Take
\phi_{0}\in\End(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r})
. Then
1\otimes\phi_{0}\in\mathscr{A}
if and only if
e(u,(1\otimes\phi_{0})u)=0
for all
u\in E[2]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. We have
e=e^{\prime}\otimes P
, from which we see that
1\otimes\phi_{0}
lies in
\mathscr{A}
if and only if
\phi_{0}
is self-adjoint with respect to
P
. That is, if and only if
\phi_{0}
is represented by a symmetric matrix in the standard basis for
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.
∎

Remark 5.34
.

Note that if
\phi=1\otimes\phi_{0}\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
, then the pairing
e(-,\phi(-))
is given by
e^{\prime}\otimes P_{\phi_{0}}
, where
P_{\phi_{0}}
is the symmetric pairing on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
given by
(u,w)\mapsto P(u,\phi_{0}(w))
.

Lemma
5.33
will be crucial for making further progress. We thus make the following assumption.

Assumption 5.35
.

For the rest of this subsection, assume that Condition
5.1
is satisfied.

Let
\phi\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
. The description of
\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
afforded by Lemma
5.33
means that we can lift
\phi
to a
\mathbb{Q}
-endomorphism
\overline{\phi}
of
A
, invariant under the Rosati involution (indeed, with
\phi=1\otimes\phi_{0}
as above, we can take
\overline{\phi}
to be any endomorphism of
A=E^{r}
given by a lift of
\phi_{0}
to a symmetric
r\times r
matrix with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}
). Via Section
2.6
we associate to
\overline{\phi}
a theta group
\mathcal{H}_{\overline{\phi}}
for
A[2]
, with associated quadratic form

q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}\colon\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])\longrightarrow\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}.

The bilinear pairing associated to
q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}
sends
(f,g)
to
\left\langle f,\phi(g)\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
.
As explained in Section
2.6
we have
q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(Z_{0})=0
and
q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(V_{0})=0
.

Lemma 5.36
.

For any
t_{1}\in T_{1}
,
v_{0}\in V_{0}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{A[2]}
lifting
t_{1}
, and
\phi\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
, we have

(5.37)

\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x},v_{0}}(\phi)=q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(v_{0}+t_{1}).

Further, we have
\im(\Psi)^{\circ}=\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
, and
\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x},v_{0}}\in\im(\Psi)
if and only if

q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(v_{0}+t_{1})=0
for all
\phi\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}.

Proof.

Recall that, by definition, we have

\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x},v_{0}}(\phi)=\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x}).

We now compute

q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(v_{0}+t_{1})=q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(v_{0})+q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(t_{1})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}=q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(t_{1})+\left\langle v_{0},\phi(t_{1})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}.

Write
\mathbf{x}=(x_{u})_{u\in A[2]}
. Then

t_{1}=\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{u\in A[2]}x_{u}\otimes u.
Thus we have

\displaystyle q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in A[2]}q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(x_{u}\otimes u)+\sum_{\{u,u^{\prime}\}\subseteq A[2]}\left\langle x_{u}\otimes u,x_{u^{\prime}}\otimes\phi(u^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}.

Recall that we have
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}=H_{\Sigma}\otimes e
, where
H_{\Sigma}
is the Hilbert symbol pairing of Section
2.5
.
Thus

\left\langle x_{u}\otimes u,x_{u^{\prime}}\otimes\phi(u^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}=H_{\Sigma}(x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}})e_{2}(u,\phi(u^{\prime}))=\alpha(x_{u})\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})e_{2}(u,\phi(u^{\prime})).

For the final equality, recall that
x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{S}
are the classes of some
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, and that
H_{\Sigma}(x_{u},x_{u^{\prime}})
is equal to
\sum_{v\in\Sigma}H_{v}(\psi_{m},\psi_{m^{\prime}})
. As elements of
\mathcal{F}
,
m
and
m^{\prime}
are positive and are units locally at all primes
p\in\Sigma
. Thus
H_{v}(\psi_{m},\psi_{m^{\prime}})
is zero save possibly at
v=2
; at
v=2
it is equal to
1
if and only if both
m
and
m^{\prime}
are congruent to
-1
modulo
4
, that is, if and only if
\alpha(x_{u})\alpha(x_{u^{\prime}})=1
.

Comparing with the expression for
\lambda_{t_{1},\mathbf{x},v_{0}}(\phi)
, to prove (
5.37
) it is enough to show that we have
q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(x\otimes u)=e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{x})u,\phi(u))

for all
x\in\mathbb{S}
,
u\in A[2]
. Fix
m\in\mathcal{F}
lifting
x\in\mathbb{S}
, and take
u\in A[2]
. Then we have

q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(x\otimes u)=q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(\psi_{m}\cup u)=H_{\Sigma}(\delta^{(r)}(u)\cup\phi(u),\psi_{m})=\sum_{p\mid m}e(\delta^{(r)}(u)(\sigma_{p}),\phi(u))=e(\gamma^{(r)}(\sigma_{x})u,\phi(u)),

where the second equality is a consequence of
Lemma

2.8
(ii), the third equality follows from reciprocity, and the final equality follows from the definitions of
\gamma^{(r)}
and
\sigma_{x}
.

To deduce that
\im(\Psi)^{\circ}=\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma},
note that by
Lemma

5.30
we have
\im(\Psi)^{\circ}\subseteq\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
.
To prove the reverse inclusion, take
\phi\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\ker(\eta_{1})
. Then by (
5.37
) we have

\Upsilon_{\phi}(\mathbf{x})=\left\langle v_{0},\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+q_{\overline{\phi}}(v_{0}+\eta_{1}(\mathbf{x}))=q_{\overline{\phi}}(v_{0})=0.

Finally, having shown that
\im(\Psi)^{\circ}=\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma},
the remaining claim in the statement is an immediate consequence of (
5.37
).
∎

Recall that we are viewing both
V_{0}
and
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
inside
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
, as explained in Notation
4.50
. In this ambient space,
V_{0}
and
\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2]
have trivial intersection. In particular,
\mathfrak{A}=V_{0}\oplus(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])
is naturally a subspace of
\oplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])
. Recall that we have assumed Condition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.38
.

The left hand side of (
5.3
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Sym_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\#(\mathfrak{A}\cap Z_{0})}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of Lemmas
5.26
,
5.33
and
5.36
, is that we have

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Sym_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}\#\{a\in\mathfrak{A}\cap Z_{0}~~\colon~~q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(a)=0\textup{ for all }\phi\in\mathscr{A}^{\Gamma}\}.

As above we have
q_{\overline{\phi},\Sigma}(Z_{0})=0
(see Section
2.6
), giving the result.
∎

Lemma 5.39
.

We have

\frac{\#(\mathfrak{A}\cap Z_{0})}{\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])}=\#(V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}\cap Z_{0}),

where the orthogonal complement is taken with respect to the local Tate pairing.

Proof.

We have

(5.40)

\#(\mathfrak{A}\cap Z_{0})=\frac{\#\mathfrak{A}\#Z_{0}}{\#(\mathfrak{A}+Z_{0})}=\frac{\#\mathfrak{A}\#Z_{0}\#(\mathfrak{A}^{\perp}\cap Z_{0})}{\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\#H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])},

where for the last equality we are using that
Z_{0}
is its own orthogonal complement. Now
\#\mathfrak{A}=\#V_{0}\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])
.
Moreover, we have
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\#H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},A[2])=\#V_{0}\#Z_{0}
, since both
V_{0}
and
Z_{0}

are maximal isotropic for
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
(for
Z_{0}
see
[
30
, Proposition 4.10]
, while for
V_{0}
this is a consequence of Poitou–Tate duality, see
[
22
, Theorem I.4.10]
).
Substituting into (
5.40
) we deduce that

\#(\mathfrak{A}\cap Z_{0})=\#(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])\cdot\#(\mathfrak{A}^{\perp}\cap Z_{0}).

Since
V_{0}
is its own orthogonal complement under
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle_{\Sigma}
,
we have
\mathfrak{A}^{\perp}=V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

By
Lemma

4.58
we have
\mathcal{S}_{b}^{r}=V_{0}\cap(\mathbb{S}\otimes A[2])^{\perp}\cap Z_{0}
. The result now follows from Proposition
5.38
and Lemma
5.39
.
∎

5.6.
Distribution of Selmer groups

Theorem 5.41
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension, let

b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is non-empty. Suppose that
5.1
holds.
Then for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\#\Inj\bigg(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r},\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])\oplus\mathcal{S}_{b}}\bigg)=2^{\frac{r(r+1)}{2}}.

Proof.

Recall that a choice of
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
is
equivalent to a choice of a pair consisting of a (possibly negative) divisor
d_{0}
of
N

and a
c\in\left(\mathbb{Z}/4N\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\times}
; and that moreover
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is non-empty
if and only if we have
c\in S
. The result is therefore
an immediate consequence of
Theorem

4.60
and

Theorem

5.2
.
∎

Lemma 5.42
.

With the assumptions and notation of
Theorem

5.41
,
we have, for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}\#\Hom\bigg(\frac{\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})}{\delta_{d}(E[2])\oplus\mathcal{S}_{b}},\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\bigg)=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1+2^{j}).

Proof.

Let
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
For a finite abelian group
B
, let
B^{\lor}
denote the Pontryagin dual
\Hom(B,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})
. For
two abelian groups
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, we have
\Inj(B_{1},B_{2})=\Surj(B_{2}^{\lor},B_{1}^{\lor})
, where

\Surj
denotes the set of surjective homomorphisms. Moreover, for
i=1,2
,
B_{i}^{\lor}
is
(non-canonically) isomorphic to
B_{i}
, so that we have
\#\Inj(B_{1},B_{2})=\#\Surj(B_{2},B_{1})
.
In particular,
Theorem

5.41
also gives the average number of surjections
from the
2
-Selmer group onto
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
\mathfrak{n}(k,r)

denote the number of
k
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
. Then, for all
finite dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces
B
, we have

\#\Hom(B,\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r})=\sum_{k=0}^{r}\mathfrak{n}(k,r)\#\Surj(B,\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}),

and an analogous formula for the sought-for average number of homomorphisms from the
2
-Selmer group to
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
.
It is well-known that we have
\mathfrak{n}(k,r)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1-2^{r-i+1}}{1-2^{i}}
,
so by
Theorem

5.41
the sought-for average is equal to

\sum_{k=0}^{r}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\frac{1-2^{r-i+1}}{1-2^{i}}\Big)2^{k(k+1)/2}\right).

The result follows from
[
10
]
*Theorem 348, applied with
a=1
and
x=2
.
∎

We now deduce Theorem
1.6
.

Theorem 5.43
.

Let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension,
and
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is non-empty.
Suppose that
5.1
is satisfied. For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let

\alpha(r)=\prod_{j\geq 1}(1+2^{-j})^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{2}{2^{j}-1}.

Let
n_{b}=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathcal{S}_{b}
. Then there exists
m_{b}\in\{0,1\}
such that
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, we have
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\equiv n_{b}+m_{b}\pmod{2}
, and
for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X):\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=2g+n_{b}+m_{b}+2r\}}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}=\alpha(m_{b}+2r).

Proof.

The existence of
m_{b}
follows from
Corollary

2.5
.
The final assertion then follows by combining this constancy of
2
-Selmer
rank parity,
Lemma

5.42
, and
[
13
]
*Lemma 19.
∎

Part II
Applications: Galois module structures and the Hasse principle

6.
Galois module structure of Mordell–Weil groups

Our first application of Part 1 is to
1.3
.

6.1.
Finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[C_{2}]
-modules

For the duration of the section, let
G=\langle g\rangle
be a cyclic group of
order
2
.

If
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module, then
M^{G}
will denote
the maximal submodule of
M
on which
G
acts trivially.
Let
\N_{G}=1+g\in\mathbb{Z}[G]
.
A
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module that is
free over
\mathbb{Z}
. A
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice is
indecomposable
if it is not
a direct sum of proper sublattices.

Theorem 6.1
.

Every indecomposable
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice is isomorphic to exactly one of the
following:

•

\mathbb{Z}
with
G
acting trivially;

•

\mathbb{Z}(-1)
, a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
1
with
g
acting by
-1
;

•

\mathbb{Z}[G]
, a free
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module of rank
1
.

Proof.

See e.g.
[
31
]
.
∎

In the remainder of the section, we will continue using the notation from
Theorem
6.1
for the representatives of the isomorphism
classes of the indecomposable
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattices.

Lemma 6.2
.

We have the following identities:

•

\N_{G}\mathbb{Z}=2\mathbb{Z}=2(\mathbb{Z}^{G})
;

•

\N_{G}\mathbb{Z}(-1)=0
;

•

\N_{G}\mathbb{Z}[G]=\mathbb{Z}[G]^{G}
.

Proof.

This is an easy direct calculation.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
M\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus n_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)^{\oplus n_{2}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[G]^{\oplus n_{3}}
be an
arbitrary
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-lattice, where the multiplicities
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
n_{3}
are
non-negative integers. Then we have
(M^{G}:\N_{G}M)=2^{n_{1}}
, and the
multiplicities are uniquely determined by the isomorphism class of
M
.

Proof.

The first assertion immediately follows from Lemma
6.2
.
The second assertion follows from the first, together with the observations
that one has
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}M=n_{1}+n_{2}+2n_{3}
and
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}M^{G}=n_{1}+n_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
C_{2}
be a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module of order
2

(necessarily with trivial
G
-action), let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
let
U\cong C_{2}^{\oplus n}
, and let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module that is free of rank
1
over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Then one has
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}(M,U)\cong U
. Moreover, the class of isomorphism classes
of extensions of
M
by
U
is parametrised by elements of
U
as follows:
for every
u\in U
, there is a unique, up to isomorphism,
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module

M_{u}
that is an extension of
M
by
U
with the property that for every
lift
m
of every
\mathbb{Z}
-generator of
M
one has
g\cdot m=\pm m+u
,
with the sign being
+
if
M\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
-
if
M\cong\mathbb{Z}(-1)
; and every
extension of
M
by
U
is isomorphic to
M_{u}
for a unique
u\in U
.

Proof.

Since
M
is free of rank
1
over
\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,U)=0
.
Consequently, the local-global Ext spectral sequence (cf.
[
22
, Example I.0.8]
)
yields an isomorphism

H^{1}\big(G,\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,U)\big)\cong\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}(M,U),

with the map from left to right sending a
1
-cocycle
\phi
to the trivial
extension
U\oplus M
but with twisted
G
-action given by
g\cdot(u,m)=(u+\phi(g)(gm),gm)
.
We also have isomorphisms

H^{1}\big(G,\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,U)\big)\cong\Hom(G,U)\cong U,

the first induced by identification of
G
-modules
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,U)\cong U
given
by evaluation of homomorphisms at a chosen generator of
M
, and the second given by evaluating cocycles at the generator
g
of
G
.
The result follows by composing these three isomorphisms.
∎

Definition 6.5
.

Let
M
be an abelian group and
m\in M
. We say that
m
is
2
-divisible in

M
if there exists
m^{\prime}\in M
such that
2m^{\prime}=m
. We say that
M
is
2
-divisible
if every element of
M
is
2
-divisible in
M
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
U=C_{2}^{\oplus n}
be as in Lemma
6.4
, and let
M
be a

\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module whose
\mathbb{Z}
-torsion subgroup
M_{\tors}
is isomorphic to
U

and such that
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}M=2
and
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}M^{G}=1
. Then exactly one of
the following assertions holds:

(1)

there is an isomorphism
M\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\oplus U
;
in this case one has
M^{G}=\N_{G}M+M_{\tors}
;

(2)

there exists
m\in M
of infinite order and

u\in U\setminus\{0\}
such that
g\cdot m=m+u
; in this case

2m\in M^{G}
is
2
-divisible in
M
, but is not
2
-divisible in
M^{G}
, and
we have
M/M_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)
;

(3)

M
has a direct summand isomorphic to

\mathbb{Z}
; in this case one has
\#(M^{G}/(\N_{G}M+M_{\tors}))=2
, and any
generator of
\mathbb{Z}\subset M
represents the non-trivial class in this quotient.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.1
the lattice
M/M_{\tors}
is isomorphic to one of

\mathbb{Z}[G]
or
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)
. If it is the former, then we are in case
(1)
,
since the module
\mathbb{Z}[G]
is projective. The claimed trichotomy follows from this and
from Lemma
6.4
. The second claims in cases
(1)

and
(3)
easily follow from Lemma
6.2
.
For the second claim in case
(2)
, note that the elements

m^{\prime}\in M
such that
2m^{\prime}=2m
are precisely the elements of the form
m^{\prime}=m+u^{\prime}

for
u^{\prime}\in U
. It follows from the explicit description of the
G
-action
that none of these are contained in
M^{G}
.
∎

6.2.
Identifying norms via cohomology

Now let
\Gamma
be a profinite group, and let
M
a
2
-divisible abelian group
with a continuous
\Gamma
-action. Let
\chi\colon\Gamma\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
be a
non-trivial continuous homomorphism, and let
H
be its kernel, so that

\Gamma/H
is isomorphic to
G
via a unique isomorphism. From now on, we will identify
\Gamma/H
with
G
.
Let
\mathbb{Z}(\chi)
denote
\mathbb{Z}
with each
\sigma\in\Gamma
acting as
multiplication by
\chi(\sigma)
, and define
M(\chi)=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}(\chi)

with diagonal
G
-action.
Note, that the
\Gamma
-modules
M[2]
and
M(\chi)[2]
may be
canonically identified with each other, since multiplication by
-1
acts
trivially on them. Similarly,
M^{H}
and
M(\chi)^{H}
may be canonically
identified with each other. Below, we will make these identifications.

Since
M
is
2
-divisible, we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow M[2]\longrightarrow M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\longrightarrow 0.

Taking cohomology gives a coboundary map

\delta\colon M^{\Gamma}/2M^{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2]).

We obtain similarly another coboundary map

\delta_{\chi}\colon M(\chi)^{\Gamma}/2M(\chi)^{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2]).

Note, that under the identification between
M(\chi)^{H}
and
M^{H}
,
the group
M(\chi)^{\Gamma}\subset M(\chi)^{H}

is identified with the kernel of
\N_{G}\colon M^{H}\to M^{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

The map
\delta
induces an isomorphism

\delta\colon\N_{G}(M^{H})/2M^{\Gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\im(\delta)\cap\im(\delta_{\chi})\subseteq H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2]).

In particular, the image
\im(\N_{G})
of
\N_{G}\colon M^{H}\to M^{\Gamma}

consists of precisely those
m\in M^{\Gamma}
for which one has

\delta(m)\in\im(\delta_{\chi})
.

Proof.

Let
g
denote the non-trivial element of
G=\Gamma/H
. We have
\Gamma
-equivariant
maps
M\rightarrow M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G]
and
M(\chi)\rightarrow M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G]

given by
m\mapsto m\otimes(1+g)
and
m\mapsto m\otimes(1-g)
respectively.
Moreover, since
M
is
2
-divisible, there is a surjective
\Gamma
-equivariant
map
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G]\rightarrow M\oplus M(\chi)
given by

m_{1}\otimes 1+m_{2}\otimes g\mapsto(m_{1}+m_{2},m_{1}-m_{2}),

whose kernel is
M[2]\otimes 1
. These maps fit into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\oplus M(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{M(\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the right-most vertical maps are the canonical inclusions. Since
M^{H}
is isomorphic
to
(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G])^{\Gamma}
via the map
m\mapsto m\otimes 1+g(m)\otimes g
, taking
cohomology yields the commutative diagram

\textstyle{M^{\Gamma}/2M^{\Gamma}~~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\big(M^{\Gamma}\oplus M(\chi)^{\Gamma}\big)/\psi(M^{H})~~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\chi)^{\Gamma}/2M(\chi)^{\Gamma}~~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phantom{hihihi}\delta_{\chi}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\Gamma,M[2]),}

where
\psi\colon M^{H}\rightarrow M^{\Gamma}\oplus M(\chi)^{\Gamma}
is given by

m\mapsto(m+gm,m-gm)
. Since each horizontal arrow is injective, to compute

\im(\delta)\cap\im(\delta_{\chi})
it suffices to compute the
intersection of the images of the two lefthand vertical maps. From the explicit
description of the map
\psi
we see that an element of

M^{\Gamma}\oplus M(\chi)^{\Gamma}
of the form
(m,0)
is equivalent modulo

\psi(M^{H})
to an element of the form
(0,m^{\prime})
if and only if
m=\N_{G}(n)
for
some
n\in M^{H}
. The result follows.
∎

6.3.
Norms and Selmer conditions

Let
k
be a number field, and let
E/k
be a principally polarised
abelian variety with full rational
2
-torsion. Let
\Sigma
be a finite set
of places of
k
, containing all places above
2\infty
and all places at which

E
has bad reduction. Recall that

\delta\colon E(k)/2E(k)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(k,E[2])
denotes the coboundary
homomorphism associated with the multiplication-by-
2
Kummer sequence, and that
for every
d\in k^{\times}
, we get the analogous map
\delta_{d}
for the quadratic
twist
E_{d}
in place of
E
. Recall also that we identify the Galois modules
E[2]

and
E_{d}[2]
, which allows us to view the image of
\delta_{d}
as also lying in
H^{1}(k,E[2])
.
Write
K=k(\sqrt{d})
.
If
\mathscr{A}
is either
k
or
k_{v}
for a place
v
of
k
, and
\mathscr{B}
is either
K
or

K_{w}
for a place
w
of
K
above
v
,
denote by
\N_{\mathscr{B}/\mathscr{A}}\colon E(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow E(\mathscr{A})
the norm map

P\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in\Gal(\mathscr{B}/\mathscr{A})}\sigma P.

Lemma 6.8
.

The map
\delta
induces an isomorphism

\delta\colon\N_{K/k}E(K)/2E(k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\im\delta\cap\im\delta_{d},

and for every place
v
of
k
the map
\delta_{v}
induces an isomorphism

\delta_{v}\colon\N_{K_{w}/k_{v}}E(K_{w})/2E(k_{v})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\im\delta_{v}\cap\im\delta_{d,v},

where
w
is an arbitrary place of
K
above
v
.

Proof.

The statement follows from
Lemma

6.7
with
M=E(k^{s})
and
\Gamma=G_{k}
,
respectively with
M=E(k_{v}^{s})
and
\Gamma=G_{k_{v}}
. See also
[
11
, Lemma 4.1]
, which proves this result via a direct cocyle computation.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose that we have
E(k)[2^{\infty}]=E(K)[2^{\infty}]=E[2]
and
\rk E(k)=\rk E_{d}(k)=1
.
Then:

(1)

if we have
E(K)/E(K)_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
, then
some Mordell–Weil generator of
E(k)
is in the image of the norm map
\N_{K/k}
;

(2)

if, moreover, for all Mordell–Weil generators
P\in E(k)

one has
K\not\subset k(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
, then the converse is also true, i.e. if
a Mordell–Weil generator of
E(k)
is in the image of the norm map
\N_{K/k}
,
then we have
E(K)/E(K)_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
.

Proof.

The result follows by applying
Theorem

6.6
to
M=E(K)
.
Note that the condition that no Mordell–Weil generator of
E(k)

become
2
-divisible in
E(K)
excludes option
(2)

of
Theorem

6.6
.
∎

For the rest of the section, we specialise to
k=\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 6.10
.

For a point
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})
that is not
2
-divisible,
let
\mathcal{F}(P)
be the family of all square-free integers
d^{\prime}
such that
the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank
\rk_{2}(E_{d^{\prime}}/\mathbb{Q})
of
E_{d^{\prime}}
is odd and such that one has
\delta(P)\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d^{\prime}}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Suppose that we have
E(\mathbb{Q})[2^{\infty}]=E(K)[2^{\infty}]=E[2]
,
assume that the
2
-part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is finite,
and suppose that we have
E(K)/E(K)_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
as
G
-modules.
Let
P
be a Mordell–Weil generator of
E(\mathbb{Q})
.
Then we have
d\in\mathcal{F}(P+Q)
for some
Q\in E[2]
.

Proof.

The hypothesis that one has
E(K)/E(K)_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
implies
that the ranks of
E(\mathbb{Q})
and of
E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})
are both equal to
1
.
The assumption on finiteness of Shafarevich–Tate group implies that

\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is equal to the rank of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
, and in particular is odd.

The result follows from this observation, and a combination of
Lemma

6.8

and
Lemma

6.9
.∎

Informally,
Theorem

6.11
says that if
E
has full
rational
2
-torsion and
d
is not contained in
\mathcal{F}(P)
for any
Mordell–Weil generator
P
, then there is a local obstruction to
E(K)

being isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[G]
as a Galois module. The families
\mathcal{F}(P)
, for
Mordell–Weil generators
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})
, are therefore the natural ones in which
to investigate frequencies of the Galois modules
\mathbb{Z}[G]
and
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}(-1)
.

We also have the following partial converse to
Theorem

6.11
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Assume the hypotheses of
Lemma

6.9
, and
assume, moreover, that the
2
-part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is finite.
Let
P
be a Mordell–Weil generator of
E(\mathbb{Q})
,
suppose that we have
d\in\cup_{Q\in E[2]}\mathcal{F}(P+Q)
,
K\not\subset\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}(P+Q))

for any
Q\in E[2]
, and
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2\dim E+1}
.
Then
E(K)/E(K)_{\tors}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[G]
.

Proof.

Since
\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2^{\infty}]
is assumed to be finite,
[
11
]
*Lemma 5.1 implies
that its
2
-torsion has even
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-dimension. Since we have

E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})[2]\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2\dim E}
, the assumption on
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

implies that we have
\rk(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=1
and that
\text{\tencyr Sh}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2]
is trivial, so that

\delta_{d}\colon E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})/2E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

is an isomorphism. Since we have
d\in\mathcal{F}(P+Q)
for some
Q\in E[2]
, we also have

\delta(P+Q)\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\im(\delta_{d})
for some
Q\in E[2]
.
The assumption that
K\not\subset\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}(P+Q))
for any
Q\in E[2]

implies that no Mordell–Weil generator of
E(\mathbb{Q})
becomes
2
-divisible in
E(K)
,
so the result follows from
Lemma

6.9
.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})
.
Then there exists a subset
S
of
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
such that
we have
\delta(P)\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
if and only if the image of
d
in

\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
is contained in
S
and all prime
numbers
p\notin\Sigma
with
p\mid d
are totally split in
\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
.

Proof.

By definition we have
\delta(P)\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
if and only if for all places
v
of
\mathbb{Q}

we have
\delta_{v}(P)\in\mathscr{S}_{v}\cap\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
, where recall from Section
2
that
\mathscr{S}
denotes the
image of the respective Kummer map. By
Lemma

2.2

(1)
, at the primes
p\notin\Sigma

with
p\nmid d
this condition is empty. For the places
v\in\Sigma
,
the intersection
\mathscr{S}_{v}\cap\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
only depends on the class of
d
in
\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
. Finally,
by
Lemma

2.2
, at the primes
p\notin\Sigma

with
p\mid d
we have
\delta_{v}(P)\in\mathscr{S}_{v}\cap\mathscr{S}_{d,v}
if and only
\delta_{v}(P)=0
. This is equivalent to
v
splitting completely in
\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
.
∎

The next result shows that for every
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})\setminus 2E(\mathbb{Q})
, the family

\mathcal{F}(P)
is a union of families
of the form
d_{0}\mathcal{F}_{c,L}
, where
L=\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
, and
\mathcal{F}_{c,L}
is as in Section
3
,
equivalently that it is a union of families
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, defined as in Section

1.3
, for appropriate
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
d
be a square-free integer, and let
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})\setminus 2E(\mathbb{Q})
.
Then we have
d\in\mathcal{F}(P)
if and only if all of the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

all primes
p\not\in\Sigma
of
\mathbb{Q}

with
p|d
are totally split in
\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)/\mathbb{Q}
;

(2)

for all places
v\in\Sigma
of
\mathbb{Q}

we have
\delta(P)\in\im\delta_{v}\cap\im\delta_{d,v}
;

(3)

the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is odd.

Moreover, there exists a subset
S^{\prime}\subset\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}

such that one has
d\in\mathcal{F}(P)
if and only if all primes
p\not\in\Sigma
of
\mathbb{Q}

with
p|d
are totally split in
\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)/\mathbb{Q}
and the image of
d

in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
is contained in
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

2.5
the class of
d
in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}

determines the parity of
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/k)
.
The result therefore follows from
Lemma

6.13
.
∎

For the rest of the section we will assume that
5.1
is satisfied.

We now prove
Proposition

1.2
from the introduction.
For an integer
d
, write
F_{d}=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
.

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety with
E(\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2\dim E}
.
Let

\mathcal{F}^{\temp}=\{d\in\mathbb{Z}:d\text{ square-free},\rk_{2}E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})=1\}.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}^{\temp}
is non-empty.
Then one has

\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}:|d|<X,E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}:|d|<X\}}=0.

Proof.

We claim that the hypotheses of
[
37
]
*Theorem 2.14, Case 2.13
are satisfied in our situation, with
N=E[2^{\infty}]
. Indeed, since we have full rational
2
-torsion,
there are no universal Selmer elements, and
the condition of being “potentially favoured” is satisfied by taking
w=1

in
[
37
]
*Proposition 4.1; the condition of being “uncofavoured”
is equivalent to our assumption that
E[2]
have no proper non-trivial
\Gamma
-invariant subgroups;
the alternating structure on
N
is induced by the principal polarisation on
E
;
and in light of the uncofavoured condition, the condition in the fourth bullet point
of
[
37
]
*Case 2.13 can be checked to be equivalent to the assumption that

\End_{\Gamma}E[2]=\mathbb{F}_{2}
; see also
[
37
]
*Remark 4.6.

Now, it follows from
[
37
]
*Theorem 2.14 that
we have
\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}:|d|<X\}\asymp X
.
If
d\in\mathcal{F}^{\temp}
is such that
\text{\tencyr Sh}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
contains
\mathbb{Q}_{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then
the rank of
E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})
is
0
, so that we certainly cannot have
E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]
.
Thus, we may restrict to those
d
for which the
2
-part of the Shafarevich–Tate group
of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is finite.
The result follows from the combination of
Theorem

6.11
,

Proposition

6.14
, and
Proposition

3.2
.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

Note that if
\text{\tencyr Sh}(E/\mathbb{Q})[2^{\infty}]
is finite, then
\mathcal{F}^{\temp}

in
Corollary

6.15
contains

d=1
, and in particular is non-empty.

On the other hand, assuming finiteness of relevant (parts of) Shafarevich–Tate groups, we now deduce from the main result of Part 1 that in some
situations, the density of
d
for which one has
E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]

among those that satisfy the necessary local conditions is positive.

Let
L=\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
.
For
b=(b_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
, recall the definition
of
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
from Section
1.3
.
Let
b
be such that
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\cap\mathcal{F}(P)
is non-empty, equivalently, by
Proposition

6.14
, such that we have
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\subset\mathcal{F}(P)
.
Recall from
Definition

4.56
the definition of the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
:

\mathcal{S}_{b}=\ker\Big(H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\Sigma}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},E[2])/\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}\Big),

where
\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}
is the image of the coboundary map
\delta_{b_{v},v}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},E[2])
.
Recall also that we have
\mathcal{S}_{b}\subset\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety of rank
1
and with
full rational
2
-torsion, let
P\in E(\mathbb{Q})
be a non-divisible point of infinite order,
and let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that we have
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\subset\mathcal{F}(P)
.
Assume that
5.1
is satisfied.
Suppose that the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is
1
-dimensional.
Assume that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, the
2
-part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of the quadratic twist

E_{d}
over
\mathbb{Q}
is finite.
Then we have

\liminf_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X,E(F_{d})/E(F_{d})_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}}>0.419.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.43
, the proportion among
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
of those for which one has

\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=2\dim E+1
is
\alpha(0)\approx 0.4194\ldots
.
The result follows from this and
Proposition

6.12
.
∎

7.
Hasse principle for genus one hyperelliptic curves

Let
f(x)\in\mathbb{Q}[x]
be a separable monic quartic polynomial.
For
d\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, let
C_{d}
be the hyperelliptic curve with affine model
dy^{2}=f(x)
.
Let
E_{d}
be the Jacobian of
C_{d}
, so that
C_{d}
defines a
2
-covering of
E_{d}
,
and let
[C_{d}]
denote the corresponding class is
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E_{d}[2])
.
When
d
is a square in
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, we drop it from the subscripts.

Since
f
is monic, the curve
C
has a rational point, so the corresponding
class
[C]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
lies in the image of the coboundary map

\delta\colon E(\mathbb{Q})/2E(\mathbb{Q})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
.
One checks readily that under the identification

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])\cong H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E_{d}[2])
of Section
2.2
,
the class
[C]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
gets identified with

[C_{d}]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E_{d}[2])
, so that
C_{d}
has a rational point if and only
if
[C]
lies in the image of
\delta_{d}
, and it has a point everywhere
locally if and only if
[C]
lies in
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Let
L
be the splitting field of
f
.
Assume for the rest of the subsection that
E/\mathbb{Q}
has full rational
2
-torsion,
and that Condition
5.1
is satisfied for
E
.
Define

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}:d\text{ is square-free and }[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\}.

The condition that
E
have full rational
2
-torsion implies that the Galois group of
L/\mathbb{Q}
is contained in the Klein
4
-group.
By
Lemma

2.2
, the condition that
[C]\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})

implies that all odd primes
p|d
at which
E
has good reduction split completely in
L
, so that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a union of families of the form
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, as defined in Section
1.3
. In particular, the following is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

3.2
.

Proposition 7.1
.

There is a positive constant
c_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
such that
we have

\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:d<X\}\sim c_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

Analogously to
Theorem

6.17
, we deduce the following result.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
2
,
\infty
,
and all places of bad reduction for
E
, and let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}

be such that we have
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and such that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank
\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is odd.
Suppose that the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
, as defined in
Definition

4.56
, is
1
-dimensional.
Assume that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, the

2
-part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of
E_{d}
over
\mathbb{Q}
is finite.
Then we have

\liminf_{X\to\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X,C_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}}>0.4194.

8.
Hasse principle for Kummer varieties

Let
E/\mathbb{Q}
be a principally polarised abelian variety with full rational
2
-torsion.
Let
g=\dim E
. Fix a non-zero class
\zeta\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])
and denote by
Y_{\zeta}
the corresponding
2
-covering of
E
.
Thus
Y_{\zeta}
is a torsor under
E
equipped with an involution
\iota
compatible with multiplication by
-1
on
E
.
For a field extension
F/\mathbb{Q}
, we have
Y_{\zeta}(F)\neq\emptyset
if and only if the image of
\zeta
in
H^{1}(F,E)
is zero.

For a squarefree integer
d
, denote by
Y_{\zeta,d}
the corresponding quadratic twist of
Y_{\zeta}
.
For a place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
and
b_{v}\in\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
,
we denote by
Y_{\zeta,b_{v}}
the quadratic twist by
b_{v}
of the base-change of
Y_{\zeta}
to
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
.
Noting that
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])=\Hom_{\cts}(G_{\mathbb{Q}},E[2])
, we denote by
L/\mathbb{Q}
the fixed field of the kernel of
\zeta
.
Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
, containing
2
,
\infty
, all places at which
E
has bad reduction, and all places that ramify in
L/\mathbb{Q}
.

Recall from Section
2.7
that we can naturally view
\Gal(L(E[4])/L)
as an additive subgroup
\Gamma
of
\End(E[2])
.
We remind the reader that Condition
5.1
is the assertion
that
\End_{\Gamma}(E[2])=\mathbb{F}_{2}
and that the only subspaces of
E[2]
stable under
\Gamma
are
0
and
E[2]
.

For
b=(b_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
, recall from Definition
4.56
that the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is defined as the subspace of
H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
consisting of elements which, for all
v\in\Sigma
, satisfy the Selmer conditions associated to
E_{b_{v}}
.

Finally, recall from Section
2.4
the definition of the local functions
\kappa_{v}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Given
b=(b_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
, we define
\kappa(b)\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
by

\kappa(b)=\rk_{2}(E/\mathbb{Q})+\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\kappa_{v}(b_{v})\pmod{2}.

By Theorem
2.4
, given any squarefree integer
d
whose class in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
is equal to
b
, we have

\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\equiv\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\equiv\kappa(b)\pmod{2}.

We will sometimes impose the following condition.

Condition 8.1
.

There exists
b=(b_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
such that (1): we have
\mathcal{S}_{b}=\{0,\zeta\}
; and (2): the parity
\kappa(b)
is odd.

Remark 8.2
.

The condition that
\mathcal{S}_{b}
be generated by
\zeta
can be rephrased as follows. The condition that
\zeta\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
is equivalent
to the condition that for all
v\in\Sigma
, we have
Y_{\zeta,b_{v}}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\neq\emptyset
; while the condition that
no element
\zeta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])\setminus\{0,\zeta\}
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is equivalent to the condition that
for each class
\zeta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
with
\zeta^{\prime}\notin\{0,\zeta\}
, there is a place
v\in\Sigma
such that
Y_{\zeta^{\prime},b_{v}}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})=\emptyset
.

Phrased in this way, we see that the existence of an element
b
satisfying part (1) of Condition
8.1
is a version of Condition E of
[
35
]
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose that Conditions
5.1
and
8.1
are satisfied.
Then there is a positive constant
c_{0}
such that we have

\#\{d\in\mathbb{Z}~~:~~d\textup{ square-free},~|d|<X,~\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\left\langle\zeta,\delta_{d}(E[2])\right\rangle\}\sim c_{0}X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

Proof.

Let
b=(b_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}
be as in Condition
8.1
. We claim that there exists a squarefree integer
d
whose class in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
is equal to
b
, and such that each prime
p\notin\Sigma
dividing
d
splits completely in
L/\mathbb{Q}
. For
v\in\Sigma
, denote by
\psi_{b_{v}}
the quadratic character
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{v}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
associated to
b_{v}
. To prove the claim it suffices to show that, for every quadratic character
\chi\colon\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, we have

\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\psi_{b_{v}}\cup\chi)=0
(see e.g.
[
27
, Proposition 5.2]
).

Fix a character
\chi\colon\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Since
L
is defined as the fixed field of the kernel of
\zeta
, the map
\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})\to E[2]
sending
\sigma\in\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q})
to
\zeta(\sigma)
is an injection. By non-degeneracy of the Weil pairing on
E[2]
, there is
x\in E[2]
such that
\chi=x\cup\zeta
. We then have

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\psi_{b_{v}}\cup\chi)=\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}\big((\psi_{b_{v}}\cup x)\cup\zeta\big)=\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\delta_{b_{v},v}(x)\cup\zeta)+\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\delta_{v}(x)\cup\zeta),

where the second equality follows from Lemma
2.11
.
Let
v\in\Sigma
. By the assumption that
Y_{\zeta,b_{v}}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\neq\emptyset
,
both
\delta_{b_{v},v}(x)
and
\res_{v}(\zeta)
lie in
\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}_{b_{v},v}
is isotropic for the local Tate pairing, we have

\inv_{v}(\delta_{b_{v},v}(x)\cup\zeta)=0
. Thus we have

\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\delta_{b_{v},v}(x)\cup\zeta)=0
. Further, by reciprocity for the Brauer group, we have

\sum_{v\in\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\delta_{v}(x)\cup\zeta)=\sum_{v\notin\Sigma}\inv_{v}(\delta_{v}(x)\cup\zeta)=0,

where the final equality follows from the fact that, for each
v\notin\Sigma
,
both
\delta_{v}(x)
and
\res_{v}(\zeta)
are unramified. This proves the claim.

By the claim, the family denoted
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
in the statement of Theorem
1.6
is non-empty.
In the presence of Condition
8.1
, the
r=0
case of Theorem
1.6

shows that the proportion of
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
with
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=2g+1
is equal to
\alpha(0)>0.41
.
In particular, this proportion is positive. Condition
8.1
guarantees that, for any

d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, we have
\zeta\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
. Moreover, for any
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
which is divisible
by at least one prime
p\notin\Sigma
, it follows from the cases
v=p
of Lemmas

2.1
and
2.2
(2) that
\dim\delta_{d}(E[2])=2g

and
\delta_{d}(E[2])\cap\{0,\zeta\}=0
. Consequently, we have
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\geq 2g+1
, with equality if and only if
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\left\langle\zeta,\delta_{d}(E[2])\right\rangle
. Combining this discussion with
Proposition

3.2
, we see that there is a positive constant
c_{b}
such that

\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}~~:~~~|d|<X,~\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\left\langle\zeta,\delta_{d}(E[2])\right\rangle\}\sim c_{b}X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

On the other hand, if
d\in\mathbb{Z}
is a squarefree integer such that
\zeta\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
, then it follows from
Lemma

2.2
(2) that any prime divisor
p
of
d
with
p\notin\Sigma
splits completely in
L/\mathbb{Q}
. Consequently, we have
d\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{b^{\prime}}
for some
b^{\prime}\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
. Up to a possibly different positive leading constant, the family
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{b^{\prime}}
has the same asymptotics as the family
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{b}
. Theorem
1.6
then shows that the limit governing the proportion of
d
in
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{b^{\prime}}
for which
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\left\langle\zeta,\delta_{d}(E[2])\right\rangle
exists (depending on the value of
b^{\prime}
it may be zero). Summing the contribution to the left hand side of the statement from each of the finitely many possibilities for
b^{\prime}
gives the result.
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

Suppose that Conditions
5.1
and
8.1
are satisfied.
Assume further that the
2
-primary part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of each quadratic twist of
E
is finite. Then we have

\#\{d\in\mathbb{Z}~~:~~d\textup{ square-free},~|d|<X,~Y_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset\}\asymp X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

Moreover, when squarefree integers
d
for which
Y_{\zeta,d}
is everywhere locally soluble are ordered by
|d|
, a positive proportion satisfy
Y_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose that
d
is a squarefree integer such that
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=\left\langle\zeta,\delta_{d}(E[2])\right\rangle.
Since the quotient of
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
by
\delta_{d}(E[2])
surjects onto
\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2]
, we see that
\dim\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2]\leq 1
.
On the other hand, since we assume that the
2
-primary part of
\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is finite, it follows from
[
11
]
*Lemma 5.1 that
\dim\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2]
is even.
Thus
\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})[2]=0
. In particular, the class of
\zeta
in
\Sha(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
is trivial,
hence
Y_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.
As in the final paragraph of the proof of Theorem
8.3
, it follows from
Proposition

3.2
that
the number of squarefree integers
d
such that
|d|<X
and
Y_{\zeta,d}
is everywhere locally soluble, is
\ll X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}
.
The result therefore follows from Theorem
8.3
.
∎

Per
[
11
, Section 6]
, we can associate to
\zeta
a
generalized Kummer variety

\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}/\mathbb{Q}
. Explicitly, denote by
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta}
the blow up of
Y_{\zeta}
at the fixed points of the involution
\iota
. Then
\iota
extends to an involution
\widetilde{\iota}
of
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta}
, and the quotient
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}=\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta}/\widetilde{\iota}
is smooth and is the sought Kummer variety. For each squarefree integer
d
, when we repeat this construction with
Y_{\zeta,d}
in place of
Y_{\zeta}
, we obtain the same Kummer variety. Thus for each
d
, we have a natural degree
2
morphism
f_{d}\colon\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}\to\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
. For each point
x\in\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q})
, there is a squarefree integer
d
such that
x
lifts to
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})
. Thus we have

\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q})=\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb{Z}\textup{ sq. free}}f_{d}\big(\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\big).

Corollary 8.5
.

Suppose that Conditions
5.1
and
8.1
are satisfied.
Assume further that the
2
-primary part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of each quadratic twist of
E
is finite. Then we have

\#\{d\in\mathbb{Z}~~:~~d\textup{ square-free},~|d|<X,~f_{d}(\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}\big(\mathbb{Q})\big)\neq\emptyset\}\asymp X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

In particular, we have
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Since
\zeta\neq 0
, we have
\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
if and only if
Y_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset.
The result now follows from Corollary
8.4
.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

Condition
8.1
implies, in particular, that
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
is everywhere locally soluble. Indeed, as explained in Remark
8.2
, for places
v\in\Sigma
it is built into the definition of Condition
8.1
that
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\neq\emptyset
. For a place
v\notin\Sigma
, it follows from the fact that
\zeta
is unramified at
v
and
E
has good reduction at
v
that
Y_{\zeta}
, hence also
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
, has a
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
-point.

Assuming finiteness of relevant Shafarevich–Tate groups, a consequence of Corollary
8.5
is that, when Conditions
5.1
and
8.1
are satisfied, the Kummer variety
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
satisfies the Hasse principle. Results of this type appear in several places in the literature
[
35
,
11
,
12
,
27
]
. Of those, the works of Skorobogatov–Swinnerton-Dyer
[
35
]
and Harpaz
[
12
]
treat Kummer varieties associated to abelian varieties with full rational
2
-torsion. Like Corollary
8.5
, these results are also conditional on finiteness of relevant (parts of) Shafarevich–Tate groups, and similarly establish the existence of rational points on
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}
by deducing the existence of points on the
2
-coverings
Y_{\zeta,d}
. However, they establish only the existence of a
d
for which
Y_{\zeta,d}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
, while Corollary
8.5
gives precise information about the number of such
d
.

To the best of our knowledge, even the existence result of Corollary
8.5
is new. As in Remark
8.2
, with the parity condition (2) removed, Condition
8.1
is a direct analogue of
[
35
, Condition E]
. On the other hand, even accounting for the difference in setup, Condition
5.1
appears at first sight to be quite different to both the remaining condition in
[
35
, Theorem 1]
and the single condition in
[
35
, Theorem 2.8]
(as in
[
12
, Remark 2.10]
, the absence of a version of
[
35
, Condition E]
in the work of Harpaz is due to the presence of a second descent step).
However, we show in Proposition
8.10
below that variants of those conditions in fact imply Condition
5.1
. Combined with Example
8.9
below, Proposition
8.10
also serves to provide a large supply of abelian varieties for which Condition
5.1
holds.

Notation 8.7
.

For a place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
, denote by
\Phi_{v}
the group of connected components of the geometric special fibre of the Néron model of
E
at
v
.
Recall from
[
12
, Definition 2.1, Remark 2.2]
that a
2
-structure

M
for
E
is a set of
2g
odd places such that
E

has semistable reduction of toric rank
1
at each
v\in M
, and such that the natural map
E[2]\to\oplus_{v\in M}\Phi_{v}/2\Phi_{v}
is an isomorphism.
By
[
12
, Remark 2.2]
, for each
v\in M
, we have
\Phi_{v}[2^{\infty}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. For such
v
, by non-degeneracy of the Weil pairing

e\colon E[2]\times E[2]\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, there is a unique
P_{v}\in E[2]
such that the reduction map
E[2]\to\Phi_{v}/2\Phi_{v}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
is given by

x\mapsto e(x,P_{v})
.

Definition 8.8
.

We say that a
2
-structure
M
is
strict
if, for any two distinct elements
v,v^{\prime}
of
M
, the Weil pairing between
P_{v}
and
P_{v^{\prime}}
is non-trivial, i.e.
e(P_{v},P_{v^{\prime}})=1
.

Example 8.9
.

Let
g
be a positive integer and let
a_{1},...,a_{2g+1}\in\mathbb{Z}
be pairwise distinct. Let
f(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{2g+1}(x-a_{i})
. Let
y^{2}=f(x)
be the corresponding genus
g
hyperelliptic curve, and let
E
be its Jacobian. Let
M=\{p_{1},...,p_{2g}\}
be a set of odd primes such that, for each
i\in\{1,...,2g\}
, we have
\ord_{p}(a_{i}-a_{2g+1})=1
and
\ord_{p}(a_{j}-a_{j^{\prime}})=0
for all distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in\{1,...,2g+1\}
with
\{j,j^{\prime}\}\neq\{i,2g+1\}
. Arguing as in
[
12
, Proof of Theorem 1.3 assuming Theorem 2.8]
, we see that
M
is a
2
-structure for
E
, and that for each
p_{i}\in M
, the point
P_{p_{i}}\in E[2]
is given by the class of the divisor
(0,a_{i})-(0,a_{2g+1})
. From the description of the Weil pairing on
E[2]
given in
[
5
, Section 5.2]
, we see that this
2
-structure is strict.

Proposition 8.10
.

Suppose that
E
has a strict
2
-structure
M
over which
\zeta
is unramified (i.e. such that
\res_{v}\zeta
is unramified for all
v\in M
). Then:

(1)

Condition
5.1
is satisfied;

(2)

if there is
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
satisfying part (1) of Condition
8.1
, then Condition
8.1
is satisfied.

Proof.

(1)
. Let
v\in M
and let
\sigma_{v}
be any element of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
that topologically generates the tame inertia group at
v
.
Recall from Definition
2.10
the map
\gamma\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\End(E[2])
.
We claim that we have
\gamma(\sigma_{v})=\phi_{P_{v}}
, where
\phi_{P_{v}}\in\End(E[2])
sends each
x\in E[2]
to
e(x,P_{v})P_{v}

(cf. Proposition
2.15
). Indeed, fix
x\in E[2]
. If we have
e(x,P_{v})=0
, then the image
of
x
is
2
-divisible in
\Phi_{v}
, hence also in
E(\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\ur})
, where
\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\ur}
denotes the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
. Thus we have
\gamma(\sigma_{v})(x)=\delta(x)(\sigma_{v})=0
. Conversely, if
e(x,P_{v})=1
, then
x
has non-trivial image in
\Phi_{v}/2\Phi_{v}
, and by
[
12
, Lemma 3.6]
we have
\delta(x)(\sigma_{v})=P_{v}
. That is,
\gamma(\sigma_{v})(x)=P_{v}
. This proves the claim.

To complete the proof, we begin by noting that for each
v\in M
, the image of
\sigma_{v}
in
\Gal(\mathbb{Q}(E[4])/\mathbb{Q})
lies in
\Gal(L(E[4])/L)
.
Indeed, since
\res_{v}\zeta
is unramified, we have
\zeta(\sigma_{v})=0
, so that
\sigma_{v}
is contained in
\ker(\zeta)=G_{L}
.
By the claim, the image
\Gamma\leq\End(E[2])
of
\Gal(L(E[4])/L)
under
\gamma
contains the transvections
\{\phi_{P_{v}}\}_{v\in M}
. Since
M
is strict, the result follows from Proposition
2.15
.

(2)
. Cf.
[
35
, Lemmas 5 and 8]
. Suppose
b=(b_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}
satisfies part (1) of Condition
8.1
. Fix a choice of
w\in M
, noting that
w\in\Sigma
. We claim that we can find
b_{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times 2}
such that the tuple
b^{\prime}
, obtained from
b
by replacing the
w
-component with
b_{w}^{\prime}
, again satisfies part (1) of Condition
8.1
. Indeed, first suppose that
\res_{w}(\zeta)=0
. Then every quadratic twist of
Y_{\zeta}
has a
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
-point. Moreover,
w
splits completely in
L/\mathbb{Q}
, hence for any element
\zeta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
, we have
\res_{w}(\zeta^{\prime})=0
also. If
\zeta^{\prime}\notin\{0,\zeta\}
, then
\zeta^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}_{b}
by assumption, so there is
v\in\Sigma
such that
Y_{\zeta^{\prime},b_{v}}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})=\emptyset
(cf. Remark
8.2
). Since
\res_{w}(\zeta^{\prime})=0
, we cannot have
v=w
. Thus we can replace
b_{w}
with
any

b_{w}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times 2}
, and part (1) of Condition
8.1
continue to hold. Next, suppose that
\res_{w}(\zeta)\neq 0
. In this case, since
Y_{\zeta,b_{w}}(\mathbb{Q}_{w})\neq\emptyset
, it follows from the first claim in the proof of
[
12
, Lemma 3.16]
that we already have
b_{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times 2}
.

Now fix
b=(b_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}
satisfying part (1) of Condition
8.1
. As is possible by the claim, we suppose that
b_{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times 2}
, where
w\in M
is fixed as above. By assumption,
E_{b_{w}}
is the quadratic twist of
E
by an unramified (possibly trivial) character. Let
u
be the unique non-trivial class in
\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}_{w}^{\times 2}
. Since
\zeta\in\mathscr{S}_{b_{w},w}
is unramified at
w
, it follows from
[
12
, Lemma 3.25]
that
\zeta
lies in both
\mathscr{S}_{b_{w},w}
and
\mathscr{S}_{ub_{w},w}
. Moreover, given any
\zeta^{\prime}\in H^{1}(L/\mathbb{Q},E[2])
, we have that
\zeta^{\prime}
is unramified at
w
also, hence by the same lemma we have
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{b_{w},w}
if and only if
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{ub_{w},w}
. Letting
b^{\prime}
denote the tuple obtained from
b
by replacing the
w
-component with
ub_{w}
, we see that both
b
and
b^{\prime}
satisfy part (1) of Condition
8.1
. We claim that exactly one of
b
and
b^{\prime}
satisfies part (2) of Condition
8.1
. For this, it suffices to show that the invariants
\kappa_{w}(b_{w})
and
\kappa_{w}(ub_{w})
(the contributions from the place
w
to the parity) are distinct. Now
\{b_{w},ub_{w}\}=\{1,u\}
, and we trivially have
\kappa_{w}(1)=0
. On the other hand,
\kappa_{w}(u)
is, by definition, precisely the dimension modulo
2
of the vector space denoted
\overline{W}_{w}
in
[
12
, Lemma 3.25]
. It is shown there that this dimension is equal to
1
.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

Suppose that
E
has a strict
2
-structure over which
\zeta
is unramified. Suppose further that there exists
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
satisfying part (1) of Condition
8.1
.

Assume that the
2
-primary part of the Shafarevich–Tate group of each quadratic twist of
E
is finite. Then we have

\#\{d\in\mathbb{Z}~~:~~d\textup{ square-free},~|d|<X,~f_{d}(\widetilde{Y}_{\zeta,d}\big(\mathbb{Q})\big)\neq\emptyset\}\asymp X(\log X)^{\frac{1}{[L:\mathbb{Q}]}-1}.

In particular, we have
\mathscr{X}_{\zeta}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Combine Proposition
8.10
with Corollary
8.5
.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

As alluded to above, the existence part of Corollary
8.11
is closely related to
[
35
, Theorem 1]
. These results are not directly comparable. While
[
35
, Theorem 1]
considers Kummer varieties associated to a product of two elliptic curves over a general number field, Condition
5.1
ensures that any abelian variety satisfying the conditions of Corollary
8.11
is simple. Nevertheless, the similarity between the statements of these results should be clear. While the existence part of Corollary
8.11
can very likely be established by the methods of
[
12
]
(even without the innovative second descent step, cf.
[
12
, Remark 2.10]
), we include it to highlight that the analytic methods underpinning the proof Theorem
1.6
are capable of establishing results on the Hasse principle for Kummer varieties of an appreciable quality to those in the literature.

In fact, we would speculate that much more may be within reach. Indeed, the
L=\mathbb{Q}
case of Theorem
1.6
respresents only a small part of the breakthrough results of Smith
[
36
,
37
]
. Adapting Smith’s results in full to Frobenian families of twists such as those appearing in Theorem
1.6
would likely allow one to prove a version of Corollary
8.5
that applies over general number fields, allows very general
2
-torsion structures, and incorporates information from higher descents.

9.
The conjecture

In this section we explain two heuristics that lead to
1.8
.

9.1.
The assumptions

Throughout this section, we fix an elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{Q}
with full rational

2
-torsion. In particular
E
has a model of the form

E/\mathbb{Q}:y^{3}=(x-a_{1})(x-a_{2})(x-a_{3}),\hskip 10.00002pta_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{Q}.

For each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
P_{i}=(a_{i},0)\in E[2]
, and write
\lambda_{i}

for the (
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivariant) homomorphism
E[2]\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
given
by
P\mapsto e_{2}(P,P_{i})
.
Identifying
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})
with
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
, we obtain an isomorphism

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\colon H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}\right)^{2}.

We will use this identification throughout this section.
Under it, the map

\delta\colon E(\mathbb{Q})/2E(\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])\cong\left(\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}\right)^{2}

is given by

(9.1)

(x,y)\longmapsto\begin{cases}(x-a_{1},x-a_{2}),&x\notin\{a_{1},a_{2}\};\\
\left((a_{1}-a_{2})(a_{1}-a_{3}),a_{1}-a_{2}\right),&(x,y)=(a_{1},0);\\
\left(a_{2}-a_{1},(a_{2}-a_{1})(a_{2}-a_{3})\right),&(x,y)=(a_{2},0).\end{cases}

If
d
is an integer, the quadratic twist
E_{d}
is given by the Weierstrass equation

(9.2)

E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}:y^{2}=(x-a_{1}d)(x-a_{2}d)(x-a_{3}d),

and we have the isomorphism
\xi\colon E_{d}\to E
,

(x,y)\longmapsto(x/d,y/\sqrt{d})
, defined over
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
.
Since
\xi
identifies the Weil pairings on
E_{d}[2]
and
E[2]
, it follows that

\delta_{d}\colon E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})/2E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},E[2])=\left(\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}\right)^{2}
is given by

(x,y)\longmapsto\begin{cases}(x-da_{1},x-da_{2}),&x\notin\{da_{1},da_{2}\};\\
\left((a_{1}-a_{2})(a_{1}-a_{3}),d(a_{1}-a_{2})\right),&(x,y)=(da_{1},0);\\
\left(d(a_{2}-a_{1}),(a_{2}-a_{1})(a_{2}-a_{3})\right),&(x,y)=(da_{2},0).\end{cases}

Fix a point
P=(x_{P},y_{P})\in E(\mathbb{Q})
that is not
2
-divisible in
E(\mathbb{Q})
, and let
L=\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
.

In the special case of elliptic curves, Condition
5.1
can be replaced
by an easier to state, although not quite equivalent one.

Proposition 9.3
.

(1)

Suppose that
5.1
is satisfied. Then
E/\mathbb{Q}
has no
rational cyclic
4
-isogeny.

(2)

Conversely, suppose that
E/\mathbb{Q}
has no rational cyclic
4
-isogeny,
and that the fields
\mathbb{Q}(E[4])
and
\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
are linearly disjoint.
Then
5.1
is satisfied.

Proof.

This is essentially shown in
[
37
]
*Example 3.1. We leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

Remark 9.4
.

The fields appearing in
Proposition

9.3
can be described
explicitly: we have

\mathbb{Q}(E[4])=\mathbb{Q}\left(\sqrt{a_{1}-a_{2}},\sqrt{a_{1}-a_{3}},\sqrt{a_{2}-a_{3}},\sqrt{-1}\right)

and

\mathbb{Q}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}P\right)=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{x_{P}-a_{1}},\sqrt{x_{P}-a_{2}}).

Assumption 9.5
.

For the rest of the section we assume that
5.1
is satisfied.

9.2.
Main heuristic: equidistribution hypothesis

We recall some of the definitions from Part 1, and also give
a very concrete description of the systematic subspace in our situation.

Definition 9.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
containing
2
,
\infty
, and all
places at which
E
has bad reduction.
Let
b\in\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
be such that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
,
the
2^{\infty}
-Selmer rank of
E_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is odd and one has
\delta(P)\in\Sel(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
, in other words such that one has
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}\subset\mathcal{F}(P)
,
where
\mathcal{F}(P)
is as in
Definition

6.10
.

Recall that we have
\delta(P)=(x_{P}-a_{1},x_{P}-a_{2})
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=\left\langle x_{P}-a_{1},x_{P}-a_{2}\right\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.
Note that for all
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, we have

\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathcal{U}^{2}=\Sel_{2}(E_{d^{\prime}}/\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathcal{U}^{2},

since by
Remark

9.4
and the definition of
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
, we have that for all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
and
all primes
p\mid d
with
p\not\in\Sigma
, both
x_{P}-a_{1}
and
x_{P}-a_{2}
are trivial
in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times 2}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{b}
be this common intersection,
and define
n_{b}=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathcal{S}_{b}
.
Since we have
(x_{P}-a_{1},x_{P}-a_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
, we have
1\leq n_{b}\leq 4
.

The space
\mathcal{S}_{b}
just defined is nothing but the systematic subspace of

Definition

4.56
.

Remark 9.7
.

By
Proposition

3.2
we have

\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}:|d|<X\}\asymp X\log(X)^{\tfrac{1}{t}-1}
for some
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
.
Assuming existing conjectures
[
41
]
on the density of
elliptic curves in quadratic twist families having (
2^{\infty}
-)rank at least
2
,
asymptotically 100% of
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
have

\rk_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=1
, so that, up to a negligible error,
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
is the family of all
square-free
d\equiv b\pmod{\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}}
such that we have
\rk(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=1
and
\delta(P)\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Note that for 100% of all
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
the intersection
\delta(E(\mathbb{Q}))\cap\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
is trivial, since for
p\mid d
,
p\not\in\Sigma
, all elements of

\delta(E(\mathbb{Q}))
are unramified at
p
, while all non-trivial classes in
\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])

are ramified at
p
.
By
Remark

9.7
we expect the rank of
E_{d}(\mathbb{Q})
to be
1

for 100% of
d\in\mathcal{F}
, so that the image of
\delta_{d}
in
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])

typically contains exactly one non-zero element.
Our main heuristic assumption is that if one fixes an odd integer
r\geq n_{b}
, then
among those
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
for which one has
\dim\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})=2+r
, the
probability that this non-zero element of
\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})/\delta_{d}(E_{d}[2])
coincides
with
\delta(P)
is
1/(2^{r}-1)
. Combined with
Theorem

5.43
, this heuristic leads to the following concrete conjecture.

Conjecture 9.8
.

Write
\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)

for the set of
d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}
with
|d|\leq X
, and let
m_{b}\in\{0,1\}

be such that
m_{b}\equiv 1+n_{b}\pmod{2}
. Then one has

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X):\delta(P)\in\im\delta_{d}\}}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha(2r+m_{b})}{2^{n_{b}+2r+m_{b}}-1},

where
\alpha(2r+m_{b})
is as in
Theorem

5.43
.

It turns out that the infinite sum in
9.8
can be evaluated completely
explicitly, and turns out to be a rational number (which, of course,
depends on
n_{b}
). This can be shown through direct manipulation of the infinite sum,
using some identities from
[
13
]
. Instead, we will now present an alternative
form of the main heuristic, and use that to evaluate the probability in
9.8
.

9.3.
Main heuristic via random matrices

Fix, for this subsection only, a prime number
p
.
Let
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. It is conjectured in
[
3
]
that the
p^{\infty}
-torsion subgroups of
Tate-Shafarevich groups of “random” elliptic curves of rank
t

are distributed like cokernels of Haar-random alternating
s\times s
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

of rank
s-t
, in the limit as
s\to\infty
over all integers
s
for which
s-t
is even.
The
p^{\infty}
-Selmer groups of such elliptic curves are then modelled
as
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{t}\oplus R
, where
R
is a random group as just described.
If
t\in\{0,1\}
, then the probability
that the cokernel of such a random matrix has
p
-rank equal to
2r
for a given

r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is, again in the limit as
s\to\infty
, shown to be

\alpha(p,2r+t)
as defined in §
9.2
– see
[
3
]
*Theorem 1.10 and
[
30
]
*Proposition 2.6.

We will now explain how one may, in fact, equivalently model the whole
p^{\infty}
-Selmer
group in terms of random matrices, recovering both the divisible part and the
finite quotient. We will then adapt this heuristic to our situation by
accounting for the systematic subspace
\mathcal{S}_{b}
defined in §
9.2
.
As we will see, this random matrix model already predicts the equidistribution
hypothesis of §
9.2
, so that we will naturally recover
the infinite sum of
9.8
. Finally, we will use this
reformulation to evaluate this sum
as an explicit
rational
number.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
T
be a free finite rank
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module,
let
