M_{T}
be an endomorphism of
T
, and let
M_{W}
be the corresponding
endomorphism of
T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
obtained
by applying the functor
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to
M_{T}
. Then one has

\ker M_{W}\cong(\ker M_{W})_{\div}\oplus(\coker M_{T})_{\tors},

where
(\ker M_{W})_{\div}
is the maximal divisible subgroup of
\ker M_{W}
. Moreover
one has
(\ker M_{W})_{\div}\cong(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{r}
, where
r
is the rank of
\ker M_{T}
.

Proof.

Write
V=T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
W=T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
We have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M_{T}}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M_{V}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M_{W}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

with exact rows, where
M_{V}
is the endomorphism of
V
induced by
M_{T}
. Applying
the snake lemma yields the exact sequence

0\to\ker M_{T}\to\ker M_{V}\to\ker M_{W}\to\coker M_{T}\to\coker M_{V}.

Here, the right-most map is induced by
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, so that its kernel
is exactly the torsion subgroup of
\coker M_{T}
, while the map
\ker M_{V}\to\ker M_{W}

is induced by
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, so that its image is isomorphic
to
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{r}
. Since
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{r}
is an injective group, the sequence

0\to(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{r}\to\ker M_{W}\to(\coker M_{T})_{\tors}\to 0

splits, and the result follows.
∎

From now on, whenever
M
is an
s\times s
matrix over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for some

s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we denote by
M_{W}
the
endomorphism of
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{s}
induced by
M
as above.

It follows from
Lemma

9.9
that the model for the
p^{\infty}
-Selmer
group of a “random” elliptic curve of rank
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

proposed in
[
3
]
is equivalent to modelling such a Selmer group
as the kernel
of
M_{W}
, where
M
is a Haar-random alternating
s\times s
matrix of rank
s-t
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
in the limit as
s\to\infty
over integers for which
s-t
is even. Concretely,
we have the following result.

Proposition 9.10
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then:

(1)

for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq t}
, the pushforward of Haar
measure on the set of alternating
s\times s
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
of rank

s-t
under the function
M\mapsto\ker M_{W}
induces a discrete probability
distribution
\mathcal{T}_{s,t}
on the class of isomorphism classes of co-finitely
generated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules;

(2)

as
s\to\infty
over integers satisfying
s-t\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence of
distibutions
\mathcal{T}_{s,t}
converges to a probability distribution
\mathcal{T}_{t}
,
with respect to which the class of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules of the form

(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{t}\oplus R
where
R
is a finite
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module has mass
1
;

(3)

the pushforward of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
under the map
A\mapsto A/A_{\div}

defines a probability distribution on the class of finite
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules
that coincides with the distributions
\mathscr{T}_{t}=\mathscr{A}_{t}
from
[
3
]
.

Proof.

All the assertions are immediate consequences of
Lemma

9.9

and of the analysis in
[
3
]
.
∎

Since
\ker M_{W}
for random “large” alternating matrices
M
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

is a model for
p^{\infty}
-Selmer groups of elliptic curves, the resulting
model for
\Sel_{p}(E/\mathbb{Q})/\delta(E(\mathbb{Q})_{\tors})
is
(\ker M_{W})[p]
for those
same matrices
M
. Note that
(\ker M_{W})[p]=\ker\bar{M}
, where
\bar{M}

is the reduction of
M
modulo
p
.

We now modify the above model so as to take into account the systematic subspace
defined in §
9.2
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

(playing the rôle of
n_{b}
from Section
9.2
),
let
m\in\{0,1\}
be such that
m\equiv 1+n\pmod{2}
. For every
odd integer
s>n
, fix an
n
-dimensional subspace
S\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}

(playing the rôle of
S_{b}
), let
\mathcal{L}\subset S
be a
1
-dimensional subspace
(playing the role of
\langle\delta(P)\rangle\in\Sel_{2}(E_{d}/\mathbb{Q})
), and let
\rA_{s}

be the set of all alternating
s\times s
matrices
M
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

of rank
s-1
such that
S\subset\ker\bar{M}
. In the interest of keeping the notation light,
we have suppressed some of the dependencies from the notation.
Equip
\rA_{s}
with Haar measure.

Remark 9.11
.

If
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
M
is a random alternating
s\times s
matrix
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with respect to Haar measure, then by
[
3
]
*Proposition 2.1 and Lemma 3.10,
with probability
1
the matrix
M
has rank
s
, respectively
s-1
when

s
is even, respectively odd. It follows that in the definition
of
\rA_{s}
we could have omitted the condition of
M
having rank

s-1
.

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{s}
be the probability distribution on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
given
by pushforward of the probability distribution on
\rA_{s}
under the
function
M\mapsto\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\ker\bar{M})
.
Then for every
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{s}(n+2r+m)=\alpha(p,2r+m),

where the limit is taken over all odd integers
s
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
S
is generated by
the first
n
standard basis vectors, so that
\rA_{s}
is the set of all
alternating
s\times s
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
of rank
s-1
such that
all entries in the first
n
columns are divisible by
p
. We then
have
\ker\bar{M}=S\oplus\ker\bar{M^{\prime}}
, where
M^{\prime}
is the submatrix
of
M
obtained by deleting the first
n
rows and the first
n
columns,
and
\bar{M^{\prime}}
is its reduction modulo
p
.
The matrix
M^{\prime}
is distributed as a random alternating
(s-n)\times(s-n)

matrix, so the assertion follows from
Proposition

9.10
.
∎

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>n}
and for an alternating
s\times s
matrix
M

over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
of rank
s-1
, let
y(M)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{s}
be a generator of
\ker M
.
It is uniquely determined by
M
up to scalar multiplication by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.
In particular,
\langle y(M)\mod p\rangle\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}
is a well-defined
line in the kernel of
\bar{M}
, which we may view as an

(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-valued random variable.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>n}
, let
U\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}
be a subspace containing
S
such that
s-\dim U
is even,
and let
\rA_{s}(U)\subset\rA_{s}
be the subset consisting of all matrices

M
such that
\ker(\bar{M})=U
. Then the pushforward of the probability
Haar measure on
\rA_{s}(U)
under the function
M\mapsto\langle y(M)\mod p\rangle\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}

defines the uniform probability distribution on the set of lines in
U
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}
be two lines in
U
, and let
\bar{X}\in\GL_{s}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
be such that

\bar{X}U=U
and
\bar{X}\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{2}
. Let
X\in\GL_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be any lift of

\bar{X}
. Then the function
\rA_{s}(U)\to\rA_{s}(U)
,
M\mapsto X^{\Tr}MX

defines a measure preserving bijection between the set of
M\in\rA_{s}(U)

with
\langle y(M)\mod p\rangle=\mathcal{L}_{2}
and the set of those with
\langle y(M)\mod p\rangle=\mathcal{L}_{1}
.
∎

Definition 9.14
.

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>n}
, denote by
\gamma_{n}(s)
the probability that a random element

M
of
\rA_{s}
satisfies
\langle y(M)\mod p\rangle=\mathcal{L}
.

Proposition 9.15
.

We have

\lim_{s\to\infty}\gamma_{n}(s)=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{(p-1)\alpha(p,2r+m)}{p^{n+2r+m}-1},

where the limit is taken over all odd integers
s
.

Proof.

For every odd integer
s=2q+1\geq 1
we may compute
\gamma_{n}(s)
by conditioning on

\dim\ker(\bar{M})
for a random
M\in\rA_{s}
: by
Lemma

9.13
we have

\gamma_{n}(s)=\sum_{d=0}^{q}\frac{\prob_{s}(\dim\ker(\bar{M})=2d+1)}{(p^{2d+1}-1)/(p-1)},

where
\prob_{s}(\dim\ker(\bar{M})=2d+1)
denotes the probability that the reduction modulo
p

of a random element
M
of
\rA_{s}
has kernel of dimension
2d+1
.
Lemma

9.12

gives the limit of that probability as
s\to\infty
over odd integers. The result follows
by combining these two lemmas and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

We now evaluate the limit in Proposition
9.15
in a
different way, arguing by induction on
n
, the dimension of
S
.

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
s>1
be an odd integer. Then we have

\gamma_{1}(s)=\frac{(p-1)p^{s-1}}{p^{s}-1}.

Proof.

Let
\Alt_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be the set of alternating
s\times s
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, equipped
with Haar measure. By
[
3
]
*Proposition 2.1 and Lemma 3.10,
the subset of those matrices in
\Alt_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
that have rank
s-1
has mass
1
.
Let
\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\subset\Alt_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be that subset.
The probability
\gamma_{1}(s)
is the conditional probability
that a random element
M
of
\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
satisfies
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}

under the condition that
\mathcal{L}\subset\ker\bar{M}
. Applying Bayes’s formula, we have

\gamma_{1}(s)=\frac{\prob(\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}\text{ and }\mathcal{L}\subset\ker\bar{M})}{\prob(\mathcal{L}\subset\ker\bar{M})}.

To evaluate the numerator, notice that the condition
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}
already implies
that
\mathcal{L}\subset\ker\bar{M}
, so that the numerator is simply equal to the probability that
a random
M\in\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
satisfies
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}
. But for random
M\in\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
the line
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle
is easily seen to be equidistributed among all
lines in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}
, so the numerator is equal to
(p-1)/(p^{s}-1)
. To evaluate the
denominator, note that the condition that
\mathcal{L}\subset\ker\bar{M}
is equivalent
to every row of
\bar{M}
being in the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{L}
, i.e. to every
row of
M
lying in a certain index
p
subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{s}
. These conditions for
the first
s-1
rows are independent of each other, while for the last row it is then
automatic, since
M
is alternating. Thus, the denominator is
p^{-(s-1)}
, and the
proof is complete.
∎

Theorem 9.17
.

Suppose that
n>1
, and let
s>n
be an odd integer. Then we have

\gamma_{n}(s)=\frac{p^{s-n}(p-1)}{p^{s}-p^{n-1}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{p^{n-1}-1}{p-1}\gamma_{n-1}(s)\right).

Proof.

Let
S^{\prime}\subset S
be a subspace such that
S=\mathcal{L}\oplus S^{\prime}
. Let
\Alt^{\prime}_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

be the set of all alternating
s\times s
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
of rank
s-1
,
as in the proof of
Theorem

9.16
. Then, like in that proof,

\gamma_{n}(s)
is the conditional probability
that a random element
M
of
\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
satisfies
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}

under the condition that
S\subset\ker\bar{M}
. Applying Bayes’s formula,
we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\prob(\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}\text{ and }S\subset\ker\bar{M})}{\prob(S\subset\ker\bar{M})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prob(\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}\text{ and }S^{\prime}\subset\ker\bar{M})}{\prob(S\subset\ker\bar{M})}.

We claim that the denominator
is equal to
p^{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(s-i)}=p^{-sn+\binom{n+1}{2}}
.
Indeed, we may assume, without loss of generality, that
S
is generated
by the first
n
standard basis vectors, in which case the condition
that
S\subset\ker\bar{M}
is equivalent to the condition that
the first
n
columns of
M
have all entries divisible by
p
. Taking
into account the assumption that
M
be alternating, these are

(s-1)+(s-2)+\ldots+(s-n)
independent conditions, each having probability
1/p
.

Again applying Bayes’s formula, we deduce that the numerator is equal to

\displaystyle\prob\big(\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle=\mathcal{L}\hskip 10.00002pt\big|\hskip 10.00002ptS^{\prime}\subset\ker\bar{M}\big)\times\prob\big(S^{\prime}\subset\ker\bar{M}\big).

The second of these two factors is equal to
p^{-s(n-1)+\binom{n}{2}}
by the same
reasoning as was just applied with
S
in place of
S^{\prime}
.
To evaluate the first factor, we may partition the set of
M\in\Alt_{s}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
that satisfy
S^{\prime}\subset\ker\bar{M}
into
those for which
y(M)\textup{ mod }p
is in
S^{\prime}
, and those for which
y(M)\textup{ mod }p
is not in
S^{\prime}
. Among the
former,
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle
is equally likely to be any of the lines
in
S^{\prime}
, of which there are
(p^{n-1}-1)/(p-1)
, and the probability for each one of these is,
by definition,
\gamma_{n-1}(s)
.
Among the latter,
\langle y(M)\textup{ mod }p\rangle
is equidistributed among the lines
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{s}\setminus S^{\prime}
, of which there are
(p^{s}-p^{n-1})/(p-1)
.
Combining everything we have just said, we deduce that we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{sn-\binom{n+1}{2}-s(n-1)+\binom{n}{2}}\frac{p-1}{p^{s}-p^{n-1}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{p^{n-1}-1}{p-1}\gamma_{n-1}(s)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p^{s-n}(p-1)}{p^{s}-p^{n-1}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{p^{n-1}-1}{p-1}\gamma_{n-1}(s)\right),

as claimed.
∎

Corollary 9.18
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
m\in\{0,1\}
be such that
m\equiv 1+n\pmod{2}
, and define

\beta_{n}=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{(p-1)\alpha(p,2r+m)}{p^{n+2r+m}-1}.

Then we have

\beta_{1}=1-\tfrac{1}{p}
, and for all
n>1
we have

(9.19)

\beta_{n}=\frac{p-1}{p^{n}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{p^{n-1}-1}{p-1}\beta_{n-1}\right).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

9.15
,
Theorem

9.16

and
Theorem

9.17
.
∎

Remark 9.20
.

Let us temporarily write
\beta_{n}^{\prime}=(p-1)^{-1}p^{\frac{1}{2}n(n+1)}\beta_{n}
. Then the recurrence (
9.19
) gives

\beta_{n}^{\prime}=p^{\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)}-(p^{n-1}-1)\beta_{n-1}^{\prime},

along with
\beta^{\prime}_{1}=1
. Successively substituting this recurrence into itself and rewriting the final answer in terms of
\beta_{n}
, we find

\beta_{n}=\frac{p-1}{p^{\frac{1}{2}n(n+1)}}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}p^{\frac{1}{2}(n-i)(n-i-1)}\prod_{j=1}^{i}(p^{n-j}-1).

By combining
9.8
with
Corollary

9.18
,
Remark

9.7
,
and the discussion in §
7
, we arrive at
1.8
.

In light of
Lemma

6.9
and
Lemma

6.8
we are also led to the following conjecture.

Conjecture 9.21
.

Suppose that
E(\mathbb{Q})
has rank
1
.
With the same notation as in
9.8
, and still under Assumption
9.5
,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X):E(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d}))/E(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d}))_{\tors}\cong\mathbb{Z}[G]\}}{\#\mathcal{F}^{b}_{L}(X)}=\begin{cases}1/2,&n_{b}=1;\\
1/8,&n_{b}=2;\\
5/64,&n_{b}=3;\\
29/1024,&n_{b}=4.\end{cases}

9.4.
Examples with
n_{b}>1

The following example shows that
n_{b}
really can be greater than
1
.
Consider the elliptic curve

E:y^{2}=(x+1505)(x+712)(x-2216),

which has LMFDB label
145119.d2
. Then we have
E(\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}.

The point
P=(3188,133380)\in E(\mathbb{Q})
is a non-divisible point of infinite order.
Write
P_{1}=(-1505,0)
,
P_{2}=(-712,0)\in E[2]
.
Viewing the Kummer map as taking values in
(\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2})^{2}
, as in Section
9.1
, we have

\delta(P)=(13,3\cdot 13),\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\delta(P_{1})=(13\cdot 61,-13\cdot 61)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\delta(P_{2})=(13\cdot 61,-3\cdot 13).

We see from this that
\mathbb{Q}(\frac{1}{2}P)
is not linearly disjoint from
\mathbb{Q}(E[4])
,
so that
Proposition

9.3
(2)
does not apply,
but one can check that
5.1
is nevertheless satisfied for
L=\mathbb{Q}(\tfrac{1}{2}P)
.

We have
\Sigma=\{\infty,2,3,13,61\}
. The local images are as follows.

\displaystyle\delta_{\infty}(E(\mathbb{R})/2E(\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{\infty}(P_{1})=(1,-1)\rangle,

\displaystyle\delta_{2}(E(\mathbb{Q}_{2})/2E(\mathbb{Q}_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{2}(P)=(5,-1),\delta_{2}(P_{1})=(1,-1),\delta_{2}(5/4,*)=(1,5)\rangle,

\displaystyle\delta_{3}(E(\mathbb{Q}_{3})/2E(\mathbb{Q}_{3}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{3}(P)=(1,3),\delta_{3}(P_{1})=(1,-1)\rangle,

\displaystyle\delta_{13}(E(\mathbb{Q}_{13})/2E(\mathbb{Q}_{13}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{13}(P)=(13,13),\delta_{13}(5,*)=(2,2)\rangle,

\displaystyle\delta_{61}(E(\mathbb{Q}_{61})/2E(\mathbb{Q}_{61}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{61}(P_{1})=(61,61),\delta_{61}(P_{2})=(61,1)\rangle.

We have

\left(\frac{-1}{13}\right)=\left(\frac{3}{13}\right)=\left(\frac{61}{13}\right)=1

and
\left(\frac{13}{61}\right)=\left(\frac{3}{61}\right)=\left(\frac{-1}{61}\right)=1
.
From this we conclude that
S_{1}=\langle\delta(P)=(13,3\cdot 13),(1,3)\rangle
,
so that
n_{1}=2
.
Moreover, if we replace
P
by
P+P_{1}
or by
P+P_{2}
, then
5.1
is again satisfied,
and it can be deduced from the above calculations that in both cases one has
n_{1}=3
.

Appendix A
Stevenhagen’s heuristic via Selmer groups

In this Appendix we recall Stevenhagen’s conjecture on the solubility of the negative Pell
equation, and explain the relationship to the results and conjectures in this
paper
[
39
]
. Whilst most of the material in this section is
well known, we believe that the particular formulation that we give,
specifically the reinterpretation in terms of Selmer groups in quadratic twist
families, is novel, and is suggestive of the elliptic curve analogue presented
in the body of the paper.

If
d
is a positive square-free integer,
then the
Negative Pell equation with parameter
d
is the equation

(A.1)

x^{2}-dy^{2}=-1,

to which one seeks solutions

x
,
y
in integers. Fix, for the remainder of the section,
a square-free positive integer
d
, and let
F=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})
.
Clearly, if
Equation

A.1
is soluble, then
-1
is a norm
from
F_{d}
, equivalently every odd prime number
p
dividing
d

satisfies
p\equiv 1\pmod{4}
.

This already shows that
Equation

A.1
with parameter
d^{\prime}

is soluble for
0\%
of square-free positive integers
d^{\prime}

asymptotically. Specifically, writing
\mathcal{F}
for
the set of square-free integers
d^{\prime}>0
all of whose odd prime divisors are
congruent to
1\pmod{4}
, a variation of Landau’s theorem (see e.g.

[
8
, §1]
) implies that

\#\{d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d^{\prime}<X\}\sim\frac{cX}{\log(X)^{1/2}},

where
c=\frac{3\pi}{2}\prod_{p\equiv 1\pmod{4}}(1-p^{-2})^{1/2}\approx 0.464...
.
In light of this observation, it is natural to ask not for what proportion
of
all

d^{\prime}

Equation

A.1
with parameter
d^{\prime}
is soluble,
but for what proportion of
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
. The expected answer to this
question is given by a beautiful conjecture of Stevenhagen.

Conjecture A.2

(Stevenhagen)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}
denote the set of square-free integers
d^{\prime}

for which
Equation

A.1
with parameter
d^{\prime}
is soluble. Then the limit

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}~~:~~d^{\prime}<X\}}{\#\{d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d^{\prime}<X\}}

exists and is equal to the irrational number

c_{\textup{Pell}}=1-\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}(1-2^{-2j+1})=0.58057....

This conjecture and its heuristic justification are given in

[
39
]
. In landmark work
Fouvry–Klüners
[
8
, Theorem 1]
proved that

1-c_{\textup{Pell}}=0.4194...\leq\liminf_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}~~:~~d^{\prime}<X\}}{\#\{d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d^{\prime}<X\}}\leq 2/3.

Following several improvements on the bounds, Koymans–Pagano proved the conjecture in
[
18
]
.
To explain the analogy
between Stevenhagen’s conjecture and the present work, we now give
a reinterpretation of the justification for
A.2

in terms of certain quadratic twist families of Selmer groups.

The unit group
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is isomorphic to
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}
,
and it is well known that
Equation

A.1
is soluble if and only if the
norm of any (equivalently every) generator of
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
is
equal to
-1
. We will study this condition via
Lemma

6.7
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\times}
denote the
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module of units in the ring integers of

\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
\chi\colon G_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
be the quadratic character of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}

corresponding to
F
, let
\mathbb{Z}(\chi)
be the group
\mathbb{Z}
with each
\sigma\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}

acting by multiplication by
\chi(\sigma)
, and let
\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)
denote the

G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-module
\mathcal{O}^{\times}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}(\chi)
with diagonal
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-action.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}
denote the subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
consisting
of elements of absolute norm
1
.
Note that the
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-invariants of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)
is canonically identified
with
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}
, so that
the short exact sequence of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-modules

1\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\mapsto x^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)\longrightarrow 1

induces a coboundary map

\delta_{\chi}\colon\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}/(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}).

Identifying
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})
with
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
via
the Kummer isomorphism, we view this as a map into
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.

Lemma A.3
.

We have
\{\pm 1\}\cap\im(\delta_{\chi})=N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.
In particular, a fundamental unit of
F
has norm
-1
if and only if
-1\in\im(\delta_{\chi})
.

Proof.

Apply
Lemma

6.7
with
M=\mathcal{O}^{\times}
and
\Gamma=G_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
and note that the coboundary map

\delta\colon\{\pm 1\}=\mathbb{Z}^{\times}/\mathbb{Z}^{\times 2}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})

induced by the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\mapsto x^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}^{\times}\longrightarrow 1

is just the restriction of the Kummer isomorphism to
\{\pm 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.
∎

By
Lemma

A.3
, understanding if (
A.1
) has a solution is
equivalent to understanding when
-1\in\im(\delta_{\chi})
. We will use Selmer
groups to first understand the corresponding ‘everywhere local’ question.
Specifically, for a nonarchimedean place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}

denote the units in the ring of integers of
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
and
\chi_{v}
the
restriction of
\chi
to
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{v}}
. Then, similarly to the construction of

\delta_{\chi}
above, for each such place
v
that is not
split in
F/\mathbb{Q}
we get maps

\delta_{\chi_{v}}\colon\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{v},N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}}=1}/(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{v},N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}}=1})^{2}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}),

where we slightly abuse notation by also denoting by
v
the unique
place of
F
above
v
.
For a non-archimedean place
v
that is split in
F/\mathbb{Q}
, the character
\chi_{v}
is trivial, and we
take
\delta_{\chi_{v}}
to be the usual Kummer map

\mathbb{Z}_{v}^{\times}/(\mathbb{Z}_{v}^{\times})^{2}\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})
.
Finally, the unique Archimedean place
v
is split in
F
by assumption, and

\delta_{\chi_{v}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\times}/(\mathbb{R}^{\times})^{2}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mu_{2})
is an
isomorphism.

Definition A.4
.

We define the Selmer group
\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
to be the group

\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=\{x\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\mid\textup{res}_{v}(x)\in\im(\delta_{\chi_{v}})~~\textup{for all places }v\}.

The Selmer group defined above may be explicitly described as follows.

Lemma A.5
.

The Kummer map
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})
realises

\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}~\textup{sq.-free }:~~x\mid\Delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}~~\textup{and}~~(x,d)_{p}=1~~\textup{for all primes }p\mid\Delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\},

where
\Delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
is the discriminant of
F/\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

At the Archimedean place
v
the condition in the definition of
\Sel^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})

is empty, so from now on let
v
be a non-Archimedean place of
\mathbb{Q}
.

An easy calculation verifies that when we identify
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})

with
\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times 2}
, for
v
non-split the map
\delta_{\chi_{v}}

is given as follows:
for
x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{v},N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}}=1}
, by Hilbert’s theorem 90 we can find

y\in F_{v}^{\times}
such that
x=y\sigma(y)^{-1}
. Then
\delta_{\chi_{v}}(x)=N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}}(y)
.

Now, since for every
y\in F_{v}^{\times}
we have
y\sigma(y)^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}}^{\times}
,
we find that
\im(\delta_{\chi})=N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{v}}(F_{v}^{\times})
. Note that for
v

inert in
F/\mathbb{Q}
this image consists precisely of the elements of
\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}
of even
valuation, and the same is true for split places. To conclude, we note that

x\in\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}
is a norm from
F_{v}^{\times}
if and only if the Hilbert
symbol
(x,d)_{v}
is equal to
1
.
∎

Note that
\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
is (visibly) a finite group,
and
\delta_{\chi}
gives an injection

\delta_{\chi}\colon\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}/(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\hookrightarrow\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q}).

As in the proof of
Lemma

A.5
, this map is given explicitly by
taking
x\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}
, chosing
y\in F^{\times}
such that

y\sigma(y)^{-1}=x
, and mapping
x
to the class of

N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(y)\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.

Remark A.6
.

For
-1
to be in the image of
\delta_{\chi}
, a necessary condition is that

-1\in\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
which, from the description given
in
Lemma

A.5
, is equivalent to
d\in\mathcal{F}
. Thus the necessary
local conditions for (
A.1
) to be soluble may be viewed as the
condition that
-1\in\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
.

Note that
-1\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}
maps to
-d\in\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})

since
\sqrt{d}
satisfies
\sqrt{d}\cdot\sigma(\sqrt{d})^{-1}=-1
, and

N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})=-d
. In particular, every element of

\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})/\left\langle\delta_{\chi}(-1)\right\rangle
is
uniquely represented by a positive square-free integer, so that we obtain a
natural injection

(A.7)

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1}\big/\mskip-3.0mu\big(\{\pm 1\}\cdot(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F,N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\big)\hookrightarrow\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q}),

where
\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
denotes the subgroup of
\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})

consisting of positive elements of
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 2}
.
For
d\in\mathcal{F}
, when we identify the quotient of
\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})

by
\langle\delta_{\chi}(-1)\rangle
with
\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
, the element

-1\in\Sel_{2}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})\subset\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/(\mathbb{Q}^{\times})^{2}
is identified with the
class of
d\in\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
. To summarise, we have now proven
the following, which roughly corresponds to the combination of

[
39
, Lemma 2.1 (ii) and Proposition 2.2]
.

Proposition A.8
.

Let
d\geq 2
be a square-free integer, and let
\chi
be the quadratic
character associated to the extension
F_{d}=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})/\mathbb{Q}
. Then in order
for the negative Pell equation
Equation

A.1
to be soluble, we must
have
d\in\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
where

\displaystyle\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=

\displaystyle\{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}~\textup{sq. free }:~~x\mid\Delta_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}~~\textup{and}~~(x,d)_{p}=1~~\textup{for all primes }p\mid\Delta_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}\}.

Moreover, this happens if and only if
d\in\mathcal{F}
. In this case, a necessary
and sufficient condition for (
A.1
) to be soluble is that the
unique nontrivial element of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1}\big/\mskip-3.0mu\big(\{\pm 1\}\cdot(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\big)
maps to
d
under (
A.7
).

Remark A.9
.

An immediate consequence of
Proposition

A.8
is that the negative
Pell equation is soluble for all
d\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=1,
since then there is a unique non-trivial
element of
\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
and there is therefore no option
but for
d
and the image of the generator of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1}\big/\mskip-3.0mu\big(\{\pm 1\}\cdot(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\big)
to coincide.

To pass from
Proposition

A.8
to Stevenhagen’s conjecture, one
hypothesises that, in absence of additional information, each non-trivial element of

\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
has equal right to be the image
\eta
of the
generator of
\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1}\big/\mskip-3.0mu\big(\{\pm 1\}\cdot(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{F_{d},N_{F_{d}/\mathbb{Q}}=1})^{2}\big)
. In particular, if
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=r
, then the ‘probability’ that
d
and

\eta
coincide is
1/(2^{r}-1)
. To make this precise, for
r\geq 1
write

\textup{Pr}(r)=\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}~~:d<X\textup{ and }\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})=r\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}~~:d<X\}}.

Then one conjectures that this limit exists for each
r
, and that

(A.10)

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}~~:~~d<X\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d<X\}}=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\textup{Pr}(r)}{2^{r}-1}.

In particular, if one assumes that
\eta
“looks equidistributed”
among the non-trivial elements of

\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
, then understanding the proportion of
d\in\mathcal{F}
such that

Equation

A.1
is soluble comes down to determining the distribution of

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Sel_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\chi)/\mathbb{Q})
as
\chi
varies over the
quadratic characters associated with
d\in\mathcal{F}
.

The aforementioned work of Fouvry–Klüners proves

\textup{Pr}(r)=\alpha\prod_{j=1}^{r-1}(2^{j}-1)^{-1}
, where

\alpha=1-c_{\textup{Pell}}
(combine
[
8
, Corollary 2]
with op.
cit. Proposition 2), as had already been conjectured and supported by
substantial partial results by Gerth at the time Stevenhagen made his conjecture.
Therefore
Equation

A.10
predicts that

\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}~~:~~d<X\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d<X\}}=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(2^{j}-1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{stevenhagen1993}{}{}, Prop. 2.8]}}}}{{=}}1-\alpha=c_{\textup{Pell}},

which is Stevenhagen’s conjecture.
Note that by
Remark

A.9
the work of Fouvry–Klüners gives the unconditional lower bound

\liminf_{X\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}_{\textup{Pell}}~~:~~d<X\}}{\#\{d\in\mathcal{F}~~:~~d<X\}}\geq\textup{Pr}(1)=1-c_{\textup{Pell}}

mentioned earlier.
